Do not swap it for `steer()` or `followUp()`, which run `_throwIfExtensionCommand` unconditionally, nor for string mangling of the user's text. The same binds any path that puts a note back into pi's queue after taking it out, and any path that prompts queued content through `prompt()`, whose `expandPromptTemplates` defaults to true where `sendUserMessage`'s defaults to false. The team runner sends every flush of queued content through one wrapper that passes false. A note is the one queued string with no `[Message from X]:` prefix to make a leading slash impossible. Sanitization follows the trust level rather than the source: `stripBidiControls` stays because a human renders the note, `stripControlChars` is gone because it flattened the newlines the note box now lets the user type, and the 500 character cap is gone because truncating changes what the user asked for. No cap replaced it at any layer, and that is deliberate: `sanitizeCancelNote` strips and trims only (`tools/shell-cancel-registry.ts`), the note textarea carries no `maxlength` (`ToolCancelControl.vue`), and the router forwards `msg.note` unchanged (`chat-handlers.ts`, the `cancelToolCall` handler). The note is one human-authored message delivered to the agent as user turn content, so a cap can only drop the end of a reason the user meant the agent to read. Exactly one user-visible echo per context, `userMessage` for the panel, `subagentSteered` for a subagent, `teamAgentUserMessage` for a team agent. Zero echoes makes the model act on an instruction the user cannot see in the transcript; two double-render it. The team seam deliberately bypasses the `MessageBus`. A note sent through the bus is dropped with no signal to the sender once the run's `unsubscribeBus()` has fired, and again when the sender token equals the agent's own name, so the sink returns false instead and the caller logs it. The panel's echo is emitted when pi accepts the note, not when the delivery is called and not when pi delivers it, which is the last point still synchronous with the click and is where the subagent and team contexts echo too. A path that takes an accepted note back out of pi's queue owes the user a correction, which is what `withdrawQueue`, shared by `stopForBudget` and `beginAbort`, emits through `dropEchoedNotes` when it drops the queue. That correction covers every echoed note not yet delivered, not only the ones found in pi's queue, because a re-steer holds notes outside pi's queue while it puts them back.

  **A queued chip batch never displaces a panel note, and the note goes first.** `resteerQueuedInputs` re-steers the whole batch on every queued message, and the `clearQueue()` each pass (`resteerOnce`) needs drops every pending steer, the note included. So it puts back the accepted notes pi still held (`heldNotes`, pi's undelivered steers matched one for one against `injectedNotes`, so a batch whose text equals a note stays a batch), then the batch, each awaited so pi's queue holds exactly that order, and re-queues follow-ups with the images they were queued with (`queuedPromptEntries.requeue`, since pi's `clearQueue()` returns text only and its agent queues are private). The notes go back through `sendUserMessage(..., { expandPromptTemplates: false })`, and the follow-ups through `prompt()` with the same options plus a `preflightResult` that keeps a follow-up a stop withdrew while the input handlers ran out of the stopped run and out of a newer live run (`takeBackQueued`), and refuses the run it would open. A note accepted while a batch is already queued triggers the same re-steer. Both reach the model in the same request, the note right after the cancelled result and the batch after the note, so one reply answers both (see the next paragraph). A note is listed in `injectedNotes` before it is handed to pi, so its delivery can never be read as the batch however soon pi delivers it.

  **Every pending steer reaches the model at the next boundary.** Every pi `SettingsManager` Damocles creates goes through `withSettingsPolicy` (`pi-session/settings-policy.ts`), which pins its `getSteeringMode()` to `all`: the folder's, which every panel session, replacement and reload builds on, the in-memory one each nested session gets (subagents, team agents, `/btw`), and the package manager's, so the rule has no exception. pi reads the mode only through that method, when `createAgentSession` builds the agent and when `AgentSession.reload()` re-syncs it after re-reading the settings files, so the pin outlives every reload. pi's default, `one-at-a-time`, hands the model one steer per boundary: a note and a batch pending together were answered in two model calls, the first reply covering the note alone. The mode is not carried by a setting. `applyOverrides` is dropped by `settingsManager.reload()`, which pi runs in `AgentSession.reload()` and in every `resourceLoader.reload()`. A value in the Damocles agent `settings.json` loses to a trusted project's `.pi/settings.json`, because pi merges project over global. pi exports no `SettingsStorage` implementation to wrap, so a storage layer would mean reimplementing pi's locked file storage. A project may not override the policy: only Damocles puts steers into its sessions' queues, the note-then-batch order and the single reply depend on it, and a repository file that changed it would split them with nothing telling the user. `AgentSession.setSteeringMode` bypasses the pin and writes the global file, and nothing in Damocles calls it. `getFollowUpMode()` is pinned to `all` the same way, which pi reads at the same two points (`sdk.js:283-284`, `syncQueueModesFromSettings` at `agent-session.js:2182-2185`, run by `reload()` at `:3008`): a prompt pi queues as a follow-up carries its UserPromptSubmit context as a second follow-up right behind it ("A UserPromptSubmit hook runs before pi"), and pi's default drains one follow-up per stop (`PendingMessageQueue.drain`, `agent.js:72-81` in pi-agent-core 1.4.0), so the model would answer the prompt without its context and a second billed request would deliver it. Every follow-up pending when a run would stop now reaches the model in one request, another extension's included. Several user messages can now arrive at one boundary, so the adapter finds each delivered batch's and note's entry by message identity for its mid-stream marker (`resolveMidStreamMarkers`), because the branch's last user entry names only the last of them. The checkpoint engine still mints one turn per boundary, keyed on the last user entry: no tool runs between messages delivered together, so a second turn would snapshot the same tree, and a mid-stream row has no rewind control. A subagent or team agent gets every steer pending at a boundary together too, each still its own user message, so no steer shares a message with peer text. `settings-policy.test.ts` pins both modes of the folder session over a project and an agent file that both ask for `one-at-a-time`, across `session.reload()`, a nested session, and that every `SettingsManager` factory call under `src/core` and `src/vscode` is wrapped.

  **A note pi accepts with no run in progress starts one, and that run belongs to the prompt the note annotates.** pi reports acceptance through `preflightResult`, after queueing (`queued`) or immediately before starting that run (`started`), so the echo still waits only for acceptance, and `noteDeliveryForMain` opens a turn for such a run because no `sendMessage` did. `handled` means an input handler consumed the note, so pi neither queued nor ran it: the note leaves `injectedNotes` with no echo, and a later stop has no discarded note to report. The note's committed entry gets its mid-stream marker from the run's opening user message, which pi emits like any other delivery. The cancelled call's `details` carry the note's text under `CANCEL_NOTE_DETAIL_KEY` (pi never sends `details` to the model, so this is not the note in a tool result), and `isCancelNoteDispatch` (`session-store/prompt-index.ts`) recognises a dispatch whose prompt is the note of a cancelled call no user message has followed yet.

  **The UserPromptSubmit hook and pi's input handlers judge each queued message once, as it is queued, and the batch carries only the messages they let through.** `queueInput` holds a message in `screeningInputs` while `screenQueuedInputs` runs the UserPromptSubmit hook on it (`dispatchInput`, as a Damocles step), then `extensionRunner.emitInput` as `interactive` input with `streamingBehavior: 'steer'`, which is how every loaded extension's `input` handler sees a typed prompt, one message at a time and oldest first, so the batch keeps the order the user typed in. A message the hook blocks, or a handler answers `handled`, loses its chip through `queueCancelled` without `returnToInput`; a block's reason reaches the user as a `notification` (`postUserPromptVerdict`), and a `transform` rewrites the message before it joins `queuedInputs`. The combined re-steer is then sent as `source: 'extension'`, as a cancel note is. No hook runs inside pi's input handlers (the configured hooks register no `input` handler), and the re-steer runs no Damocles screening, so a hook never judges the combined batch: it would run once per re-steer on text it already judged, owe its `additionalContext` once per pass, and block or allow a message by the messages beside it, and a block there names no chip, so every chip would stay pending. With no hook configured and no input handler loaded, a message is admitted synchronously, so the first re-steer still starts before `queueInput` returns. Every screening hook runs under `PiSession.screening`, one `AbortController` per panel: each path that takes the queue back (`returnQueue`: ESC, the budget stop, a new chat, a resume switch, disposal) aborts it, which kills each hook still judging a message, with its process tree, and spawns no later hook for that message (`dispatchInput`, `runHook`), then replaces it, so a message queued afterwards is screened once as usual under a live signal. A late result is fenced by identity, not by a flag: `returnQueue` empties `screeningInputs` in the same step, so the loop no longer finds its input at the head and drops its verdict, its context and its title.
- **A UserPromptSubmit hook's `prompt` is the text the user typed.** Claude Code documents `prompt` as the submitted text, but Damocles may hand pi other text for the same typed text: blocks leading it, a template expanded because blocks lead it, or a skill or `/init` rewrite. PiSession judges the typed text itself: `UserBroadcast.content` for a sent message and `QueuedInput.typed` for a queued one, else the text it sends. Other extensions' `input` handlers still see the text pi was given.
- **A UserPromptSubmit hook runs before pi, as the last pre-dispatch step of a send** (`PiSession.promptPi`, after `beginTurn` and right before `prompt()`). It is a pre-submit gate that needs nothing from pi's run, so it sits before the one commit point: a Stop, a new chat, a resume switch or the session's disposal while it runs aborts the send's signal, which kills the hook's process tree (`runHook`, the launcher's `kill({ tree: true })`) and returns the message whole ("A send holds the session from its first step"). It runs after the composer's resource reads and the Always-loaded MCP wait, which share that signal: the echo is on screen while the hook runs, so a Stop visibly takes it back, and `beginTurn` makes the chat busy, so no finish action (D33) can start between the hook and `prompt()`. The cost is that a hook that blocks the first prompt of a session can wait out the MCP wait first. The thinking level, a session entry, is set after the hook, so a send stopped during it writes nothing; the hidden working-folder note and undelivered background results, when owed, are appended before it, since they precede `beginTurn`, and stay owed to the conversation either way. A sent extension command (`isExtensionCommand`, the same check as the MCP wait's) runs no hook, as pi runs `_tryExecuteExtensionCommand` before its input handlers (`agent-session.js:1640-1646`). A queued message is always judged, a leading `/name` included: only its place in the batch decides whether pi runs it as a command or the model reads it as text. A block shows its reason, keeps the echo, opens no run, returns the attachments with the `unsent` outcome, and lets the messages held meanwhile go to screening, as pi's `handled` verdict does. Added context belongs to the message that produced it and reaches the model only in the request pi delivers that message in, as one hidden `damocles-hook-context` custom message (`display: false`; neither the adapter nor the history loader renders a custom message) that `PiSession.queueHookContext` queues right behind the message from that message's own `preflightResult`. pi persists it as a custom message entry when it delivers it (`agent-session.js:760`), like any message of the run, so no past entry is rewritten. It cannot ride on `before_agent_start`: pi emits that event in `_prepareRun` (`agent-session.js:1428`, `:1488`) before it reports `started` (`onStarted`, `:1441`), so no handler can tell which `prompt()` opened the run (a note, a re-steer batch, a re-queued follow-up or another extension's `sendUserMessage` can win the race), and a steer or follow-up delivered mid-run fires no `before_agent_start` at all (pi-agent-core drains them at a boundary, `agent-loop.js:90`, `:116`, `:191`, `:197`, or in `agent.continue()`, `agent.js:263-272`). The sent prompt keeps its context in `promptPi`'s own closure. On `started` it is steered into the run the prompt opens, whose first drain takes it before the first request (`agent-loop.js:90`). pi awaits every `agent_start`, `turn_start` and opening-message listener before that drain (`agent-loop.js:55-60`), so a re-steer pass or `takeBackQueued` can clear pi's queue first; `PiSession.runStartContexts` keeps the message object of each such context while pi still holds it (`agent.peekQueuedMessages()`, `agent.js:212-215`), and `clearQueueForPutBack` steers it back right after the `clearQueue()`, ahead of everything the pass puts back. A batch or re-queued follow-up that opens a run gets the same treatment. On `queued` it is queued as a follow-up behind the prompt, and the prompt's `QueuedPromptEntries` record carries it, so `requeueFollowUp` puts it back behind the prompt whenever a re-steer clears pi's queue. A queued message carries its context on its `QueuedInput` from screening, and every re-steer pass steers the contexts of the inputs in its batch right behind the batch on `queued` or `started` (`admitSteer`); a delivered input has left `queuedInputs`, so it is never owed again. pi drains every pending steer and every pending follow-up together (`withSettingsPolicy`, "Every pending steer reaches the model at the next boundary"), so a message and its context share one request. pi's `clearQueue()` drops custom messages too (`agent.clearAllQueues()`, `agent-session.js:1949`), so every path that drops a message from pi's queue drops its context with it: `dropQueue` (ESC, the budget stop, a new chat, a resume switch, disposal, a stopped run) and `takeBackQueued` (a withdrawn follow-up or batch). A pass that clears pi's queue to put it back (`resteerOnce`, `takeBackQueued`) puts back exactly the contexts of what it puts back: the run-start context pi has not drained yet, each re-queued follow-up's, and the batch's. Nothing is queued for a message pi refuses (a throw, no disposition), answers `handled`, or opens a run with after a Stop or a replacement overtook it (`stopRunAtStart`), since that run still commits whatever its first drain finds. A prompt pi defers while `agent_settled` handlers run (`agent-session.js:1632-1635`) reports its disposition later, and its closure still holds the context then. The extension holds none of it: the configured hooks' `before_agent_start` handler injects only the output of `before_agent_start` hooks. `sessionTitle` renames the session when the verdict is admitted.

  **Re-steers run one pass at a time, so pi never holds two batches.** A pass awaits pi's input handlers inside `prompt()`, which another loaded extension can hold for as long as it likes. Two passes in flight at once each clear pi's queue before the other has queued anything, then both queue, and the model receives the first batch and then the combined one. A queued message that arrives while a pass runs sets `resteerRequested`, and the running loop makes one more pass that reads the buffer as it is then; the first pass starts synchronously, so the common case still steers before `queueInput` returns. `steeredCount` records how many leading inputs the batch pi holds carries, set from `preflightResult`, which pi calls synchronously right after queueing and so before any boundary can deliver it. A delivery consumes exactly those inputs, so one queued after that batch was steered keeps its chip and goes out with the next pass rather than being reported delivered. `admitSteer` acts on pi's disposition: on `started` the batch opens a run of its own, which it opens as a turn because no `sendMessage` did; on `handled` an extension command or input handler consumed the batch, which pi neither queued nor ran, so its inputs leave the buffer and their chips are cancelled.

  **A send holds the session from its first step until pi has it, and a Stop before then returns it whole.** `PiSession.claimSend` (from `sendMessage`, or from `beginSend` for the composer's send handler, which still has to read its attached MCP resources) sets `processingFlag` and records the send as an `OutgoingPrompt` with its own abort signal, so a second `sendMessage`, `beginSend` or `compact` is refused until it ends, and two sends never race to pi. Before `prompt()` (pre-dispatch: the handler's resource read, `ensureStarted`, the waits for a replacement, a model switch, an abort or pi settling a run, the Always-loaded MCP wait, the working-folder note and the undelivered results, the UserPromptSubmit hook, whose process the abort kills, and last a second wait for pi settling a run, since a run that streamed at the first one can reach its `agent_settled` handlers while those steps await and pi would defer the prompt), a Stop, a new chat, a resume switch or the session's disposal (`stopSends`) aborts the signal, returns the message at once (`interruptRecovery` with its text and images, then `onWithdrawn` with its chips) and frees the session, so the next send goes ahead at once; the stopped send's next check (`sendOvertaken`) ends it as `withdrawn` without a word, and neither pi nor the session file ever sees the prompt. The UserPromptSubmit hook's context stays in the send until pi reports `started` or `queued` for it, so a send stopped before `prompt()`, or in the gap before pi opens its run, leaves that context to no run ("A UserPromptSubmit hook runs before pi"). The handler passes the same signal to `readResource`, and races the read against it because a server still connecting cannot see it. A message queued while a send is on its way and pi is not streaming yet (`heldInputs`) steers into the run once pi gives its verdict on the send, released a microtask after `preflightResult` because a re-steer clears pi's queue, and goes back to the composer if the send ends without reaching pi. Once `prompt()` holds the message (other extensions' input handlers, the auth check, `before_agent_start` with memory and Compass injection), a Stop no longer returns it: pi's `abort()` marks only a run that exists (`agent-session.js:1972-1974`) and `_startRun` clears the mark (`:1425`), so the `started` observer aborts the run itself (`stopRunAtStart`), and pi stops it at `agent_start` before any request (`:712-713`) while still committing the prompt (`agent-loop.js:48-63` in pi-agent-core 1.4.0): the echo stays as a sent, stopped turn. `stopRunAtStart` also aborts the Agent from a one-shot `agent_start` listener of its own, because pi's `dispose()` disconnects the session from its Agent (`:1023`, `:1001-1006`) and with it the mark-to-abort step, and a replacement or disposal can dispose the session before pi's input handlers release the prompt; without it that run would send a billed request nobody sees. pi queuing it takes it back as below; pi rejecting it returns it. A send after that Stop on the same session waits for the held prompt to settle (`promptInPi`) unless another run streams, because two prompts racing to open a run on an idle session get the second refused as already processing. A new chat, a resume switch and a disposal return a held prompt pi has not accepted at once instead, since their conversation leaves the screen, and abort the run pi may still open with it, which the old file keeps as a stopped turn only if pi had not disposed that session yet. An extension command (`/todos`) opens no run, so it neither waits for MCP nor uses the first prompt's wait, and, sent, runs no UserPromptSubmit hook. `preflightResult` only observes: Damocles never throws from it, because pi treats it as internal (#11044).

  **A stopped run delivers nothing more from pi's queue, and nothing it held reaches the next run.** pi keeps both queues across `abort()`, and the next run polls the steering queue before its first request (`runLoop` in `@earendil-works/pi-agent-core`, `agent-loop.js`), so a leftover batch or note would reach the model at the start of the next prompt, unmatched and counted as a prompt. ESC (`beginAbort`) and the budget stop (`stopForBudget`) both go through `withdrawQueue`, which clears pi's queue, returns every chip to the composer, the ones still being screened included, whose hooks it kills (`queueCancelled` with `returnToInput`, which the webview appends after whatever the box holds, images included), returns every prompt pi queued into the run with its text, images and terminal attachments (`dropQueue`), and drops the notes pi held with their echoes corrected, since each annotates a command in the turn that was stopped. A queued message was never sent, so no stop may discard it. `beginAbort` clears the queue again once the abort settles, for a note a re-steer was putting back. A new chat (`reset`), a resume switch (`setResumeSession`) and the session's disposal (`runDispose`, which a folder switch and a cross-folder resume run before the panel moves) return the queue the same way (`returnQueue`) without the note warning, since the transcript the echoes stood in is replaced, and the first two forget `injectedNotes`. `runDispose` returns it before it sets `_disposed`, which silences `emit`, together with every `sendMessage` prompt pi has not accepted yet, the one pi's input handlers still hold included (`stopSends`): `interruptRecovery` carries its text and images back and `onWithdrawn` its chips. A send goes back at most once (`OutgoingPrompt.withdraw`). When the handlers release it, `preflightResult('started')` aborts the run pi still opens with it on the disposed session before any request (`stopRunAtStart`), which records nothing of it once pi has disconnected the session, and `preflightResult('queued')` takes it back out (`takeBackQueued`, or `dropQueue` once the runtime is gone), even while the runtime is still installed. A caller that posts `sessionCleared` or `conversationCleared` posts it after the reset, because the webview can put a message back only while it is still on screen (`approvePlan` with clear context posts `sessionCleared` after `clear()`); the old run streams until the replacement aborts it, so its queue is dropped at once and nothing queued in one conversation reaches the next. A withdrawn message pi queues only after the stop has wound down, into a newer live run, is taken back out by `takeBackQueued` from the `preflightResult('queued')` of the re-queued follow-up (`requeueFollowUp`), of the batch (`admitSteer`), or of a `sendMessage` prompt pi's input handlers held across the Stop (its signal aborted), whose own record is withdrawn first (`queuedPromptEntries.withdrawOne`), so a later prompt of the same text keeps its images and sidecars. pi has no single-message removal, so it clears the queue, drops the last copy of a withdrawn follow-up's text, puts the follow-ups back at once with their images, and hands the steers to the next re-steer pass (`takenSteers`), which puts back only the notes, ahead of the batch; a run still stopped or a session already replaced has its whole queue dropped instead (`dropQueue`). A note for a stopped run (`runStopped`: the budget stop pending, or an abort not yet settled) is refused at the click and again at acceptance, when a stop landed while pi ran its input handlers, and the user is told it was not sent instead of shown an echo; queueing it would bill a continuation past the limit, because `_runBeforeSettleBoundary` continues on a non-empty queue whatever the decider answers. A re-steer the stop withdrew (`batchWithdrawn`: its inputs are no longer the head of the buffer, or the session was replaced) is judged in `admitSteer`, from `preflightResult`, which is the one point every steer passes after all of pi's input and `before_agent_start` handlers, whichever extension registered them and in whatever order they run. pi calls it synchronously right after queueing the batch into the running run, where a withdrawn batch is taken back out, or after the `before_agent_start` handlers of a run it opens with it, before that run's first request, because the run ended while those handlers ran. A run cannot be refused there: Damocles never throws from `preflightResult`, which pi treats as internal (#11044). An ESC or a budget stop that lands while pi holds a re-steer (`batchInPi`) leaves that batch to pi's verdict (`stoppedBatch`) instead of returning its chips: opened as a run, it is sent and stopped (`stopRunAtStart` aborts the run, which pi stops at `agent_start` before any request, `agent-session.js:712-713`, the batch stays in the transcript and `queueBatchProcessed` collapses its chips into it); queued, it is taken back and its chips return; rejected, they return; handled, they are cancelled. A follow-up whose re-queue pi holds is left to pi's verdict the same way (`queuedPromptEntries.withdraw` with `keepRequeuing`): a run it opens is stopped with its entry's sidecars recorded, a queue takes it back. A new chat, a resume switch and a disposal return both at once instead, since their conversation leaves the screen, and a run pi later opens with one is aborted the same way. Never move the check into an `input` handler: pi runs handlers in extension order, so a handler registered after the check could still hold the batch, and every await after the handlers (the auth check, a pre-prompt compaction, `before_agent_start`, image resizing) is a window an ESC can land in. A pass never calls `prompt()` while pi runs the `agent_settled` handlers of an ended run: it waits for pi to settle that run (`PiSession.piSettling`, the send's wait) before it reads the buffer, and again before the batch's `prompt()` when a run ended while the notes went back. pi defers a prompt made meanwhile into that settle and returns from `prompt()` at once (`agent-session.js:1632-1634`), so the pass would no longer know whether pi holds the batch: a Stop would return chips pi still sends, and the next pass would defer an overlapping batch that pi delivers again after the first. `waitForIdle()` cannot serve, since pi is idle as those handlers start (`:685`, `:1983-1985`). Messages queued during the settle go out in that pass, as one batch. `queueInput` accepts a message while pi runs those handlers (`RunLifecycle.mayQueue`), since the webview still shows the turn running until `agent_settled`. With nothing queued before it, the run it was typed in is over, so it is a send of its own (`afterSettleInputs`, `sendAfterSettle`): once pi settled that run and the send holding the session ended, the messages queued meanwhile go out together through `sendMessage`, with the UserPromptSubmit hook, prompt index, sidecars and memory candidate of a typed send, as a follow-up when pi has a run open; their chips give way to the send's echo, and a Stop before then returns them. Behind messages already queued for that run it joins their batch.

- **The shell process-lifetime path has no timer, interval or process-table read on any platform, and neither arm carries shell-specific knowledge** (`tools/process-tree.ts`, run by the process launcher's worker). Windows puts each spawned shell in its own job object, nested in the panel's session job, which carries `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE`, and terminates the call job explicitly on abort; POSIX signals the process group, which works because both shell tools start their shell on the launcher with `detached: process.platform !== 'win32'` (`tools/bash-tool.ts`, `tools/powershell-tool.ts`, carried over from pi's own bash tool at `packages/coding-agent/src/core/tools/bash.ts` in pi 1.4.0) and a non-interactive bash leaves its background jobs in that group. **Two things are carried over from that tool, not invented here, and both have to move when it moves: the `detached` flag, and the exit code a signal-killed shell reports.** Damocles reimplements the operations so it can start the shell on the launcher in the panel's session job, so it owns the second one too: a null exit code becomes `128 + <signal number>` when a signal named the termination and `1` when none is named, applied where the tracked bash operations return, after the abort and timeout throws, and where the PowerShell operations return, after theirs. The parity is behavioural rather than textual, and describing it as "the same code" is how the next editor gets it wrong. pi reads `child.signalCode` after its wait resolves; Damocles reads it from the launcher's `exited` (the worker's `exit` event) and carries it out of `waitForShellExit`, which resolves `{ code, signal }` for that reason. Only an external kill reaches the mapping in the bash tool, because the abort and timeout throws still precede it, so `aborted` and `timeout:<seconds>` are unchanged and a cancelled or timed-out command still reports no exit code. The PowerShell operations throw `aborted` and `timeout:<seconds>` before their exit-code line too; without that ordering a timed-out command would gain a trailing `[exit code 137]` and the timeout text would change. A null exit code no longer passes as success in either direction: pi's tool body throws `Command terminated without an exit code` on one, and neither Damocles spawn site can report a terminated shell as a null exit code. `bash-operations.test.ts` > "an externally killed shell" pins the two implementations to the same answer rather than to a fixed number, because win32 reports a plain exit code where POSIX reports a signal. Job membership is inherited and cannot be escaped, `BREAKAWAY_OK` deliberately unset, so a severed parent link stops mattering and the kernel walks nothing. Two earlier designs were tried and deleted, a process-table poller and an MSYS `ps -W` group lookup, and both were compensating for not having that one property; the measurement that killed the poller is that the fork helper lives about ten milliseconds while the cheapest query reporting parent links costs 384ms, so no polling rate can observe the link. `createShellJob(pid, shellJob)` must stay the first statement after `spawn` in the process launcher's worker (`core/process-launcher/worker.ts`), where spawn and assignment happen together for every process it starts, the shell tools', hooks' and git's included: the two assignments cost 0.13ms and Git Bash forks its background jobs 10 to 20ms in, so the margin is about a hundredfold, but a child created before assignment is outside the job permanently and unkillable. A throw from it (the POSIX sentinel failing to start) is logged in its own `try`, and the process stays tracked and killable through its pid or group, without its job. The no-running-cost rule is asserted, not argued, by `bash-operations.test.ts` > "no running cost in the process-lifetime path", which drives a full call including the abort and pins the global `setInterval`, `node:timers` and `node:timers/promises` all uncalled plus exactly one spawn; a poller reaching for any of the three goes red, checked by reintroducing one.
- **The Windows jobs are nested, and only the outer one kills on close.** A call job holds one shell call; a session job holds the panel and carries `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE`. The two ways a call job ends are not symmetric and must never be described together. Stop terminates it, which kills every member, the background jobs the command left included, and reaches nothing outside that call; that is the defect this exists to fix. The end of a normal call merely releases the handle, which kills nothing. The whole difference is one `TerminateJobObject`, and grouping the two is how a reader concludes that Stop leaks. **The rule that keeps this correct is that the call job never carries kill-on-close**: releasing its handle at the end of a call has to leave a deliberately backgrounded process running, which is what makes Windows match the POSIX process group, and adding that one flag is the silent undo (it reads as harmless and turns the "outlives its call" case red, which is how it is pinned). The assignment order, session job then call job, is convention and not the thing keeping this correct: reversing it was mutation-tested and every case still passed, because the shell ends up a member of both jobs either way. Do not write, or rely on, "the order forms the nesting". `BREAKAWAY_OK` stays unset, since a child that could leave defeats the design.
- **The session job is per panel, held on `PiSession` and created through the process launcher (`Launcher.createSessionJob`), and there is deliberately no module-level instance.** The one other owner is `ScmService`, which holds one for source control's git reads (see "Source control"). One panel is one `PiSession` and a user can have several open, so a host-wide job would either kill another panel's commands when one panel is disposed, or, if never closed, degrade session cleanup into host-exit cleanup. Neither failure shows up in a single-panel test. Its lifetime is the panel and not the conversation: `PiSession` survives `reset()` and `clear()`, so tying the handle to the pi `AgentSession` would make `/clear` kill a process the user backgrounded earlier, which is the exact behaviour the nesting exists to preserve.
- **State the parity, then name the mechanism, because they are not the same thing.** Stop, background survival, cleanup at session dispose and cleanup after a host crash now behave the same on Windows, Linux and macOS. The mechanisms differ and must never be collapsed into one sentence. Windows nests job objects and gets the dispose and crash cases from `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` on the outer one (`tools/process-tree.ts`). POSIX has no job object, so `createShellSessionJob()` returns a sentinel-backed handle instead (`tools/shell-sentinel-client.ts`, driving the bundle built from `tools/shell-sentinel.ts` to `dist/sentinel.js`): one helper process per panel, started by the launcher worker that owns the panel's session job, `detached` with `stdio: ['pipe', 'ignore', 'pipe']` (stderr carries the sentinel's own failure reports to the log), so that worker keeps the write end of the helper's stdin and writes each shell's process group id down that pipe right after it starts the shell. The host thread never holds the pipe. Both platforms take the crash guarantee from the same property, the kernel acting at process teardown with no cooperation from the dying process: Windows releases the last job handle, POSIX closes a dead process's file descriptors, so a pipe whose only writer has died gives its reader EOF. **One pipe is both the registration channel and the liveness channel, and that is the design rather than an economy.** A heartbeat, a goodbye message or any other separate liveness signal would need code running in the dying host, which is precisely what SIGKILL and an OOM kill deny. Panel close and host death are therefore one code path: `SessionJob.dispose()` has the owning worker call the sentinel handle's `dispose()`, which calls `child.stdin.end()`, and calling `end()` rather than dropping the reference is what actually produces the EOF. Do not add a second killer inside `dispose()` on the grounds that it is synchronous and can report failure; it would need its own copy of the identity check below, and that check is the one part of this feature that can kill a stranger's process if it is wrong. Never write that the two platforms reach this the same way.
- **The write end must reach the sentinel and nothing else, or the EOF never arrives.** A duplicate of that fd in any longer-lived process holds the pipe open, and the guarantee degrades silently to "the sentinel acts when the last copy closes", which for a long-running server is never. It holds today because libuv creates the parent-side stdio ends close-on-exec, and only the fds named in a child's `stdio` array are `dup2`'d onto 0, 1 and 2, which clears close-on-exec on those copies alone. Launcher workers are threads of the host process and share its fd table, so the worker hands every start an explicit `stdio` list, `['pipe', 'pipe', 'pipe']` when the caller names none. Checked against a live `/proc` snapshot under WSL rather than assumed: with a detached shell and its background jobs running, the shell's fd table held exactly `0`, `1`, `2` and nothing else, while the host held the write end and the sentinel held its peer. Anything that later spawns with an explicit fd list, or gives a descendant of the host `stdio: 'inherit'`, has to be re-checked against that table, and the check is behavioural too: if `dispose()` on a live panel no longer kills the group, an inherited write end is the first place to look.
- **No sentinel on Windows, and the reason is not symmetry.** `createShellSessionJob()` dispatches on platform in its first statement and the win32 arm never reaches `shell-sentinel-client.ts`. A job object is kernel-owned and has no userspace component that can be killed, starved or crashed, so it is strictly better than a helper process; putting a sentinel beside it would add a weak point and buy nothing. This is asserted rather than argued, by `process-tree.test.ts` > "starts no sentinel on win32, where the job object already gives the guarantee", which pins zero `startShellSentinel` calls and zero spawns. The cost the POSIX arm pays for having no job object is real and worth knowing before anyone adds a second helper: one idle sentinel measured about 43 MB VmRSS and 7.1 MB private on Linux, per panel.
- **The sentinel re-identifies a group immediately before it signals it, and that check is the one part of this feature that can kill a stranger's process if it is wrong.** The sweep runs possibly hours after registration, and operating systems recycle pids, so `kill(-P)` on a bare remembered id is a silent kill of whatever holds P now. Two things stop that (`tools/shell-sentinel.ts`, `sweep`). First, an anchor: the sentinel reads the leader's start time at registration, `/proc/<pid>/stat` field 22 on Linux and `ps -o lstart=` on macOS, and refuses to signal when the live process holding P no longer matches it. The anchor is read by the sentinel and never by the launcher, which is what keeps `createShellJob` free of a process-table read right after `spawn`. Second, for the case where the leader is already gone, which is the headline case `sleep 600 &` produces within milliseconds, the member anchors taken at the `x` record do the identifying instead. Both rest on the same kernel property: **both kernels refuse to hand out a pid that is currently in use as a process group id**, so while any process still has `pgid == P` no new process can be given pid P, and one identified survivor therefore vouches for the whole group. Refusing to kill whenever the leader is gone would refuse exactly the case panel-close cleanup exists for, so that branch is required, not an optimisation; what it may never do is fire without a matching anchor. Do not replace the anchor with a plain "is pid P alive" check, and do not unregister a group when its call ends: a process backgrounded with `&` has to stay registered or panel close cannot reach it, which is the whole difference this feature exists to remove.
- **The pid-reservation claim is measured on Linux and read-only on macOS, and the difference matters to whoever edits `sweep` next.** On Linux it is `__change_pid()` in `kernel/pid.c`, which walks every pid type and returns without freeing the number if any task still holds it, `PIDTYPE_PGID` included. That was confirmed by running rather than by reading: with `/proc/sys/kernel/ns_last_pid` steering the allocator, a request for a pid in use as a non-empty group's id was refused and skipped, and the identical request succeeded the instant the group emptied. On macOS it is the "find an unused PID" loop in `forkproc()`, `bsd/kern/kern_fork.c`, whose condition includes `pghash_exists_locked(pid)`, so the allocator advances past a pid in use as a pgid. **That macOS reading has never been executed, because no Mac was reachable.** The two platforms are equal on this point, and the only difference left is that macOS anchors are one-second resolution against Linux's clock ticks. **There is deliberately no session-id filter on either platform, and reinstating one would be a regression rather than a hardening.** An earlier design tested `sid === pgid` before signalling a leaderless group, which was worth having only because that branch signalled without any anchor at all. Against member anchors it can only subtract: a backgrounded job that calls `setsid`, which is what every daemonizer does, has `sid !== pgid` while still being a process the sentinel anchored by pid and start time, so the filter would turn correct kills into misses for precisely the long-lived background processes panel-close cleanup exists to reach. The field was never viable on macOS in any case, which is how the question first arose: `ps`'s `sess` keyword reports a kernel pointer rather than an id (`adv_cmds`, `ps/keyword.c`, `KPTR`), and Darwin has no `sid` keyword at all.
- **The sweep never signals a group it has not positively identified, and the member anchor is what makes that possible when the leader is gone.** An earlier design signalled a leaderless group on the strength of its membership alone, which after a pid wrap could kill a stranger's processes; that hole is closed and must not be reopened. The launcher worker that owns the session job writes two records, `r<pgid>` right after it starts a shell (inside `createShellJob`) and `x<pgid>` at that shell's `exit` event, before a background job lets go of its output, and the `x` is the whole point: **at the instant the shell exits, every process still in the group is ours by construction**, so the sentinel snapshots each one with its own start time. At EOF a group is signalled only if the leader is alive and matches its anchor, or some member anchor is still alive and matches. Otherwise it is skipped. One matching anchor is sufficient because a pid cannot be recycled while a group still holds it, so a single identified survivor proves the whole pgid was never reissued. Do not reintroduce an unanchored branch, do not treat `x` as an unregistration (the group stays tracked, or a deliberately backgrounded process becomes unreachable at panel close), and do not replace the snapshot with a poll: it is one process-table read at one known event, inside the sentinel, never in the extension host and never on a schedule.
- **Known residual, and it fails the safe way. The general condition is "no anchored member is alive at sweep time", which is wider than the race that first suggested it.** Two shapes reach it, and the second is the one you will actually meet. First, a host killed between the shell exiting and the `x` record being written: that group has no member anchors at all. Second, and with no crash involved, a group whose anchored members have since exited leaving processes they forked after the snapshot. The snapshot is one read at one instant, so a double-forking wrapper, the shape `sh -c 'sleep 2; sleep 600 &' &` produces, anchors the wrapper and never sees the process it leaves behind. Measured under WSL rather than reasoned about: with a correct `r` and `x` pair, the late-forked process survived a `kill -9` of the host, `sleeps after: 1`. Note what does not reach it: `sleep 600 &` anchors the sleep itself, and `npm run dev &` anchors npm, which stays alive as the parent, so both clean up. Those processes leak. **That is a miss, not a stranger's death, and the difference is the entire reason the design changed.** A leaked process is the pre-2.23.0 behaviour for one group in a narrow window; a wrong kill is data loss with no warning and no log the user will ever read. Anyone tempted to widen the sweep to catch this case is trading the second failure for the first and must not. Two escapes were examined and rejected on evidence rather than taste: holding an fd on `/proc/<pid>` does not reserve a pid, which the kernel documents explicitly, and `pidfd_send_signal` returns ESRCH once the leader is dead, so it refuses precisely the case that needs covering.
- **The accepted residual is `kill -9` on the sentinel itself.** No userspace process can refuse SIGKILL, so killing the helper loses the cleanup for that panel, and there is no in-process defence against it. Linux could close this with a PID namespace or a systemd transient scope. macOS has neither, and no job objects and no cgroups, so closing it on Linux alone would create a three-way asymmetry, and the PID-namespace form would additionally change what every shell command sees of its own process tree. The user reviewed both options and chose to leave the gap. Do not reopen it, and do not substitute a mutual watchdog: two processes each restarting the other is a new failure mode of its own and still loses to a `kill -9` that names both.
- **koffi is required lazily, behind the win32 check, and its failure is reported rather than swallowed.** It is the project's only native dependency, so `createShellJob` returns on `process.platform !== 'win32'` before the loader is reached and no POSIX path ever requires it. The native binary lives in a per-platform `@koromix/koffi-<platform>-<arch>` optional dependency resolved at run time, which is why koffi is an esbuild external and reaches the VSIX through `scripts/extension-externals.mjs` and the generated `.vscodeignore` (run `npm run sync:vscodeignore`, never hand-edit it). A load failure logs the consequence in user terms and degrades to the bare `taskkill /T` sweep, which cannot reach a severed parent link, and each of the four kernel32 calls that can fail logs which call and which pid. There is no empty catch anywhere in the module: the degradation is announced at the point it is decided, so a user reporting surviving processes has a log line naming the cause. The process launcher's worker loads koffi too, from `app.asar.unpacked/dist`, so the desktop package unpacks `node_modules/koffi` whole beside its prebuilds.
- **Every process start Damocles owns goes through the process launcher** (`src/core/process-launcher/`), except the ones `docs/perf.md` "Starts outside the launcher" lists, each with its reason. Lint holds it: `child_process`, `node:child_process` and `cross-spawn` (a value import, a dynamic import or a `require`) are refused in `src/core`, `src/desktop/main` and `src/vscode` (and the rest of the trees their blocks cover) but in tests and the files `PROCESS_STARTERS_CORE` and `PROCESS_STARTERS_DESKTOP` in `eslint.config.mjs` list (the launcher worker and what only it loads, the sentinel, the Compass worker's git, the pty and formatter hosts), whose later blocks restate their tree's electron and vscode bans; the voice sidecar's `killChildSync` imports `spawnSync` under an inline disable. `scripts/__tests__/child-process-lint-scope.test.ts` proves the ban in every folder of each tree it lists, the restated bans, and that no host bundle (desktop main, Damocles Core, the extension) includes an allowed file. libuv's `uv_spawn` calls `CreateProcessW` synchronously on the thread that starts the process, so the launcher starts processes on a pool of worker threads (`dist/process-launcher-worker.js`, run with `SHARE_ENV` so a child inherits the same `process.env`); the host thread only posts requests and reads events. On Windows, every start in a Damocles process holds that process's spawn lock (`spawn-lock.ts`) around its synchronous `spawn`, on every thread: libuv 1.52 keeps a child's stdio ends inheritable for the length of `uv_spawn`, so a start on another thread inside it hands its child copies, and a long-lived child then holds a short one's output open so that its `close` never comes. Workers that start processes adopt the lock from their `workerData` before their first start, `releaseSpawnLockOnExit` frees it when such a worker ends inside a start, an `execFileSync` or `spawnSync` would hold it for the whole run (only `killChildSync` does, at deactivate), and host threads take it through `lockThreadSpawns`; what stays outside and why is `docs/perf.md` "Concurrent starts on Windows". Each host process installs its own pool before its first start (`installProcessLauncher`: desktop main before the login shell read, Damocles Core at its start in `core-host/index.ts`, VS Code in `activate`); tests install `createInProcessLauncher`, which runs the same request handling on the test's thread. A process's pipes belong to the worker that started it, and its output crosses as transferred buffers that the worker stops reading past `MAX_UNACKED_BYTES` unacknowledged. A start goes to a worker not inside a start, preferring one with no live process up to `POOL_SIZE` and the fewest live processes past it; more workers start only while every one is inside a start, up to `MAX_WORKERS`, and past that starts queue. A start with `crossSpawn` (MCP stdio servers, npm) is resolved on the worker as cross-spawn resolves it: on Windows through PATH and PATHEXT, a .cmd or .bat shim run through `cmd.exe` with escaped arguments, and a file not found reported as `ENOENT` from cmd.exe's exit code 1. On Windows every start gets its own call job (no kill-on-close), so a tree kill reaches what the process started, whatever signal it names; no call job carries `BREAKAWAY_OK`, so a descendant that asks to leave it (`CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB`) fails to start, under git, ripgrep, MCP servers, npm and hooks as under the shell tools; a POSIX tree kill of a detached start sends its signal (SIGKILL when it names none) to the group; the worker releases it at `close`, so a tree kill still reaches what an exited process left holding its output, as a POSIX group kill of a detached start does until `close`. A kill with `whileRunning` (the shell tools' Stop and timeout) is refused once the worker saw the exit, so a Stop that races the exit never reaches the jobs a finished command left running. A write resolves once the chunk reached the process's stdin and rejects with the write error, or when the process closes or its worker dies first. A start with `exitOutputIdleMs` (the shell tools, so a backgrounded job holding the shell's stdout does not hold the call open) has its output closed by the worker once that long passes after the exit with no new chunk; the timer runs beside the reads on the worker, so a worker held inside another start delays both alike, as the host thread did when it ran both. A session job is created once on one worker; on Windows its raw handle value (koffi 3 hands a pointer as a BigInt, and handles are process-wide) goes with every start that names it, to any worker, and its handle is closed only once no start naming it is still unanswered, so no worker assigns to a closed or reused handle; a POSIX session job is its sentinel, whose pipe lives on the worker that started it, so every start naming it runs on that worker. When a worker dies, every process it ran fails with `LauncherWorkerDiedError` and a replacement worker kills each one, because a worker's end does not end its children: through its call job (Windows), its process group (a detached POSIX start), or its pid alone (a non-detached POSIX start, whose descendants survive, and a Windows start without a job, through `taskkill /T`). A process that already exited is reached only through its job or group, never by a pid another process may hold by then. A POSIX session job's sentinel sees its pipe close and kills its groups, and the job's next start makes a new sentinel; a Windows session job whose creator died is closed by another worker from its raw handle. `dispose()` (main's pool at quit) fails every process, ends the workers, kills what they ran the same way and closes every shared session job; a session job disposed after it starts no worker.

## Permissions and plan mode

- All tool calls route through the central permission gate (`permission-gate.ts`): read auto-allowed, write/shell → diff approval.
- **A check that cannot decide blocks the call, whatever the tool** (`gateErrorFallback`, used when the gate throws, when no panel owns the session, and when a PreToolUse hook failed for a write or shell call). A read is not exempt, because a deny or ask rule may name the file it reads and an undecided check cannot show that none does. The block is policy-worded and never terminates. A rule list entry that is not a string never reaches the matcher: `readPermissionsFromPath` drops it and logs the file path and index (or that the list is not a list), never the value, which may hold a secret.
- **A PreToolUse hook an abort ended blocks the call** (`dispatchToolCall`, `PreToolUseHookGate.run`). The gate hands the hooks the signal its approval prompt and checkpoint wait take (the run's, combined with the call's Stop for a shell call), and `runHook` kills the running hook's process tree on its abort, as for every other hook event; no later hook in the chain starts. The chain then reports `{ aborted: true }`, never null (null means no hook matched), and no decision the hooks before it returned applies: no rewrite, context or allow, because the killed hook may have been the one that would deny. Their system messages are still shown, since a notice to the user decides nothing. The gate answers every wait that signal ended the same way (`abortedWhileWaiting`): a Stop gets the Stop's block, and a run abort an unasked deny worded with `formatPolicyBlockReason`, as an aborted approval prompt settles through `buildUnaskedDenyResult`; never `terminate`, never the full gate and never an allow. pi 1.4.0 replaces a `tool_call` result with "Operation aborted" when the call's signal aborted while the handlers ran (`prepareToolCall`, `agent-loop.js:506-511` in pi-agent-core), but a nested call a tool makes through `executeTool` is checked against the signal that tool passes (`extensions/runner.js:719`, `agent-session.js:391-407` in pi-coding-agent) while the handler's `ctx.signal` is the run's (`agent-session.js:2818`), so pi can use the gate's answer.
- **A prompt moves its owner's card to `awaiting_approval` in either store, and status writes follow `replacesToolStatus`** (`stores/tool-cancelled-status.ts`). The live statuses share one rank, because a prompted call goes running, awaiting_approval, running in the order core sends them, and nothing live replaces an outcome. A card awaiting approval shows no Stop.
- **A permission mode or YOLO change re-checks every open approval and skill prompt** (`PermissionHandler.setPermissionMode`, `setDangerouslySkipPermissions`). Each open prompt holds `runsUnasked`, the gate's own verdict (`runsUnasked` in `permission-gate.ts`) over the settings rule the gate read once for that call (`CanUseToolContext.rule`, which `canUseTool` also decides from), and one the new state would run unasked is approved as the user's yes would be, posting `permissionAutoResolved` with `approvedBy`. Questions, forms, plans and elicitations stay open, and a re-check that throws leaves its prompt open. Option 2 on an Edit, Write or GenerateImage prompt, "Yes, and accept all edits this session", shows only in Default mode, for any owner, and switches the whole chat to `acceptEdits`; shell and other tools never offer it.
- **A panel starts from its folder's defaults, except a fork, which takes its source's mode and YOLO, and a desktop engine restart's reopen, which takes what the ended core held** (`HostSetUpOptions.permissions`; "Desktop host", the engine crash restart). Recovery never grants a chat more than it had. YOLO goes back to its default whenever the panel starts a new conversation (`resetForNewConversation`: a clear and every folder switch that replaces the conversation). A chat move (`chatMove`: a hand-off, or a worktree chat's first send) continues its conversation in another folder, so the chat keeps its mode and YOLO, which resetting could raise, and drops only the session skill approvals of the folder it left (`dropSessionApprovals`). The restore moving a restarted chat to its conversation's folder keeps both.
- **`HostCapabilities.bypassPermissions` false hides the composer's YOLO control, except while YOLO is on, so the user can always turn it off** (`App.yoloRefusal.test.ts`); core's `permissionModeRefused` still shows the calm refusal notice.
- **Settings rules decide before any mode or tool default, deny > ask > allow across every settings file** (`EvaluatorManager.matchRule`). A deny in any file wins, then an ask in any file, then an allow, so a repository's committed allow never overrides the user's own deny or ask, and an ask prompts even when a more specific allow matches. Only `dangerouslySkipPermissions` skips rules. An ask rule prompts wherever Damocles would otherwise run the call unasked: a read-only tool or read-only-annotated MCP tool in any mode, an Edit, Write or GenerateImage in acceptEdits, a read-only shell command in plan mode or for a read-only agent, an in-process module tool (`GATEABLE_MODULE_NAMES`), and the plan file. Plan mode's and the read-only agent's blocks still come first. A tool with no prompt of its own gets the generic webview prompt (`ApprovalManager.handleToolPermission`), which registers in `PermissionState` like every other prompt; never answer one with a host modal, which leaves the session reading as working while it waits.
- **The plan-file carve-out resolves the path as the tool does** (`PermissionHandler.isPlanFile`, `resolveToCwd` against the session cwd), in the evaluator and in the gate's plan-mode check alike. `isPlanFilePath` takes the absolute path the tool writes; a relative path would resolve against the process cwd and let a plan-mode write through to a file the evaluator does not see as the plan file. Both the path as written (`spelledPath`: 8.3 names, case and `\\?\` resolved, no link followed) must sit below the plans folder spelled the same way, and the file it resolves to (`canonicalPath`, links and dangling links followed) below the canonical plans folder, with a `.md` name; on win32 the comparison and the extension ignore case. An existing file with more than one hard link is not the plan file either (`nlink > 1`): a hard link in the plans folder shares its content with a name outside it, and no path comparison can see that. A junction, symlink or hard link inside `~/.damocles/plans` therefore never carries the carve-out to a file outside it, and a `~/.damocles` that is itself a link (a dotfiles repository) still works, because each side is compared with the plans folder resolved the same way. It is evaluated after the settings rules, so a deny or ask rule on the plan file wins. In plan mode, a `Bash`/`PowerShell` command `readonly-shell.ts` (fail-closed allowlist) proves read-only auto-runs and any other goes through `canUseTool` (prompt, settings rules, YOLO approves); non-plan-file `Edit`/`Write` stay blocked, YOLO included (the session plan file is the sole write carve-out); memory/Compass/enabled MCP tools stay available. A subagent whose resolved toolset has no write tool (`ResolvedToolset.readOnly` → `readOnlyShell`) gets the same shell rule in every mode, so a shell write never runs without passing the approval flow. Under YOLO only the prompt (`plan-mode-guidance.ts`, the agent and reviewer prompts) keeps the shell from writing.
- A block the runtime made itself (settings rule, plan mode, read-only agent, hook) uses `formatPolicyBlockReason`/`POLICY_BLOCK_MARKER`; only a real user rejection, an approval-prompt answer or a Stop of a shell call the gate still held, uses `formatDenyReason`/`FEEDBACK_MARKER`. Never tell the model the user refused something the user was never asked. A policy block renders as "Blocked" with its reason and a user rejection as "denied"; `FEEDBACK_MARKER` is persisted in old sessions, so its value must not change.
  - The deciding flag is `PermissionResult.policy`, set only by `buildUnaskedDenyResult`, and it covers every deny nobody answered: a settings rule, no webview, no tool use id, an abort before the answer, teardown. The gate and the three tools that call `canUseTool` themselves (AskUserQuestion, BrowserRequestInput, ExitPlanMode) word a `policy` deny with `formatPolicyBlockReason`. A prompt manager knows the user answered only through `userAnswered` on its result, which only its resolve method sets, so any other settle builds an unasked deny. An abort of the call's signal always settles a prompt as that deny, approval and plan prompts alike: the signal aborts only when the run stops or the user stops that shell call, and there is no abort that means yes. A prompt tool that ran under the aborted run (AskUserQuestion, BrowserRequestInput, ExitPlanMode) also gets the stopped marker on its result (`stopped-tool-result.ts`), so its card reads stopped, live and on reload (`resolveCancelledStatus`), never "Blocked".
- **File rules match the path the tool writes, with Claude Code's gitignore semantics** (`EvaluatorManager.matchFileSpecifier`, shared by Read, Edit, Write, GenerateImage and the searches below; spec: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/permissions.md, "Read and Edit"). `file_path` resolves with the tools' own `resolveToCwd` against the session cwd (`PermissionState.cwd`, which the panel sets from its folder next to `workspacePath`; the home folder when there is no project). `fileRulePattern` (`permission-settings.ts`) splits a rule's path into an anchor and a gitignore pattern: `//path` from the file system root; `~/path` from the home `resolveToCwd` uses; `/path` from the root of the settings file that holds the rule (`FilePermissions.root`: the project folder for the four project files, the file's own directory for a home file, as Claude Code resolves `/path` in `~/.claude/settings.json` to `~/.claude/path`); anything else, with a leading `./` dropped, from the cwd. An anchored pattern keeps its leading `/`, so `~/.zshrc` is home's own `.zshrc` and `//**/.env` is needed to reach any depth. A drive-letter path (`C:/x/**`, `C:\x\**`) is a Damocles extension, read as `//c/x/**` with `\` as a separator; everywhere else `\` is gitignore's escape (`\[2024-06\] Reports/**` matches the literal folder). Matching is node-ignore (`ignore`, the gitignore library pi uses) on paths in POSIX form (`C:\Users` as `/c/Users`, a UNC path as `//server/share`), each made relative to the rule's anchor first, so a rule never matches above its anchor and node-ignore never sees a path it would reject (a `\\?\` or `\\.\` device path is not under `//`; the spelled and canonical forms below give its drive form). Depth: a bare name (`.env`, `*.env`) matches at any depth under its anchor and never in a parent folder; a relative pattern with exactly one folder before its last segment (`src/**`, `secrets/**`, `src/*.ts`, after dropping `./`) matches only at `<cwd>/src` as an allow rule and as `**/src/**` (any depth under the cwd) as a deny or ask rule; every other shape (`/src/**`, `src/components/**`, `**/src/**`, `build/`) matches the same in every rule type. A path node-ignore compiles into no rule that can match (`usableGitignorePattern`: blank, a `#` comment, a lone trailing `\`, a bracket that never closes or names an unknown class, ported from node-ignore's `index.js` and kept in step with it) still guards that exact anchored path as a deny or ask rule (on Windows read as a path, `\` as a separator, so `Read(vaultdir\)` guards the folder `vaultdir`); as an allow rule it approves nothing. Negation: a deny or ask rule whose path starts with `!` matches nothing itself and carves its paths out of the relative (`path`, `./path`) rules for the same tool listed before it in the same list of the same settings file (gitignore order, so a first-listed `!` carves nothing); the `!` pattern is always cwd-relative, even as `!/x`, `!~/x` or `!//x`, so it never reaches an anchored rule, and gitignore's parent rule holds (`Read(secrets/**)` then `Read(!secrets/public/**)` still blocks `secrets/public`). Carve-outs never cross settings files. An allow rule cannot be a carve-out. An allow rule needs the requested path inside the cwd and the canonical path inside the canonical cwd, both matching the rule, so `..`, another drive, `~` or an absolute path outside the cwd never earns an allow, an anchored allow rule included. The requested path's cwd-relative form is taken between `spelledPath` of both, which resolves 8.3 names, case, `\\?\` prefixes and loopback admin shares segment by segment and stops at the first link, so `<8.3 cwd>\src\a.ts` earns `Edit(src/**)` while a path through a link keeps the link's name. Deny and ask rules match the requested, spelled and canonical forms, each relative to the anchor as written, spelled and canonical, so no path form escapes them. On win32 the match ignores case (macOS and Linux match case), and an NTFS stream suffix (`name::$DATA`, `dir::$INDEX_ALLOCATION`) is cut off for deny and ask and bars an allow. Every file rule also checks the canonical path (`core/canonical-path.ts`): `realpathSync.native` of the deepest existing ancestor plus the segments below it, so a symlink, junction or 8.3 short name resolves even for a file not yet created, and a UNC path to a drive's admin share on this machine (`\\localhost\C$`, `\\127.0.0.1\C$`, `\\::1\C$`, `\\0--1.ipv6-literal.net\C$`, the host name, any interface address, and their `\\?\UNC\` and `\\.\UNC\` forms) becomes the drive path first, since realpath returns it unchanged. A dangling link is followed to its target (up to 40 hops), because writing through one creates that target, and a segment realpath cannot resolve (missing, unreadable, looping) ends the walk instead of throwing, which would block the call through `gateErrorFallback` instead of applying the rules. The cwd is canonicalized the same way.
- **`Edit` rules cover every tool that edits files, and `Read` deny and ask rules cover Grep, Glob and Ls** (`EDIT_RULE_TOOLS`, `SEARCH_TOOLS` in `evaluator-manager.ts`), as Claude Code applies them. An `Edit` rule, bare or with a path, also matches Write and GenerateImage; a Write or GenerateImage rule still matches only its own tool. A `Read` deny or ask rule (allow never) matches a search through its search root as a file, the root's contents (`<root>/` against the glob, so `secrets/**` covers a search of `secrets` or anything below it) and the root joined with Grep's `glob` or Glob's `pattern`, so `find secret.txt` or `grep --glob secret.txt` is blocked or asks. A search rooted above a covered path is decided by the root and runs; Grep and Glob then leave every file a `Read` deny or ask rule covers out of their results (`PermissionHandler.readRuleFilter`, passed into `createGrepTool`/`createFindTool`) and append a notice saying so. Asking there would fire for every search of a project with a `Read(.env)` rule, since a bare name matches at any depth, and an ask nobody can weigh turns into reflex approval; leaving the files out keeps their content from the model without stopping ordinary searches. Ls is pi's tool and lists names only, so a covered folder's name still shows above it. A file an ask rule covers is left out rather than prompted for, because a search cannot prompt per file; reading it with `Read` prompts. A rule's literal leading folders are resolved too (spelled and canonical), and the rest of the pattern also matches from there, so a `//`, `~/` or `/` rule written through a linked folder also covers its target, Claude Code's `/etc` → `/private/etc` case; on Windows a `//` rule's folders resolve only from a drive (`//c/...`), since `//tmp` is `/tmp`, not `C:\tmp`. A relative deny or ask rule's folders resolve only to a place inside the cwd, so an 8.3 name in the rule covers the long spelling while a link out of the cwd adds nothing. A `Read` deny rule with a path (not ask, not allow) also blocks Edit, Write and GenerateImage on the paths it covers, including creating a file there. acceptEdits does not auto-approve an Edit, Write or GenerateImage whose path is inside the cwd as written but resolves outside it through a link or junction (`EvaluatorManager.linksOutOfCwd`, Claude Code's "Writes through a symlink"); it asks instead. A `Read` rule matches the file pi will open: pi's `resolveReadPath` (`dist/core/tools/path-utils.js`, used by `read.js` through `resolveReadPathAsync`) falls back to a narrow no-break space before AM/PM, NFD, a curly apostrophe and NFD with a curly apostrophe when the requested file is missing, and the package does not export it (its `exports` map has no `core/tools` entry), so `evaluator-manager.ts` ports it and the two must be kept in step. macOS case is not folded.
- **Settings rules apply to MCP tools.** Deny and ask rules are evaluated before MCP's default allow, and an MCP tool no rule names still auto-allows. An ask rule prompts even for a read-only-annotated tool, because the annotation is the server's own claim. Claude Code's three forms match against the call's MCP descriptor, never the pi name: the gate looks up the server's config name and the server's own tool name (`GatePermissionContext.mcpToolIdentity`, from the live descriptors for a panel and from the frozen `NestedMcpToolset.identity` for a nested agent) and passes them as `CanUseToolContext.mcpTool`. `mcp__server` and `mcp__server__*` match when the server part equals the config name as written or as Claude Code writes it (`[^A-Za-z0-9_-]` as `_`), and `mcp__server__tool` does the same for both parts at every `__` split. The pi name is sanitized, cut to 64 characters and hashed, so matching on it let `mcp__srv` cover `srv_` or `srv-`, `mcp__a` cover `a__b`, `mcp__my-srv` cover `my.srv`, and two server names sharing their first 50 characters share rules. A rule equal to the pi name also matches, because the renamed-tool migration writes rules that way; pi names are unique within a session, hashed or not. A call with no descriptor matches only that exact-name form. The three MCP resource tools (`list_mcp_resources`, `list_mcp_resource_templates`, `read_mcp_resource`) are Damocles tools, not descriptors. The gate gives a call that names a non-empty string `server` the identity `{ server, tool }`, read from the input after PreToolUse hooks ran, since a hook's `updatedInput` can change the server. So `mcp__secret`, `mcp__secret__*`, `mcp__*` and `mcp__secret__read_mcp_resource` deny or ask rules cover it, in every mode and for nested agents. They are in `READ_ONLY_TOOLS`, so with no rule they auto-run. A list call without `server` has no identity. Instead it asks `PermissionHandler.mcpResourceRuleFilter`, which leaves out each server a deny or ask rule covers for that list tool, plus each resource a deny on `read_mcp_resource` covers (bare or URI-scoped; ask rules hide nothing). It then names what it left out. A read also leaves out each returned content, as a directory-style read returns child resources, whose URI such a deny covers or an ask rule covers that does not also cover the read itself (`McpResourceRuleFilter.contentCovered`), and says how many. A read cannot ask per content, as Grep and Glob cannot ask per file and leave out what a `Read` deny or ask rule covers; the ask rule that covered the read itself has already asked, so a server-wide ask (`mcp__vault`) still reads a directory, and the content under the read's own URI is never left out by it. YOLO filters nothing. A hook's `match` regex sees the same identity: for a resource call naming a server, `matchEntries` (`hooks/dispatch.ts`) tests it against the real tool name and against `createMcpToolName(server, tool)`, for `tool_call` and `tool_result` hooks. The payload's `tool_name` stays the real name. An `mcp__` rule with parentheses is skipped, as Claude Code skips it, with one Damocles extension: `mcp__<server>__read_mcp_resource(<uri pattern>)` (`mcp-resource-rule.ts`, `matchesMcpResourceUriRule` in `evaluator-manager.ts`). Its pattern is the text between the first `(` and the final `)`, so a URI holding parentheses round-trips. `*` matches any run of characters and is the only wildcard, matched in linear time. The rule matches a `read_mcp_resource` call whose server the tool part names (as the pi name `createMcpToolName` gives, hashed when long, as written, or as Claude Code spells it) and whose `uri`, as the model sent it, matches. A server that accepts other spellings of the same resource needs a wildcard or a server rule. Its tool part is literal: a `*` there (`mcp__*__read_mcp_resource(<uri>)`) matches nothing. Claude Code has no tool by that name, so no Claude Code rule changes meaning. `readPermissionsFromPath` drops any `mcp__` rule with parentheses that can match nothing, in every list (`inertMcpSpecifierRule`: not that form, a wildcard tool part, or a tool other than `read_mcp_resource`), logging the file path and index, since a deny that silently matches nothing fails open; `PiRuntime` logs a `damocles.tools.disabled` entry of those forms, or a bare `mcp__<server>__read_mcp_resource`, by file and index at init and on each change (`inertToolListEntry`). Tool-name wildcards (`*` matches any run of characters, line breaks included, through `matchesToolPattern` in linear time, because a resource call's `server` is model-written and unbounded; never a generated regex, which backtracks) follow Claude Code's "Tool name wildcards": a deny or ask rule's tool name may be a glob of the full name (`*` matches every tool, `mcp__*` every MCP tool, `Bas*` Bash), with the server and tool parts of an `mcp__` glob matched against the descriptor's names as above. An allow rule may glob only after a literal `mcp__<server>__` (`mcp__puppeteer__*`, `mcp__github__get_*`); `readPermissionsFromPath` drops any other allow glob (`*`, `B*`, `mcp__*`, `mcp__git*__x`), an allow rule starting with `!`, an allow rule whose path is not a usable gitignore pattern, and an allow `Write(path)` rule (Claude Code consults only `Edit(path)` and `Read(path)`; a deny or ask `Write(path)` is still matched, because dropping it would fail open), logging the file path and index, never the value.
- **`terminate` is per-batch, not per-call, and it is emitted only for an unexplained "no".** pi ends the turn only when every finalized result in the batch is terminating and the batch is non-empty. `executeToolCalls` dispatches to a sequential or a parallel batch runner, and both take the flag from `shouldTerminateToolBatch`, which requires `finalizedCalls.length > 0 && finalizedCalls.every((finalized) => finalized.result.terminate === true)`, consumed by the agent loop as `hasMoreToolCalls = !executedToolBatch.terminate` (`@earendil-works/pi-agent-core@1.4.0`, shipped at `node_modules/@earendil-works/pi-agent-core/dist/agent-loop.js:471-473` and `:172`; the line numbers are only meaningful against that installed version, so re-read the file before trusting them). So denying one of three parallel calls does not stop the agent: the other two ran and produced results the model must see. That is correct, not a bug. Do not "fix" it by terminating harder. Damocles sets it in exactly two places: the interactive `canUseTool` deny, conditional on `PermissionResult.interrupt`, and a `tool_call` hook that explicitly opted in with `"terminate": true` alongside its deny (read strictly as the JSON boolean `true`; a string or number is ignored). `interrupt` is set by exactly two functions, `buildUserDenyResult` and `buildUserFileEditDenyResult` (`src/core/permission-handler/utils.ts`), and only when the user actually answered the prompt (`ApprovalResult.userAnswered`) and left the feedback box empty. A deny with feedback is *instruction* the model must act on, so it must not end the turn. Every deny the user was never asked about is built by `buildUnaskedDenyResult`, which cannot set it. Grepping `interrupt` under `src/core/permission-handler/` is therefore a complete enumeration of the terminating sites. Keep it that way. Everywhere else stays non-terminating on purpose: the gate's own error fallback (a Damocles bug must not kill the user's turn), settings-rule denials (policy, not a user decision), and above all the plan-mode / read-only-agent write blocks, whose entire reason for existing is to make the model self-correct. Terminating there breaks plan mode.
- The on-disk plan file is the single source of truth for plan-mode handoff. `ExitPlanMode` takes no `plan` argument; everything reads it via `ChatSession.getPlanContent()`.
- **A plan's version is stamped by core and recorded with its call; the webview never counts plans** (D51, `plan-version.ts`). `ExitPlanMode` numbers its call one more than the plans the user was shown before it on the session's branch, which runs from the root, so the plans a compaction summarised count, and a fork or a rewind continues from the plans its own branch holds. A call that showed the user no plan (no plan file, not in plan mode, stopped before `PlanManager` posted it) records a null number and counts for nothing; `PlanManager` marks every result after the post with `planShown`, so a plan the user saw and then stopped keeps its number, and a stop is worded as one, and a result recorded before the stamp existed counts as a plan. Both plan-mode tools are `executionMode: 'sequential'`, because pi gates every call of a parallel batch before running any: a plan file written beside `ExitPlanMode` must exist when it is read, and calls batched after `EnterPlanMode` must meet plan mode's checks. The number goes on the result's `details` (`PLAN_VERSION_DETAIL_KEY`) for every outcome, a sent-back plan included, which is why a denial is returned as an `isError` result rather than thrown (a thrown error's result has empty `details`) and the adapter and the subagent stream bridge forward an error result's `details` as the reload does, and on the plan approval request through `CanUseToolContext.planVersion`, so `canUseTool`'s input stays as the model sent it. The plan card and the dock banner read that number (`usePlanSummary.ts`); a call recorded before the stamp existed shows no version rather than a counted one, since a count over the loaded messages restarts after a compaction or in a paged history.
- A `damocles.*` setting whose workspace value grants a capability or relaxes a guard must be listed in `capabilities.untrustedWorkspaces.restrictedConfigurations` in `package.json`.
