Every setting that declares no `scope` defaults to `window`, so a repository sets it from its own `.vscode/settings.json` and `config.get` returns that value in a workspace the user never trusted. Listing it makes VS Code serve the user value instead until trust is granted, with no code change at the read site. The bar is a capability grant, not a preference: a budget, a model choice or a display toggle does not qualify, and an over-broad list breaks legitimate per-workspace configuration for trusted users. The exception is `damocles.imageGeneration.model`, which decides what each image costs on the user's OpenRouter key and is user-only like `damocles.imageGeneration.enabled`.
- Compass is off in an untrusted workspace, and the gate is `isCompassEnabled(platform, folderPath)` (`compass/index.ts`), never a call site. That one function is what tool eligibility (`moduleToolNames`), the system prompt section, the webview handlers, the sidebar views and `ensureInitialized` all read (the views directly, the rest through the `CompassService.isEnabled` getter that returns it), so trust belongs in that function and the rest follows with no other edit. The reason is what Compass does with a repository: it walks the entire tree, parses every source file into `~/.damocles/compass/<hash>/graph.db`, returns raw source lines to the model through `compass_review_context(include_source)`, and runs git inside the workspace. `damocles.compass.enabled` sitting in `restrictedConfigurations` only stops a repository turning Compass on for itself and does nothing about a user who enabled it globally, which is everyone who uses Compass. Prevent the work, do not filter its output: untrusted means no worker, no walk and no watcher. Trust is granted and never revoked in place, so the service listens on `TrustService.onDidGrantTrust`, filtered to its own folder, and starts then with no window reload, if something already asked it to start, and there is no reachable state in which an index built while untrusted keeps serving.
- Every git call in `compass/git.ts` passes `-c core.fsmonitor=false`, an argv rather than a shell command string, and no `ExecEnv`. Git reads the repository's own `.git/config` first and runs `core.fsmonitor` as a command while refreshing the index, so `git diff` in a hostile clone is arbitrary code execution; `compass/__tests__/git.integration.test.ts` verifies against real git that the hook fires without the override and not with it. An `ExecEnv` from the checkpoint engine would retarget git at its private bare repo and answer about the wrong repository. This is the same finding as the ones fixed in `mcp-local-gitignore.ts` and in the team ledger's tree fingerprint (`tools/team-tools.ts`, which also runs no git in an untrusted folder), so treat any new git caller as needing both, unless it is gated on trust as the source control engine is: that engine runs the user's git with hooks, `core.fsmonitor` and filters, as VS Code does, and starts no git process at all in an untrusted project (see "Source control").
- **The branch label never runs git** (`workspace-folders/git-head.ts`). The chat header and the desktop Projects list show a folder's branch from one `BranchTracker` in `WorkspaceFolderRegistry`, which reads `.git`, a `.git` file's `gitdir:` and `<gitdir>/HEAD` with bounded reads and re-reads HEAD on its watcher. That is why it may run in an untrusted folder, where a `git` process would honour the repository's `core.fsmonitor`. Because the folder is untrusted, a `gitdir:` that resolves to a UNC or device path outside the folder's own root names no repository (opening it would send the user's NTLM credentials over SMB), and `.git` and `HEAD` are read only when they are regular files, opened non-blocking so a FIFO never holds a thread. Only the parsed name leaves core: a branch that passes git's `check-ref-format` rules with no control or bidi-override character, or a detached HEAD's 7-character id.
- Plan-mode guidance has one source: `pi-session/plan-mode-guidance.ts` (`buildPlanModeGuidance`), consumed by `agent-start.ts`, `tools/plan-mode-tools.ts` and `plan-mode-change.ts`. Edit the builder, not the call sites.
- **A plan-mode change reaches the model as a hidden `damocles-plan-mode-change` message whenever the latest plan-mode statement in its transcript is stale** (`plan-mode-change.ts`): a section patch, a notice, or a successful `EnterPlanMode`/`ExitPlanMode` result, whichever is newest. It is checked before each later step (chained onto pi's `prepareNextTurnWithContext`), at prompt start (a `before_agent_start` handler after the prompt handler, since pi patches a section only when it changes), and before a plan-mode nudge. The composer's mode button, Shift+Tab and YOLO stay enabled during a turn, because an approval prompt exists only then.
- The Damocles system prompt has one assembler: `agent-start.ts` (`assembleDamoclesSystemPrompt`). Never read `session.systemPrompt` for display. It holds pi's boilerplate outside a turn.

## Browser

- A page Damocles closed or released reads no more navigation state (`PageEntry.closing`), since Chromium still reports its requests failing while it closes; a quit closes every page this way.
- Browser tools resolve tabs through the caller's `BrowserAgentScope`, never a global active page. A chat's main agent and its human share that chat's human scope (`BrowserService.chatScope`): on VS Code every chat gets the one `PRIMARY_SCOPE_ID`, and with `WindowService.chatBrowserPane` (desktop) each chat panel gets its own, registered for the chat's lifetime and kept across a browser teardown as the primary one is. Every subagent/team agent passes its own id, created with the chat its session runs in, and its scope is disposed at settle (tabs closed only on success). A human scope is never disposed by an agent and is the only kind that reveals a page it opens or navigates.
- Every page records the chat panel that owns it (`PageEntry.chat`), separately from its scope, because a failed subagent's kept tab outlives its scope: an explicit open takes the scope's chat, a popup takes its opener's chat (and the opener's scope while it lives, else that chat's human scope), and a racing `context.on('page')` registration reads the explicit owner first. Core passes it as `PanelOptions.owner`; VS Code ignores it. With chat-owned browser pages (desktop) a page whose owner is unknown or already closed is closed at registration, never re-attributed to whichever chat launched Chromium, and a chat panel's dispose closes every page it owns. The human's paths resolve the requesting chat on desktop and the primary scope on VS Code: the chat header's open browser (`showForChat`, which reveals the page the human watches there at its address and opens a blank page only when there is none), View › New Browser Page and the page's own New Tab (the page's chat), a pick requested from a chat (that chat's page) and a pick from a page's toolbar (posted to the page's chat, else the active chat). On desktop the page's editor tab draws its navigation chrome, so core omits the page's in-page toolbar there and posts `navigationState` (loading, back and forward, from `Page.getNavigationHistory`) for the host to read; the VS Code page HTML is unchanged.
- Anything a page produces is hostile input on the extension host. Console/network/picker output is redacted and bounded at capture (`redaction.ts` + `collectors.ts`), never at a render site. The guarantee must not depend on whoever adds the next reader. The in-page bridge's caps live in the page's own world and are the page's to edit, so every bound is re-enforced host-side in `installContextObservers`/`onConsoleBinding`. Every redaction pattern must be linear-time (unrolled-loop form, bounded affixes): it runs synchronously on the host, so a backtracking pattern is a remote editor freeze.
- `ScreencastHealth`'s stall clock is armed by intent (`noteWanted`, on panel visibility), not by the CDP `start()` call. `start()` runs only once the page's webview is listening (`BrowserPanel.listening`): on its `ready` message, or on a hidden-to-shown edge when the host kept the hidden page running (`PanelHost.retainsContextWhenHidden`, the desktop page views), since such a page posts no second `ready`. A VS Code browser panel has no `retainContextWhenHidden`, so its webview is rebuilt on show and only `ready` starts it. Anchoring the clock on `start()` would make the watchdog blind to a lost handshake.
- **A blank open never navigates** (`openBlankForScope`, `openTabForScope`, `openNewTab`, `restoreChatPages`, and `openForScope` on a page whose last loaded address is `about:blank`). A page Damocles just created or adopted already is `about:blank`, and its tab accepts typed addresses as soon as it is presented, so a `Page.navigate('about:blank')` sent after that supersedes the typed address (the typed navigation is answered `net::ERR_ABORTED`). An agent's explicit `browser_open about:blank` on a page that shows something else still navigates. View › New Browser Page always opens a new page: a launch it starts presents its first page, and behind a launch already in flight it opens a new tab.
- **The user-agent rewrite is applied before a tab is presented, and nothing awaits between registering a page and presenting it** (`doRegisterPage`). The context's user agent is read once per launch over a browser-level CDP session (`Browser.getVersion`), never with page JS, which stalls while a dialog is open. Registration awaits that read and applies the override before its teardown check, so a teardown during either await drops the page instead of presenting a tab for a dead context, and a typed address's first request carries the clean UA.
- **An agent navigation follows Playwright's `page.goto` contract** (`navigateEntry`). A `net::ERR_*` answer to `Page.navigate` (DNS, refused, TLS, aborted) rejects `openForScope` and `openNewTab`, so `browser_open`, `browser_navigate` and `browser_tabs` return a tool error. A navigation that becomes a download rejects too, with a message pointing to BrowserDownloads, since `isDownload` comes with `net::ERR_ABORTED`. Any HTTP status counts as loaded: the main frame's navigation response status (`PageEntry.documentStatus`) comes back from the open, and the tool result names a status of 400 or more. That includes an empty-bodied 4xx/5xx, which Chromium answers `net::ERR_HTTP_RESPONSE_CODE_FAILURE` after the response arrived. A signal that has already aborted sends no `Page.navigate`.
- **`lastUrl` keeps the last address the main frame loaded** (`onMainFrameNavigated`). Chromium commits its error page as `chrome-error://chromewebdata/` after a failed load; that commit leaves `lastUrl` alone, and the address bar, the tab and every agent-facing URL (`getScopeCurrentUrl`, `listTabs`, a snapshot's `[URL]`) show the address that failed (the main frame's failed navigation request), as Chrome's omnibox does. Desktop saves the address its page tab shows, so a restored page whose load fails keeps its tab and retries the same address on every restart. A failed load is logged once, by origin only, since a URL can carry tokens: by the tool's error result for an agent, by the restore path for a restored page, and by the address bar for a typed address, which keeps its https-to-http fallback. A refused address-bar entry is logged by its origin only, never its text.
- `damocles.browser.devToolsPort` (default off) opens an unauthenticated loopback CDP endpoint on the logged-in profile when enabled; it is a launch-time flag, so a change needs a relaunch.
- Chromium's sandbox is always on (`chromiumSandbox: true` in `launcher.ts`); never pass `--no-sandbox`, whatever environment fails to launch. Pages render in the user's logged-in profile. Chromium refuses to run as root with the sandbox on, so `launchBrowserContext` refuses a POSIX root process before launching, and maps Chromium's "Running as root without --no-sandbox" and "No usable sandbox" launch errors to localized messages that say how to fix the environment.

## Teams and subagents

- **Team agents get the same plan-mode notice, carrying their role's directive** (`teamPlanModeStatement` in `team/prompts.ts`). Their prompts keep the mode the team started or resumed in, while their gate reads the live mode. Subagents get no notice: their gate reads the live mode.
- **A resume note is never a steer** (`withResumeNote` in `shared/steer.ts`): on every resume and restart path (a subagent reopened from its file or re-run from its task, a team lead relaunched as interrupted, failed or plain, or restarted from its task) the note follows the prompt under `RESUME_NOTE_RULES`, every line quoted with `> ` so none opens with the marker, and the agent does what it asks, follows it where it explicitly changes or replaces a planned step, and ends planned work early only on the note's explicit instruction to stop, skip or drop work.
- Team message delivery branches on `TeamMessage.kind`, never on rendered text (a peer can mimic any prefix). `scratchpad-notice` reaches only a `standby` specialist; `ledger-notice` reaches no inbox at all (the ledger is a pull surface). Both still broadcast, so persistence/timeline/`getInbox` see every write. Filter at delivery, never at emission. Delivery to a member is at least once, with an idempotent receiver: every queued delivery carries ids (a bus message its `messageId`, an operator steer or user note a UUID minted when its run queues it), the checkpoint's undelivered lists carry them, and right after pi commits a delivered message as a user entry the runner appends `damocles-team-delivered { ids }` to the same member session (one microtask after its `message_end`, because pi appends the message synchronously once listeners return). A resume, from a live or a sealed checkpoint, drops an undelivered entry only when that session records all of its ids, because pi can commit a delivery after the snapshot read it or after the stop took it; it reads those records asynchronously before the restored state is written (`dropDeliveredMessages`). A stop's take and a live snapshot's peek both list a delivery handed to pi that pi has neither queued nor committed, and a delivery pi refuses before committing it goes back to the head of the pending list, so no path drops a message from every list. A prompt that pi's input handlers still hold when the member stops, or when its run throws and lets go of the session, is one of those: pi opens its run only once the handlers return, which neither pi's `abort()` (`agent-session.js:1971-1980` in pi 1.4.0) nor the session's disposal waits for. So every prompt the runner hands pi, steers included, carries a `preflightResult` that aborts the run pi opens with it after that point at its `agent_start`, before any request (`abortRunAtStart`, which also aborts the Agent from a listener of its own, because a disposed session no longer turns its abort mark into one), and a stopped runner hands pi nothing more. The message stays where the stop or the failure listed it: a team stop's take and a failure's peek keep it for the resumed member, and a member's own Stop drops it with the rest of its inbox. No held prompt keeps the run open, a steer or one the loop awaits (the opening task, a woken message): the runner tracks every prompt it hands pi until it settles (`handOff`), and a stop ends the wait on each one pi still holds, though it still waits for a run pi already opened, whoever opened it, which the stop's abort settles. The runner hands pi its opening task and each message it prompts only once pi is idle (`RunLifecycle.untilIdle`), so a run a user's pi extension opened (`sendUserMessage`, a prompt its `agent_settled` handler deferred into the settle) ends first, and passes each as a follow-up, so a run another prompt opens while pi's input handlers hold it takes it as a follow-up rather than refusing it; a stop aborts a run pi is still preparing at its start (`abortRunAtStart`), even once the session is let go of, and the runner lets go of the session only once no run past its `agent_start` is open in it. A subagent's run does the same (`runSubagent`), and a steer that reaches a subagent while pi settles the task's own run is answered `finished`, since that run takes no more messages. The configured hooks pi awaits in that run's wind-down, PostToolUse in a call's `tool_result` and SubagentStop in `agent_settled`, take the stop's signal (`AgentMcpContext.stopSignal`, the specialist's own controller or the team's for the lead, passed on as `SubagentGateContext.stopSignal`, which is required, so a plain subagent passes its record's `abortController` signal too), so the stop kills each running hook's process tree and starts none after it; pi hands an `agent_settled` handler no signal of its own, since the agent's run is over by then (`agent.js:217-219` in pi-agent-core 1.4.0). A PreToolUse hook takes no stop signal of its own: the stop aborts the member's pi run (`onAbort` in `agent-runner.ts`, `forwardAbortSignal` in `subagent-runner.ts` for a subagent), whose signal the gate hands the hooks, so it is killed the same way and its call blocked ("A PreToolUse hook an abort ended blocks the call"). Without a stop a hook is still bounded by its `timeoutMs` (60 s by default, at most 600 s). The runner sets no timeout of its own: a handler of a pi extension the user installed, which pi loads into every member session (`package-manager.js:2055`, and the project's `.pi/extensions` in a trusted project, `:2035-2038`) and which nothing can abort, can hold the run, and the runner waits for it as pi's own `prompt()` does (`_startRun` awaits `_emitAgentSettled`, `agent-session.js:1471`), because letting go would dispose a session pi still writes that run's wind-down to (`_commitBoundaryDrafts` after the `agent_before_settle` handlers, `:1572-1573`) or opens a run on (a prompt an `agent_settled` handler queued, `:1632-1634`, `:695-699`). pi's `abort()` waits only until the session is idle (`:1971-1981`), which it already is while the `agent_settled` handlers run (`:685`). A team stop is still bounded by the drain (`SPECIALIST_DRAIN_TIMEOUT_MS`), so a team can end while a held member run still owns its session file (next bullet). The card says what such a wait is for once it outlasts `WIND_DOWN_NOTICE_MS` (3 s, `wind-down-notice.ts`), longer than a wind-down nothing holds takes, a hook tree's kill included: the owner of the agent's card (`TeamRunner.publishWindDown` for a member or lead, fed by its `AgentRunner`'s `onWindDown`, `AgentManager.postWindDown` for a subagent) holds that state and publishes a `statusUpdate` `windingDown` naming the agent as `parentToolUseId`, again at each lifecycle event, and clears it (`windDown: null`) as its wait ends, before the settled status. Its phase comes from pi's public events: `start` until `agent_start` (the agent loop's own `agent_start` handlers count as `run`), `run` until `agent_end`, whose tool calls and handlers hold the stop's signal, then `settle` until `agent_settled`. It lists the events whose handlers pi may be awaiting with no signal: `input` and `before_agent_start`, or `agent_settled`, with `agent_before_settle` only while the session still streams after a stop that came once the run had ended (pi skips those handlers for a run aborted before its end, `agent-session.js:1450`, `:1457`, and turns `isStreaming` false as its `agent_settled` handlers start, `:685-689`). It lists the extensions loaded from files (`resourceLoader.getExtensions()`) that registered a handler for one of them, or every one for `run`. pi reports no handler it is awaiting, so the card lists candidates and never names one as the holder. The state is display only: it never changes the wait, is never persisted, logged or recorded (it stays out of the checkpoint, the team log and the agent files, and agent times never read it), and the webview never infers it. Its owner holds it in memory, so a reloaded webview is sent it from there: the running team's `getTeamState` carries `windDowns` by agent id, which `requestTeamData` answers a reloaded card with and the team store applies to every agent of that team, and after a replay of the bound conversation rebuilt its subagent cards from their files, which end a run that has not settled, `HistoryManager.loadSessionHistory` calls `PiSession.replayLiveAgents`, whose `AgentManager.replayLive` posts each unsettled run's card as running again (`taskStarted`), its Background Tasks row once it started, and its wind-down. A settled member status also clears it, because a runner that wrote `team-completed` sends nothing more, and that runner's `getTeamState` carries no wind-down. A resumed member or subagent that waits for its session file shows the notice of the run that still owns the file, under its own id, through the same owner, so a reload during the wait is sent it too, and clears it as its wait ends (next bullet). The main chat's Stop shows the turn stopped at once, while its next send waits for the stopped run in `promptPi`: for `abortPromise`, for the prompt pi held at the Stop (`promptInPi`), and for pi to settle the run whose `agent_settled` handlers it runs, whoever opened that run (a cancel note's, a re-steer's or a re-queued follow-up's, an extension's `sendMessage` with `triggerTurn`, a prompt pi deferred), since pi defers a prompt made while those handlers run and returns from it at once (`agent-session.js:1632-1634`, running it only once they return, `:695-699`), and its `abort()` and `waitForIdle()` return at once there, the session being idle already (`:685`, `:1072`, `:1983-1985`). That wait (`PiSession.piSettling`) is a pre-dispatch step of the send ("A send holds the session from its first step") and reads the same run lifecycle the notice does (`RunLifecycle.untilSettled`, "pi's run lifecycle"): it ends at that run's `agent_settled`, so pi's `prompt()` then opens a run of the send's own, with its `preflightResult`, prompt-entry watcher, sidecars, checkpoint baseline and memory candidate. No path-loaded pi extension runs in the main chat (`FolderRuntime`'s `extensionsOverride`), so its holders are Damocles' own handlers, such as the configured Stop hook pi awaits in `agent_settled` with no signal, and tools that ignore the abort, and its line names no extension. `PiSession` binds the run lifecycle of each session it binds (`trackRunLifecycle` in `subscribeEvents`, the one record its notices, its prompts' settle waits, its queue and its compaction read) and arms the same notice on it (`watchWindDown`, so the phase follows the run before any Stop); `endStoppedRun` starts its delay, as `stopRunAtStart` does for the run pi opens after a Stop with a prompt it held then. It has one end, whichever prompt opened the run: pi has finished the run it had open when the delay started (`WindDownNotice.runSettled`), at that run's `agent_settled`, which pi publishes once the handlers return and before the prompts they deferred open the next run (`:689-699`), or at once when no run was open (not `isStreaming`, and no `agent_settled` pending since its `agent_start` or `agent_end`). `abortPromise` and `promptInPi` cannot serve as that end: both settle only after the deferred prompts' runs, and the abort settles at once during the handlers. The notice is then cleared if it showed and the next one armed, and an unbind ends it. Its state rides the session state (`sessionStateChanged.windDown`, published only by `publishSessionState`), not a prompt kind, since nothing on it waits for an answer, so the republish on a webview restart (`onWebviewReady`) carries it, and the status bar shows it where the working phrase goes. A send waiting in `promptPi` meanwhile still waits, unrefused and in order, and a second Stop still gives it back to the composer, since no send reaches pi while it settles a run. A `/compact` waits for the same settle before pi's `compact()`, which would otherwise compact while the handlers run (its `abort()` returns at once there, `:2231`). That wait is a pre-dispatch step like a send's: a Stop, a new chat, a resume switch or the disposal (`stopSends`) aborts its signal (`compactionBeforePi`), which ends the wait at once, and the compaction never starts; a Stop's chat shows it as a stopped manual compaction (`compactionAborted`, "Compaction stopped before it finished"), and `/compact` echoes only once accepted, so no echo is left. A compaction that finds a run open after those waits, one no send opened (a cancel note's, a re-steer's, an extension's), is refused as during a send's turn, since pi's `compact()` aborts the run it finds (`:2231`). pi's own auto-compaction runs inside a run and stops with it. `forgetSession` runs as the run ends and releases the member's file (next bullet), so a run pi opens with a held prompt afterwards commits nothing to the member's file, which a resume may already have reopened, and a failure that prompt settles with is logged. A held woken message is an inbox message and stays undelivered like a held steer. A held opening task is never inbox content and never reaches the file, so a member with no earlier file restarts from its task, as after any stop before its task was committed, and one with a file resumes from it. The member's transcript shows a run's opening prompt only once pi commits it, as pi committed it (the user `message_end` of the run its `preflightResult('started')` opened), so neither the live card nor a reload shows a task pi never received; the task itself shows from the team's record of the attempt (`TeamAgent.task`, in the work fields entry below). Exactly once is impossible because pi writes the delivered message itself and its entry cannot carry the ids, so a crash between those two appends delivers the message again. History and transcript readers skip the entry.
- **A team member's or subagent's session file has one writer: the run that opened it, until that run settled** (`agent-file-owner.ts`). A stopped run a user's pi extension holds can outlive everything that stopped it: the team's drain, a session switch's `retireAgents` wait and a panel close all let go of it while pi can still append that run's wind-down to the file (the bullet above). So the run takes the file before pi opens it (`takeAgentFile` in `TeamRunner.createMemberSession` and `AgentManager.run`), and only the same run releases it, once its run settled and its session was disposed: the team runner's `forgetMemberSession`, which the `AgentRunner` calls after its wind-down wait, and `AgentManager.afterComplete`, which also runs after an earlier `dispose`. Never at the team's end: the drain keeps a team stop bounded, so a team may show Stopped while a held member still owns its file. A team resume, a restart from a member's task and a subagent resume that find the file owned in this process wait for the release (`waitForAgentFile`), with the owner's wind-down notice on the waiting card, and their stop (Stop team, the member's Stop, a panel close, a delete's reset, a subagent's Stop or ESC) ends the wait, so the run ends stopped without opening the file. Nothing sent meanwhile is lost or misrouted: a waiting member's runner subscribed to the bus and bound its note sink before it opens the session, and a waiting subagent queues steers until its session exists, so both reach the session the run opens. A redispatch starts a new attempt in its own file and never waits. The key is the file's pi session id, the store id for a new file (`teamMemberSessionId`, the subagent id) and for a reopened one the id its name carries (`agentFileSessionId`, from pi's `<timestamp>_<sessionId>.jsonl`, `session-manager.js:713`, `:1241`, `:1396` in pi 1.4.0), so nothing opens a file to learn its owner (pi's `SessionManager.open` can append to a file it opens, `:366-367`) and a fork's copy, which pi gives a new id, has its own. The run holds the file as the writer of its session lease (`session-store/session-lease.ts`), so a file another Damocles process holds, which a handover can leave behind, is refused rather than waited for, because no event from another process says when it lets go. A team resume and a subagent resume check it in their validation (`agentFileHeldElsewhere`), before anything is written, so the refusal names why, the team or subagent stays resumable and nothing is recorded; `takeAgentFile` refuses again at the open, for a process that took the file after the check. A lost lease mutes the session's file writer as a panel's does. A delete does not wait for a held member or subagent run, whose files lie in the deleted conversation's agent data: `deleteAgentData` first mutes every session of this process that owns a file there (`muteAgentFilesUnder`), the detach a held session can make, because a recursive removal that fails part way (Windows refuses one while another process has a file open) would leave the folder, and pi's next `appendFileSync` (`session-manager.js:812`) recreates a removed file in a folder that still exists. `AgentManager` settles each record's promise by record, never by id, because a resume reuses the id while the stopped run it replaces may still settle.
- The verification fingerprint is computed by the extension, never supplied by an agent (`team_record_verification` has no `fingerprint` param, asserted by test). It must fail visibly: `unverifiable`, never a hash that silently missed files. `git status` needs `-uall -z` and paths resolved against `--show-toplevel`. Without all three it goes blind to real edits (collapsed untracked dirs, C-quoted paths, subdirectory workspaces) and a stale pass looks reusable forever. `team_read_scratchpad` adds `currentFingerprint` to every read that returns the `verification` section, the unchanged marker included, because the tree can change while the ledger does not. It is the only way an agent that cannot record a run (a read-only reviewer) or did not record the latest one tells which entries cover the current tree.
- The team profile catalog is generated, never hand-edited: change `agent-profiles/`, run `npm run generate:profiles`, commit the output (a pinned test locks the exact set). Its mirror policy (`TRACKED_DIVISIONS`, `EXCLUDE`, `DIVISION_ALLOWLIST`) is duplicated in `~/.claude/scripts/sync-claude-agents.mjs`, which governs `~/.claude/agents`. Separate repos, no shared state, so edit both together. Both scripts and the generator fail closed (flattened-path collision, duplicate profile id): a profile must never go missing silently.
- Each nested spawn path collapses to one `buildAgentToolset` call (`agent-manager.ts`, and both `team-runner.ts` spawn sites), made inside the per-spawn path for the same reason `buildExtensionFactory` is invoked per spawn: it must read live panel state at spawn. Hoisting it to a construction-time local would freeze the snapshot at team-construction time and silently miss a server the user enabled mid-run. `mcp.names` must reach `tools:`, `customTools` and `mcpToolNames:` from that one call, since an MCP tool that `tools:` does not name can never be activated. And `tools:` must never be narrowed to the deferred baseline, which evicts the tools permanently while ToolSearch still reports success.
- The read-only-agent rule is a shell approval guard (an unproven command goes through `canUseTool`), not a general capability ceiling. `toolCategory('mcp__*')` is `'other'`, so the read-only branch does not catch MCP: a read-only subagent (Explore/Plan, or any agent with no write tool) can invoke a non-annotated MCP tool, and in `default`/`acceptEdits` it still routes through `canUseTool` and prompts. That is a decision, not an oversight. Filtering it would make nested agents stricter than the panel and stricter than plan mode, and most servers omit `readOnlyHint` entirely so it would fail closed against the common case. The trust boundary for MCP is the server enablement list, not the agent type. Do not "fix" it in either direction without changing this line. A team specialist spawned with `kind: 'reviewer'` gets the same posture: `buildTeamEngine().buildAgentToolset` drops every write-category name, `teamAgentPiToolNames` withholds `team_record_verification` (a reviewer judges the implementors' runs in the `verification` ledger and records none of its own; both the names and the definitions read that one function), and the toolset returns `readOnly` computed from the names it resolved (as `resolveAgentToolset` does), and the runner hands that flag to the same spawn's `buildExtensionFactory` as `readOnlyShell`. Never pass `kind` to the gate directly, so no path can pair a write tool with an open shell. `kind` is persisted on `agent-spawned`, so a resumed reviewer keeps the posture, and a redispatch re-declares it.
- A subagent's model is configuration, never the spawning model's choice: the `Agent` tool deliberately has no `model` param. `resolveSubagentModel(agentConfig)` takes only a config. Template `model:` > `damocles.explore.model` (Explore only) > the panel's session model. An agent is never spawned without a model, since pi would then seat it on one it picks itself: the last step fails the spawn when the chat's model cannot run with today's credentials and plan, read again at each spawn: signed out (naming the provider to sign in to), outside the plan (`modelInPlan`), or no model it can run at all. Effort follows the model, and `Agent` has no `thinking` param: a template that pins `model:` uses its `thinking:` (else pi's default), and every other agent uses its template `thinking:`, else the panel's effort. Explore on Default (`''`) runs on the cheap model matched to the panel's provider (`resolveCheapModelFor`: Haiku 5.5, GPT-6 Luna, or the provider def's `cheapModel`, which for a GitHub Copilot chat, a GPT one included, is Copilot's Haiku 5.5) at `medium`, and on the panel's model and effort when that cheap model is signed out, outside the plan or outside `enabledModels`; on a picked model (any `DEFAULT_MODELS` value; `damocles.explore.model` and `.effort` are application scope, read with no folder by `readExploreSetting`, so a project file never sets them) it runs at `damocles.explore.effort`, else `medium`, both clamped by pi. A template `thinking:` beats both levels. An unresolvable/unauthed/out-of-scope template `model:` fails the spawn naming the template, and a signed-out or out-of-scope picked Explore model fails it naming `damocles.explore.model` and the provider to sign in to. A model outside the plan fails it in one wording wherever it is refused (a template, Explore, a team role, a resumed agent, the chat's own send and `/btw`): "your {provider} plan does not include {model}", the provider by its display name and the model by its label (`refusalNames`, `notInPlanClause`). Never fall back to another model, which leaves a broken choice working-but-wrong forever.
- **Every background outcome reaches the parent once per branch** (`undelivered-results.ts`, `deliveredBackgroundResults` in `agent-records.ts`). Each background invocation's outcome reaches the model exactly once on a branch, as a delivered result or as an interruption-notice entry. The parent branch and the agent files are the source of truth; the `AgentManager` map is only a cache of the bound session's agents. D(branch), the delivered set, is the `toolCallId`s in every `damocles-subagent-results` entry's `details.agents[]` plus those in every `GetSubagentResult` fetch marker (`SubagentResultFetchDetails` in that result's `details`). Three paths deliver, and each records the invocation's `toolCallId`: the keep-alive injection at turn settle and the prompt-start delivery are pushes, and both deliver only `AgentManager.deliverableLive()` (plus, at prompt start, the file results), which drops what is already in D; a `GetSubagentResult` fetch is a pull, so it always answers and records the marker. D is the only record of delivery: nothing on a record is set when its result reaches the model, because the keep-alive's draft and a fetch marker are delivered only once pi commits them. pi commits each settle's draft before the next settle runs, so the keep-alive sees its own earlier injection in D. The prompt-start delivery reads D again with no await before its append, so two prompts racing past the early check append one message. It appends only to an idle session: while a run streams, pi defers a custom message sent with `triggerTurn: false` to that run's settle (`sendCustomMessage` pushes it onto `_pendingCustomMessages`, flushed in `_runBeforeSettleBoundary` after the handlers' drafts are committed, or before the first request when it was sent while the run was starting, `agent-session.js` in pi 1.4.0), so D would miss it when that settle's keep-alive reads D and the same records would be injected twice. A streaming session therefore gets no prompt-start delivery and no notice: the records stay with the run's keep-alive, and both flags stay set. The prompt-start delivery appends one hidden results message before the interruption notice and before `beginTurn`, so a rewind cannot split an invocation from its delivery. Both run only for a prompt that will commit a user message: a prompt the budget refuses and a pi extension command (`/todos`) get neither. The live records are delivered even when the file scan fails, and the notice runs only after a delivery that appended or had nothing to append, so a card-stopped agent is never both announced as resumable and delivered later. A live record is deliverable when it is background, terminal, not `discarded`, ran the agent's latest invocation on the branch and is not in D. A record whose own invocation entry failed to append still counts as the latest while its `Agent` call is on the branch, because any later invocation of that id would have replaced it in the manager. Only `abortAll`/`dispose` set `discarded`, so an agent stopped from its card (`abort()`) is delivered with its partial output and resume note. A resume switch first retires the bound session's agents the way a panel close does (`retireAgents`: `abortAll('shutdown')`, the team cancelled as a `shutdown` stop so it stays resumable, the turn aborted, the subagent and team runs awaited up to `ABORTED_AGENTS_SETTLE_TIMEOUT_MS`, then `clear()`), so the session switched to never sees them and a draining team never streams into its panel, and the first session's next bind finds each one in its file, resumable or deliverable by the scan. While it retires them, `stopRequested()` is true, so a turn held in `agent_before_settle` commits neither a keep-alive nor a plan-mode nudge to the session being retired; `clear()` writes each stopped record's status to its agent file at once, as `dispose` does, so a run that outlives the wait cannot record its status after the conversation was re-bound and scanned, and a run that ends after `clear()` posts nothing to the panel. The timeout caps only the agent runs: the turn's own `abort()` is awaited in full, as pi's `switchSession` awaits it again in `teardownCurrent`. Leaving a running turn is a Stop: `setResumeSession` ends it through `stopTurn`, the part of `beginAbort` that sets the turn idle and abandons the calls pi never started (`markAborted`), and `publishTurnStopped` (`sessionCancelled`, processing off) before it queues the switch. `beginAbort` publishes only after it stopped the agents, so a queued subagent the Stop completes posts its stop before the webview cancels what still runs. `retireAgents` aborts the stopped run (`endStoppedRun`) and the switch's record (`damocles-turn-stopped`, `persistTurnStopped`) lands after that abort and before `switchSession`, while the old session is still bound. `stopTurn` stops no agent, so `retireAgents` still stops them as `shutdown`. A switch that does not reach `retireAgents` (no target file, a target the panel let go of, a lookup that throws) ends the stopped run as a Stop does, its agents stopped as `user` (`abortStoppedTurn`, in the switch's `finally`). The run is aborted only once the target lookup (one stat or readdir) resolved, because only a switch that goes ahead stops its agents as `shutdown`; until then pi can still stream text into the stopped turn and start a call `markAborted` already showed stopped. Each queued switch carries its own cancel flag, set when the panel stops holding the target (`syncSessionLeases`: a reset, a delete's detach, another target, dispose) or loses its lease (`onSessionLeaseLost`), and checked after the lookup and again after `retireAgents`, so a switch never binds a session whose lease this panel no longer holds; the detach a lost lease starts forgets the loss in `lostLeases`, which is why the flag lives on the switch. Both flags are set before `switchSession`, because pi opens the target file before tearing the bound session down, so a switch that throws on an unreadable file leaves the old session bound with its agents already retired. An agent the manager does not hold is deliverable from its file when its latest invocation's `Agent` result was `async_launched`, that invocation is not in D, and its segment status is `completed`, `steered`, `aborted` or `error`. `stopped` is excluded because a file cannot tell a card stop from an abort kill (the notice announces user and shutdown stops, and budget and reset stops are discarded by design), and so is an invocation with no recorded status. The live check runs at every prompt start with no flag, which covers ESC, a budget stop and a team cancel without file IO. The file scan runs once after a stored session is bound (a resume at start, a fork, a resume switch) through `undeliveredScanPending`, which clears only after a scan that read every candidate (`collectUndeliveredFromFiles` reports `incomplete` when a candidate's file fails to read, or has no indexed file while `indexAgentFiles` reported a file it could not read), so an IO failure such as a Windows `EBUSY` is retried at the next prompt. Never scan files on every prompt: abort-killed agents never enter D, so their files would be re-read forever. The interruption notice skips an agent whose latest invocation is in D, because a delivered result already carries its status note and resume call, and an agent whose file could not be read or might be an unindexed one, because from the branch alone a finished agent reads as interrupted and the retried scan would then deliver it too. `GetSubagentResult` and `SteerSubagent` answer from a live record only when it ran this branch's latest invocation of that id (`AgentManager.branchRecord`), and otherwise through `AgentManager.readBranchAgent`, which resolves only a well-formed id that this branch invoked, never an arbitrary file in the folder. A finished agent's fetched result is its file's status entry, else what the branch holds (its results injection or its foreground `Agent` result), else its status note. Verbose `GetSubagentResult` formats the agent file's messages, because a finished record no longer holds its session.

- **A user's Stop ends only that subagent or team, and the parent's turn goes on.** A subagent, foreground or background, queued or running, stops through `stopSubagent { agentId }` → `PiSession.stopSubagent` → `AgentManager.abort(id, 'user')`. Its `Agent` result (foreground) or its keep-alive delivery (background) carries the partial output with the STOPPED BY THE USER note and the resume call, and no other agent and not the turn is aborted. The card learns the record id from `subagentStart`, which the manager posts when it creates the record, before the record can wait in the queue; a queued record that is stopped resolves its card at once (`stopQueued`). A team has exactly one stop of a named team, `TeamService.stopRunningTeam`, taken by the user's Stop team (`stopTeam`, from `cancelTeam { teamId }` behind a confirmation), the lead's own Stop (the same action; `TeamRunner.cancelAgent` refuses the lead) and the `cancel_team` tool. It records itself on a resume still validating (the resume claim's first stop), which then never launches, seals the checkpoint, makes the blocked `create_team` or `resume_team` return the partial results under the cancelled header, and requests the interruption notice. Every team stop names its cause (`TeamStop`): 'user' for Stop team and Esc, 'parent' for `cancel_team`, 'shutdown' for a panel close or session switch, 'reset' for a clear, which seals `stoppedBy: 'reset'`; no resume accepts that, so nothing stays resumable and no file has to be deleted, which Windows could refuse. Each run has one checkpoint (`checkpoint-writer.ts`), keyed at the run's start (`Date.now()`, or past the restored key for a resume, whose `resumeTeam` writes the restored state under the new key before `team-resumed` marks the old one used; a stop that lands before that write leaves the old checkpoint as the newest and writes nothing, so nothing recreates a folder a delete removed, and one that lands while the write is awaited seals the restored state under its cause, `sealUnlaunchedResume`, so the newest checkpoint keeps the cause, and keeps what ended the lead's last run, `interrupted` for a crash's live snapshot and `leadFailure` for a failure, since no run continued from that state) and rewritten live, with `live: true`, `asOf` and `messagesThrough` (the last bus message the event log holds, so an append that failed never names a message a resume cannot find), after every team log entry, every member `tool_execution_end`, `turn_end` and `agent_end`, every turn-end park and every wake the runner loop applies after pi's last event (`onStateBoundary`), and every steer. The live snapshot peeks each run's undelivered messages and never takes them; the stop's take stays the only consumer. The writer keeps one write in flight and one pending, writes `<key>.json.<pid>.tmp` then `renameWithRetry`, removes a stale temp before its first write (logging, never reporting, one it cannot remove), never fsyncs, creates the folder only at its first write (a later write after a delete fails ENOENT and is logged), and reports write failures into the persistence write errors, never into the team. A seal sets `stoppedBy`, drops `live` and ends live writes; the drain's usage patch re-seals on top, which also writes again a seal of any cause that failed to land, and finalize awaits the writer. A seal whose content cannot be built is reported and leaves the last live snapshot, and the team still stops. The result header and `cancel_team` call a stopped team resumable when any content of its checkpoint is on disk and the stop was not a reset: a seal that fails over a landed live snapshot leaves the team resumable, read as interrupted. A panel's dispose stops its team as a 'shutdown' and resolves once that seal is on disk (`TeamService.dispose`, awaited by `PiSession`'s dispose, which VS Code's `deactivate` and desktop's quit await), so a reload records the cause. A completed run's checkpoint stays as it is, and `resumableCheckpoint` ignores it because the log says `completed`. A live checkpoint whose team this process does not run (`isTeamRunningHere`) reads as `interrupted`, "stopped when Damocles exited while it ran", and resumes from the snapshot: a member the log spawned after it is restored from that spawn (running, work fields at zero, the snapshot's cumulative usage, nothing undelivered), bus messages logged after `messagesThrough` join their recipients' undelivered lists under the live delivery rules (for a member restored from a spawn, only those logged after that spawn), every entry whose ids the recipient's session already records is dropped, the lead is told the run was interrupted and to re-read team status first, and any other roster mismatch refuses, as every mismatch does for a sealed checkpoint. While this process runs the team (`teamInterruptionCause`), its live checkpoint is no interruption but undecided: the interruption check reads it as `running` and runs again at the next prompt, since a stop's seal may not have landed yet, and a sealed checkpoint reads as its cause even while the team still drains here. A fork copies a checkpoint only when its `asOf` (its key, for a file written before `asOf`, or one that is not JSON, which is copied as it is so the fork reads it as the source does) is at or before the fork point, because a live checkpoint rewritten later holds state the forked log lacks. A lead that fails before any stop is the run's stop as well (`stopOnLeadFailure`): it seals the checkpoint with `stoppedBy: 'failed'` and the failure's kind (`leadFailure`) before the abort, so the members keep their live statuses, and keeps the lead's undelivered messages, which its failed run read before its hooks went, a run whose session never opened included (`AgentResult.undelivered`); the team ends `failed` under the failed header, resumable, and the resumed lead is told its run failed (`buildFailedResumePrompt`). A lead that failed to start is not offered for a resume, since one would fail to start it the same way: its header names no `resume_team` and the interruption notice leaves it out, as a specialist's start failure is offered no re-run. The first stop of a run wins either way: a cancel before the failure makes the lead settle cancelled, because the runner ignores a failed call of an aborted run, and a cancel after it finds the run already synthesized. The checkpoint records the first as `stoppedBy`, and the result header and the interruption notice state it, so the model knows who or what stopped the team. An owner that aborts the turn for another cause stops the team with that cause before the abort, because the tool's abort listener stops it as 'user'. A stop that lands after the agent or team finished changes nothing and is answered with `subagentStopRejected` or `teamCancelRejected`, because the webview shows the control as stopping and nothing else would clear it; a stop that landed is answered by the run's own completion. A specialist's own Stop (`cancelTeamAgent`) has no rejection answer, so the webview shows it stopping only until that member's status changes. Esc still stops everything (`abortAll`, `cancelActiveTeam`).

- **`team_standby` and `team_report_complete` end the turn from the engine, not from the model.** Both used to set a flag and return, leaving the model free to carry on, and it did: 771 of 826 recorded standby calls were followed by more work. The stop is a `finishTurn` decider registered through `installTurnDecider`, which defers to the hook pi already installed, keyed on the tool *result* rather than the call because both tools throw for the wrong role and the agent needs that turn to read the error. It holds no opinion while `session.agent.peekQueuedMessages()` is non-empty. Never `session.pendingMessageCount`: that counts the `_steeringMessages`/`_followUpMessages` mirror, which `sendCustomMessage` bypasses by queueing on the agent directly (`agent-session.js:1858-1863` in pi 1.4.0), so a queued follow-up reads as zero there and the decider ends a turn pi was about to feed. Nothing is lost when it does, because `_runBeforeSettleBoundary` continues on `hasQueuedMessages()` anyway; the cost is an extra continuation request and a one-turn delay. Do not make the tool block instead: the team state machine runs only after `session.prompt` resolves and pi has no tool timeout, so a block hangs silently until abort. Do not abort either, which marks the agent failed.

- **A team agent whose last model call fails ends failed** (`agent-runner.ts`). The runner reads the error after each `prompt()` it awaits resolves, when pi has finished retrying, so a call pi retried never fails the agent, and a parked agent that is woken fails on that wake's call the same way, without the terminal-contract reconcile. It ends through the path a run that throws takes: status `failed`, the `Agent "X" failed: …` broadcast, `AgentResult.error`, and a `finalResponse` of the error and then the text the run wrote before it (`failureOutcomeText`, the shape a failed subagent reports), which `agent-completed.result` records. The lead learns it from the direct `system` message every specialist's end sends it, which names the error and `team_redispatch_specialist`, the one way to re-run a failed specialist. A failed lead, like a lead whose run throws, ends the team `failed` with the partial results and a resume checkpoint, unless a stop came first, so `resume_team` relaunches the lead as it does after a stop; a member whose aborted run settles after the failure, or that the drain then forces terminal, ends `cancelled` with its tabs kept open, as it does after a stop, and only a completed team turns a member's aborted run into `completed`. A failed call's text is not the agent's response and its tool calls never ran, so neither counts toward `finalResponse` or the tool count.

- **Scratchpad read state is per session and must be cleared when a session is replaced.** The unchanged marker withholds a section on the premise that its text is already in the reader's context. `redispatchSpecialist` gives an agent a brand new session under the same name, so `Scratchpad.clearReader(name)` runs alongside the other per-attempt resets. Without it the re-run's first read answers "unchanged since you last read it" for text it has never seen, and because the marker holds until a peer writes a new version, `mission-brief` (which has no other writer) is withheld for the rest of the run. Appending is not reading, so `appendTo` records nothing: `team_record_verification` echoes only the last 25 of up to 200 ledger entries.

- **A reviewer's sign-off covers one landed revision of each implementor it reviews** (`review-coverage.ts`, whose `ReviewCoverage` is the one owner of the pairs, stamps, sign-offs, dismissals and owed re-reviews). A `kind: 'reviewer'` spawn must declare `reviews`, the implementors it reviews (`[]` for a reviewer of nobody). An implementor may not declare any, a reviewed name may not be a reviewer, and a reviewer reviews no reviewer. A redispatch that omits `reviews` keeps the previous pairs, so every `agent-spawned` entry records the effective list, `[]` included: a resume reads the pairs only from each member's latest spawn. A reviewer whose log has no `reviews` (spawned before pairs existed) has none to keep, so its redispatch must pass `reviews`. A resume reads the pairs from an untrusted log, so it checks each spawn's `reviews` with the spawn rules against every member's kind, and each name in the checkpoint's coverage against the roster and each dismissal's `why` as one line, and refuses the resume on a failure, before any coverage rule reads them. A redispatch may not drop a pair whose review of landed work is unsatisfied: the refusal names `team_dismiss_review`, the recorded bypass, and is checked before anything changes. An implementor's work lands at a stamp `{ attempt, round }`, `round` being its revision-round count, on four paths: `reportComplete`, `convertStrandedStandby`, `convertOwedTerminalToReview`, and a run that settles `completed` unapproved, which only the revision cap produces. That last check reads the raw run status, because an approved or synthesis-released run settles `cancelled` and synthesis clears the round counts. A re-land in the same round keeps the stamp. A checkpoint without `review.coverage` lands every implementor that is `confirmedComplete` or `completed` at its `{ attempt, rounds }` on restore, so settled work always has a stamp. A reviewer with pairs reports only after reading the current version of every section its implementors authored (`checkReviewerReportGate`), and it passes one verdict per implementor, `approve` or `changes_requested`, recorded against each implementor's stamp at that moment (null if it had not landed). An implementor with no landed stamp has nothing to judge, so its verdict may be left out; its first landing then re-requests every live reviewer that reported without one, as it does a stale sign-off. A sign-off is current while its stamp equals the landed one, and a review is satisfied by a current `approve` or by a dismissal bound to the current stamp. Rule A: a landing that changes the stamp invalidates every older sign-off of it, and each reviewer whose run is still subscribed gets a direct `[RE-REVIEW REQUESTED]` message. A run holds its note sink exactly as long as its bus subscription, so that is the test: a run in the gap between its loop ending and its promise settling gets nothing and owes nothing, and a settling run's owed re-review is cleared. Its state changes first: it leaves `confirmedComplete` and turns `running` before the send, so no review-round notice counts it settled. Invalidation happens at landing, never at the revision request, so no reviewer is woken to review half-done work. An owed re-review may report past `MAX_SPECIALIST_REVIEW_ROUNDS`, because it is not a lead revision round. A reviewer's report in its last revision round is refused like an implementor's, but only after its read gate and verdicts pass and its verdicts are recorded, since a reviewer's work is its verdicts as an implementor's is its landing. So the `team_request_revision` that sends a stale reviewer back clears the block in any round, and the cap still ends the run. Only a report discharges it, and a cancel or redispatch of the reviewer clears it. At the cap a bare end earns one nudge and never a conversion to awaiting-review, since keepAlive holds a converted run only below the cap. Rule B: an implementor is approved only when every reviewer's review is satisfied, and a current `approve` satisfies it only while the reviewer has read every section the implementor authored at its current version (the report gate's staleness), because an implementor woken by a peer can change them without landing. That reads as stale, names `team_request_revision` on the reviewer, and wakes nobody. Rule C: a reviewer is approved only once every implementor it reviews is final (`isSpecialistFinal`: completed, cancelled or failed) and each completed one is satisfied. Rules B and C and the `[REVIEW ROUND READY]` coverage lines all come from one decision per pair (`ReviewCoverage.decisions`), so a line says "approval blocked" exactly when approval throws. Every rejection names the moves that clear it and never a tool that would throw: a completed reviewer cannot be redispatched, so its one move is a dismissal; a never-spawned implementor is spawned or cancelled; a reviewer still re-reviewing is waited for. `team_dismiss_review` needs a reason of at least 10 characters and is allowed only for a final reviewer that is not current or for a current `changes_requested`; a live reviewer with a stale sign-off must re-review instead. A dismissal records why the review was unsatisfied when the lead dismissed it, and `synthesizeResult` puts every dismissal at the top of the result, above a list of any review still unsatisfied on a path that bypassed the synthesis gate. The gate and that list cover every implementor with a landed stamp that is not cancelled or failed, standby included, because synthesis releases a standby member as completed. The block quotes the lead's reason as JSON, and a roster name must be one line with no control or format characters (Unicode Cc and Cf, checked before the event log is written), so no text it holds can add a line or reorder one. Implementor to implementor cross-review stays prose and advisory: only a reviewer's verdict gates. A log with no `reviews` declares no pairs, so no rule applies.

- **A team agent's work fields are per attempt; its usage fields are cumulative.** Status, last tool, active time (a stopwatch derived from entry timestamps, next entry), tool count, result, effort and task describe the attempt running now. A `resume_team` continues the attempt, so its tool count carries on; a redispatch starts a new attempt at zero. The tool count counts a call when pi starts it (`tool_execution_start`, which `AgentRunner` turns into `onToolCall` and `teamAgentToolCall`), never when its assistant message is sealed: a call the gate blocks is started and counts, while a call of a failed or aborted model call, or one an abort skipped in a cut batch, never starts and counts on no surface. Every count only grows, so no consumer handles a decrease. A subagent's count (`toolUses`) counts `tool_execution_end`, the same calls. Tokens and cost sum across every attempt, because a cancelled attempt's spend was still spent. Three places must agree or a reopened team contradicts the live one: the runner (`carriedUsage` plus the running attempt's usage, and `carriedToolCallCount`, restored from the checkpoint, plus the tool calls since the resume), the loader (each `agent-completed` entry holds its own run's usage, so it sums those, and takes the work fields from the last, including `toolCallCount`, which is the attempt's running total), and the webview store (which resets the work fields when the `attempt` counter advances). `attempt` is 0-based; a log written before it existed has no field and its spawns are the first attempt. Never write cumulative usage into `agent-completed`, or the loader double counts; never write a per-run tool count there, or a resumed member's count restarts. A member's result is the `result` its attempt's latest `agent-completed` recorded (`TeamAgent.result`): every path that writes that entry sends the same value on its `teamAgentStatusUpdate`, `getTeamState` carries it (a resume restores it from `TeamEventLog.lastResults` only for a member it leaves as it is, and clears it for every member it launches, as the loader's `agent-resumed` does), a redispatch clears it, and the loader reads it from the same entries, so the overlay shows a settled member's result live as a reload does, and a member that runs or parks has none. A result is an outcome: the `team_report_complete` summary, a completed run's final text, or a failure's `failureOutcomeText`. A stopped run (`cancelled`) with no report records none, since its trailing text narrates a step it never finished (`agent-runner.ts`); the overlay says it stopped before reporting, and the partial results list every launched member with its outcome, its filed report (a cancel builds them before its members settle) or "Stopped before reporting.". A member's card shows the `description` the lead gave `team_spawn_specialist` or `team_redispatch_specialist`, which `agent-spawned` records and the spawn's status update sends, and a log written before labels shows none. The task is team metadata, never derived from the transcript: every `agent-spawned` records the attempt's `task` (the lead's opening task too, whose `specialization` is a label), the spawn's status update and `getTeamState` carry it, a resume restores it from the spawn, and the loader reads it from the same entries through `spawnedTask` (`persistence.ts`), which reads a log written before `task` from a specialist's `specialization` and gives such a lead none. The member overlay shows it as the prompt and leaves out a first transcript message equal to it, which is that task as pi committed it, so a member stopped before pi committed its task shows the same task and no message for it, live and after a reload. A team card's per-run totals are a separate figure: `TeamRunLog` (`runs.ts`) takes them from the `run` field of `team-completed` or `team-cancelled`, and otherwise derives them from the `agent-completed` entries of that run. A run's usage has the same counters as an agent's (`TeamRunSummary.usage`: each token kind and the cost), so the card's tokens and cache rate use the definitions `AgentUsageStats` uses on the agent cards and overlays, and the runner, `TeamRunLog` and the store each derive all of them. A log written before those counters recorded `run.tokens`, input plus output only; it is read into `legacyTokens` and never into the token kinds, so such a card shows that figure and no cache rate.

- **A team agent's active time is a stopwatch derived from entry timestamps** (`src/shared/team-stopwatch.ts`: `activeMs`, the attempt's closed segments, and `runningSince`, the open segment's start or null). A segment opens at the member's `agent-spawned` entry, which starts a new attempt at zero, or at its `agent-resumed` entry, which continues the attempt, park mode included: time spent awaiting review or in standby counts, because the stopwatch runs from a launch or resume until the run settles. It closes at the member's `agent-completed` or the run's `team-completed`, and closing a closed segment does nothing, so a member that settles after `team-completed` keeps the figure that entry fixed. A runner that has written `team-completed` appends no further entry and sends no further webview message (`appendEntry` and its gated `onMessage`), since a resumed runner may own the log and the cards by the time a member it gave up on settles. A run cut short by a reload closes its open segments at the entry before the next `team-created` or `team-resumed`, or at the log's last entry, matching the run cut-short rule. The time a team spends stopped between runs therefore never counts. `TeamRunLog` (`runs.ts`, `memberActiveMs`) is the one derivation: the loader reads it and `parseTeamEventLog` hands it to the runner's restore as `TeamEventLog.activeMs`. It keys each member by agentId: `agent-spawned` starts a new attempt, `agent-resumed` opens a segment only for that attempt and only while none is open, and `agent-completed` closes one only when its `toolUseId` (the runner stamps its run's on every one) names the open run; an entry without the field, from a log written before it, closes as before. The live runner stamps one `Date` into each opening or closing entry and into the stopwatch, so a live card and a reloaded card agree to the millisecond; it sends the stopwatch on the spawn update and on every settle update, a run that throws included, and in `getTeamState`, and `getTeamStatus` reports it. The team's finalize also sends one, before `teamCompleted`, for every member it forced terminal after the drain timeout or whose open segment it closed at `team-completed`, because a member that settles after that entry sends nothing. It builds those updates right after the `team-completed` append, before its await, where a late settle could still change a member. A member forced terminal there has no `agent-completed`, so the loader applies `team-completed.agentResults[].status` to every member still active. A run that throws before its settle handler wrote `agent-completed` writes one from the throw path, `failed`, with the same `Date` as its stopwatch; a throw after that entry keeps the status it recorded (a per-launch flag), so a run never has two. The webview store applies only that stopwatch, after the attempt reset, and never stamps an agent time from its own clock; a member's card and overlay tick while `runningSince` is set, never by status, since an approved member's run counts until it settles. The team overlay sums its runs (`runsStopwatch`: each ended run's duration plus the live run's open span), so the gap between runs is excluded there too. `agent-completed` carries no `durationMs`.

- **Every effort badge shows the effective level pi ran at, never the requested setting** (`src/shared/effort-badge.ts`). The value is the session's `thinkingLevel`, which pi returns after clamping the requested level to the model (`_clampThinkingLevel`), so a subagent requested at Max on a model whose top level is High shows High, and Damocles' `ultracode`, which reaches pi as `max`, shows Max. It is published only when `effortBadgeApplies` (`src/core/pi-session/pi-models.ts`) holds: the model reasons (pi's `Model.reasoning`), its catalog entry does not set `supportsEffort: false`, and pi's `compat.supportsReasoningEffort` is not false (GitHub Copilot's Gemini and Kimi K3 models reason, but pi sends them no level). With no model there is no badge, and a model the rule excludes shows no badge rather than "Off". A chat reply carries the level pi stamped on its `AssistantMessage.thinkingLevel`, on the existing `assistant` and `assistantReplay` shapes: live the rule reads the session's model, on reload the registry entry for the message's provider and model id. Which reply shows a badge is derived at render time (`useVirtualizedMessages.ts`: the first reply after each user message, and any later reply in the turn whose level differs from the last one shown), so nothing about placement is stored. A subagent's effort is stored per run: `onSessionCreated` reads it from the new session and writes it on the launch record of a fresh run or the segment record of a resume, because a resume can reopen at a different level; the reload gives a spawn card the launch's value and a resume card only its own segment's, so a resume that recorded none shows none, as its live card did. An `effort` this build does not know (a newer build's level, in a shared `~/.damocles`) is dropped on read, never a reason to reject the launch or segment entry. The bridge sends it in `subagentModelUpdate` once the session exists, with no model when the launch recorded no label, and its once-guard keys on the (model, effort) pair, so the spawn-time model message does not swallow it. A replay applies `effortBadgeApplies` to the model it finds in `PiRuntime.modelRuntimeReady()` (a model the registry lacks shows no badge), which waits only for init to build the model registry, not for its credential sync. The replay reaches the runtime through `PiRuntime.unlessRetired()`: a panel restored at startup replays before its session creates the runtime, so the replay may create it, but once `disposeInstance` retired it at shutdown nothing creates one, because a late replay's runtime would start watchers nothing disposes. A team agent's `effort` is a per-attempt work field: `agent-spawned` is written before the session exists, so the session helper appends an `agent-session-started { agentId, attempt, effort }` entry once it does, and the live `teamAgentStatusUpdate` beside `logFilePath` carries the same value. A redispatch starts the attempt with no effort; a resume, which continues the attempt, takes the effort of its new session; an entry whose attempt is no longer the member's current one is ignored. The runner, `persistence.ts` (through `TeamRunLog.memberEffort`, which the resume reader's `TeamEventLog.efforts` also uses) and the webview store (which resets it when `attempt` advances) apply that one rule. Records and logs written before this existed carry no effort, and show no badge.

- **A live agent's usage is a `getSessionStats()` delta, never a sum of `message_end` usage** (`session-usage.ts` `runUsageMeter`, used by the subagent stream bridge and the team `AgentRunner`). The baseline is taken when the run attaches to its session, so a reopened session's earlier runs stay on earlier cards. pi's total counts every billed entry, compaction summaries and cache warms included, which is what keeps the tokens consistent with the cost; a per-message sum misses both. pi notifies `message_end` listeners before it persists the message, so a reading taken inside the listener lags one response: the reading is deferred a microtask. A subagent's budget roll (`AgentManager.rollCost`) takes the same reading the bridge sends the card, through `onUsage`, so each response is read once and the budget never runs a response behind; the run's end reads once more (`settleUsage`) for spend that raised no event. The reload path rebuilds the same figure per segment with `usageOfEntry` (`agent-records.ts` `parseAgentEntries`). A resume segment records `dollarBilled` from the model its session reopened on, and the loader prefers it over the launch entry's flag, so a resumed card is labelled the same live and after a reload.

- **The twenty-one team tools have one naming table, `src/shared/team-tool-labels.ts`.** The Tools panel catalog, the card in the transcript and any future reader all take their label from it: `team-tools.ts` keeps the descriptions and the schemas but no labels of its own, mapping each spec through `toSpec`, which throws at module load for a name with no entry. That is deliberate and must stay: a `?? name` fallback would render a card reading `team_write_scratchpad` and nobody would notice for a release. The table is imported by both the webview bundle and the extension host, so it imports nothing at all. The raw snake_case name is still what the expanded overlay shows, because session logs and greps are keyed on the name the agent actually called, so a label must never replace the name in a persisted or searchable surface. Adding a team tool means adding its label in the same change.

## Webview overlays

- **An overlay's z-index is derived from the shared overlay stack, never hardcoded and never left to DOM sibling order** (`composables/useOverlayEscape.ts`, consumed by `OverlayShell.vue`). Every overlay used to render at `z-50`, so equal-z siblings painted in the order they appear in `App.vue`: a tool overlay opened from inside a team agent overlay opened behind it and looked like a dead click, while the same card worked from a subagent overlay purely because that mount is written earlier in the file. The stack `useOverlayEscape` already keeps is the same ordering paint order needs, and the composable now returns `zIndex` = base + stack depth. Reordering mounts in `App.vue` fixes one pair and leaves the next nested overlay to rediscover the bug, so it is not the fix. Mount order is what the stack records and a nested overlay mounts strictly after the one it opens over; Vue guarantees nothing about sibling order, which is exactly why the stack rather than the DOM is the source. An overlay that reintroduces a fixed `z-` class cannot be opened over. Every close path of an overlay (X, scrim, Escape) closes only that overlay, never the ones beneath it, as the WAI-ARIA dialog pattern requires; a modal host prompt stays until it is answered. An overlay holding unsent text (plan feedback, a side-question follow-up, a new memory or profile edit, a steer, an edited MCP server form or one whose write is in flight) passes `OverlayShell` `hasDraft`, and a scrim click then leaves it open, because a stray click beside the panel must not throw the text away; X and Escape still close it. Closing the voice model download hides it and never cancels the download, which only its Cancel download button does. The prompt navigator's toggle (`useOverlayPriority.ts`) opens only while the stack holds no overlay but the navigator itself and no foreground surface outside the stack is up (a dock prompt, desktop's settings modal), so a new overlay needs no entry there. Every modal dialog, `ui/alert-dialog` and `ui/dialog` content, is a layer of the stack (`useDialogLayer`) that holds its place only while open (`OverlayOptions.active`), so it opens above the overlay or proposal diff it was opened from, an overlay opened over it paints above it and takes Escape, and its exit keeps the layer it had while open. reka traps focus and blocks outside pointer events on modal dialog content, so a plain overlay opened above an open dialog does not take the pointer or focus; no flow opens one. A question main stacks over the desktop overlay's Connect dialog (the trust question of a picked folder, any `message` or worktree dialog) is no plain overlay: `OverlayDialog` is a reka alert dialog layer (always open, rendered in place, marked `data-overlay-layer`), which pauses the trap beneath and takes its focus a tick after mount. When the control that opened an overlay is gone, focus goes into the overlay beneath it (`topOverlayRoot`, `restoreFocus`), else to `[data-overlay-return-focus]`.

- **The overlay stack's Escape listener stays on `document` in the bubble phase, so a control inside an overlay can keep Escape for itself** (`composables/useOverlayEscape.ts`, consumed by `OverlayShell.vue`; the control is `components/ToolCancelControl.vue`). The cancel note textarea closes its note on Escape and stops the event, which only works because the stack's handler runs later, on an ancestor node. Bind it in the capture phase, or on the overlay root, and it fires first and throws away a half-typed note by closing the overlay around it. `overlay-stacking.test.ts` goes red if either moves.
- **While a reka popup or dialog is open, the overlay stack leaves Escape to it** (`composables/useOverlayEscape.ts`). reka's dismissable layers (Select, Popover, DropdownMenu, the range calendar's Popover, Dialog) hear Escape on `window`, which a keydown reaches after `document`, so the stack's handler runs first. It therefore returns without stopping the event whenever an open element carries `data-dismissable-layer`; a stopped event never reaches the popup, and Escape closes the whole overlay around a half-picked date range. A stack overlay that is itself built on a reka dismissable layer (the image lightbox's `DialogContent`) marks that element `data-overlay-layer`, or the stack would yield Escape to the overlay forever. A dialog that is a layer of the stack beneath the top overlay is not such a layer: the top overlay keeps Escape when every open dismissable layer is the root of an entry beneath it.
- **A host prompt is a modal layer of the stack** (`ExtensionUiDialog.vue`, `useOverlayEscape(onClose, { modal: true })`). It is a reka `Dialog` marked `data-overlay-layer`, mounted only while a prompt is queued, and paints on `MODAL_Z_INDEX`. A plain element under an open reka modal gets no pointer events and loses focus to the modal's focus trap; a nested reka layer, such as a reka Select popper, takes both. An overlay opened while a modal is up is inserted below it in the stack, so Escape and the Tab trap stay with the prompt the user sees. While the stack yields Escape to an open reka layer beneath the prompt, reka dismisses only its highest layer, the prompt, so nothing may close the settings modal on a window-level Escape of its own.
- **The settings modal is a stack overlay in both documents** (`components/settings/SettingsModal.vue`, `useOverlayDialog`). In VS Code it registers in the chat page's overlay stack; on desktop the overlay page mounts the same component (`src/desktop/shell/overlay/settings/OverlaySettings.vue`), where it registers in that page's own stack. Its selects bind `usePopperZIndex`, a host prompt (`ExtensionUiDialog`) stays the modal layer above it, Escape clears a non-empty search before it closes, and `OverlayApp`'s own Escape leaves a settings request to the modal. Host-only sections reach the modal through its `host` prop (`HostSettings`), never a host sniff.
- **A selection inside a group moves as one transform-only indicator** (`composables/useSlidingIndicator.ts`, `components/SlidingIndicator.vue`). The composable measures the selected item against its positioned parent and re-measures on resize; the component draws the box from fixed rounded caps and a middle scaled on one axis (X for pills and tabs, Y for the `ring` and `soft` variants in vertical lists), so options of different sizes animate without animating width or height and without stretching the corners. Chromium snaps the unscaled middle to whole device pixels and the scale multiplies that error, so the middle is about 100px long and always a whole number of device pixels at the current `devicePixelRatio` (a fixed 100px is 187.5 device px at 150% with a 1.25 zoom, and its bar paints most of a device pixel past its end). It places itself without animating whenever its group mounts, so an opening popup does not fly the indicator in.
