A group of equal columns (`SegmentedToggle`'s `equal` variant) uses its `columns` mode: one piece a column wide, sized by the caller's class and moved whole columns by transform, never measured, so its first transform is its place; it shows nothing while no option is selected. A second indicator implementation, or one that transitions `width`, breaks the motion rules.
- **A dock prompt's options take focus as they mount, unless focus is in an editable field, another dock prompt or an open overlay** (`useDockPromptOptionsFocus` in `composables/useDockPrompt.ts`, through `components/DockPromptOptions.vue`, the options list of the permission, question and skill cards). reka's `ListboxRoot` focuses its highlighted item one tick after it mounts, whatever holds focus; the composable holds that focus back with the root's `focusable` flag until the item is highlighted, so a card that appears while the user types in the composer never takes the keystrokes. This is the only focus-on-appear path: a second `focus()` call beside it would bypass the rule. The question card keys its list by question, so each question's options mount and follow the same rule.
- **The MCP resource picker is a quick pick whose filter never gives up focus** (`components/McpResourcePicker.vue` on `ui/command`, as VS Code's `quickInput.ts`). The filter names the highlighted row through `aria-activedescendant`; the arrow keys move and loop at either end, Ctrl+Home and Ctrl+End move to the first and last row (VS Code's `quickInput.first` and `quickInput.last`), PageDown and PageUp move a page and stop at either end (`quickInput.pageNext` and `quickInput.pagePrevious`), and Enter runs the row's one action (Open, Attach, Fill in, Load more or Retry, Back), with reka's own selection prevented. Plain Home and End, with or without Shift, move the filter's caret, as an editable combobox's do (WAI-ARIA APG); `CommandInput` owns that for every `ui/command` filter (`isListEdgeChord`), because reka's `ListboxFilter` takes Home and End for the list whatever the modifiers. It owns the page keys too (`isListPageKey`, `ui/command/page.ts`), which reka's filter ignores: as VS Code's `List.focusNextPage` and `focusPreviousPage`, a page key highlights the last (first) row the list's scroll area shows whole, measured from the rendered rows and narrowed by a row's scroll margin, and from that row scrolls a page and highlights that row of the next page; PageUp onto a group's first row also shows its heading. The first row stays highlighted while rows arrive until the user moves, and a page key is a move; Back, leaving the template form and Load more highlight the row they return to or the first new one, and focus goes back to the filter, so it never falls to the page. A row that cannot be attached is `aria-disabled` with its reason in its text and does nothing on Enter; reka's `disabled` would make the arrow keys skip it. reka's `ListboxItem` re-renders an option's attributes only when its highlight changes, so state that changes under an option goes in its content. The list keeps the reka root mounted under the template form, because a root mounting again highlights its first item a tick later and overrides the row the form returns to. Server URIs and URI templates render through `visibleText`; the raw value stays in `data-uri`, the requests and the attached chip's data.
- **A body control marked `data-overlay-initial-focus` takes focus on open, ahead of the frame's close button** (`useOverlayDialog.ts`; `OverlayShell`'s X is `data-overlay-fallback-focus`). A search box an overlay opens on carries the marker; marking the X instead makes every body marker lose to it.
- **Every full chat overlay is an `OverlayShell`; confirmations are the restyled `ui/alert-dialog`** (`components/OverlayShell.vue`, `components/ui/alert-dialog/`). The shell gives each overlay its stack registration, focus trap and return, and the reference header (icon tile, title, subtitle, chip, `OverlayHeaderAction` buttons); an overlay that builds its own frame or opens on `ui/dialog` loses Escape ordering. App.vue mounts each overlay with `v-if` inside a `t-overlay` `<Transition>`, so its exit plays. A yes/no question (delete a session, rewind, rewind before a compaction, overwrite a settings file, remove all voice files) stays an alert dialog: it is modal by role and has no header actions. The image lightbox and the host prompt stay on their reka dialogs, which mark `data-overlay-layer` and are already handled by the stack.
- **Binding a plan file takes an id core issued, never a path** (`requestPlanFileCandidates` and `bindPlanToSession` in `core/chat-panel/message-router/handlers/workspace-handlers.ts`, `components/BindPlanOverlay.vue`). Core lists the folder's plan files from `listWorkspaceFiles` (the `@` autocomplete's excludes apply): markdown under a `plans` folder, or whose name has "plan" as a word. It issues a random id per file and keeps the ids per panel with the session they were listed for, replacing them with every list and dropping them when the panel closes; a folder switch replaces the session, so it retires them. Core answers every request, or the overlay never leaves loading: a listing that throws answers `listFailed`, and one that outlives its session or panel answers an empty list and issues nothing. The webview store closes on a session or folder change. `bindPlanToSession { candidateId }` binds only a file behind an id from that panel's last list for its current session; any other id binds nothing and is logged. Without an id core opens the OS file dialog, whose answer never passes through the webview, so no webview message names a file for core to read. Choosing a file in the overlay is the overwrite confirmation: the session's existing plan file is overwritten in place.
- **Popper content inside an overlay paints one above it** (`usePopperZIndex` in `composables/useOverlayEscape.ts`, bound by `ui/popover/PopoverContent.vue`, `ui/select/SelectContent.vue`, `SettingSelect.vue` and the two `ui/dropdown-menu` contents; a dialog is not popper content but a layer of the stack, above). Popper content is portalled to `body`, so it stacks against the overlay rather than inside it; a fixed `z-50` ties with an overlay at the base of the stack and sinks behind one opened at depth 1 or deeper. Every stack overlay provides its z-index, and the popper binds that plus one; outside any overlay it keeps `z-50`.

## Streaming views

- **Every view that streams output follows it through `useStickToBottom`, and nothing else writes its scroll position to the bottom** (`composables/useStickToBottom.ts`). The views are the transcript (`App.vue`), an `OverlayShell` given a `followKey` (subagent, team agent and side-question overlays), the thinking block (`ThinkingIndicator.vue`) and live shell output (`LiveOutputPane.vue`). Two desktop views keep the same rule through their own scroll APIs: a D36 log tab in Monaco (`CodeEditor.vue`) and the terminal in xterm (`TerminalView.vue`), which follows output only while `viewportY >= baseY` and whose Scroll to the end button calls `scrollToBottom()`. Any upward scroll stops following, and reaching the end again resumes it.
- **The view's position is the only evidence that the reader left the bottom.** Each read compares `scrollTop` with the last recorded value and the max scroll (`scrollHeight - clientHeight`) with the last recorded max. Following stops when the view moved up and either it is no longer within 2px of the bottom, or a user gesture holds it and the max scroll did not decrease. Outside a gesture, then, only a move out of the bottom band stops it: a script such as the prompt navigator's jump does, a clamp or rounding inside the band does not. During a gesture any upward move stops it, even 1px, so a short trackpad scroll is not undone by the next chunk. The position covers every way of scrolling (keys with modifiers, a scrollbar press, a wheel the browser latches to a nested scroller that has reached its end), which classifying the input cannot. A gesture a nested scroller takes (the thinking block inside the transcript, live output with `overscroll-behavior: contain`) leaves the view unmoved, so it keeps following.
- **A user gesture holds pinning; it does not decide.** Three inputs start a hold: `wheel` on the view (not a Ctrl zoom); `keydown` on the view's document, because keyboard scrolling can move the view while focus is outside it, for ArrowUp/Down, PageUp/Down, Home/End and Space, with or without Ctrl, Meta or Shift, but not with Alt, not once handled (`defaultPrevented`), not in text entry, and not Space on an activatable control; and `pointerdown` on the view, held until `pointerup` or `pointercancel` on the window, which covers mouse, pen and touch, scrollbar thumb and track presses, a text-selection drag and middle-click autoscroll. While held, a read still records the position and decides, but never writes `scrollTop`, because a pin written during a smooth scroll cancels its animation. The hold ends once the view has gone 150ms without scrolling: each wheel or key event and each `scroll` event while held restarts that window, and a pointer hold starts it on release, so momentum and track-click animations keep it alive. When it ends, a still-following view pins to the new bottom.
- **Following resumes when the reader is at the bottom: after a downward move, after the max scroll decreases (content shrinks or the view grows), on a downward gesture at the bottom where the view cannot move (a downward wheel; ArrowDown, PageDown, End or Space, also with Ctrl or Cmd), and through the jump-to-latest button.** `scrollToBottom` ends any hold and pins. The button calls `jumpToLatest`, which first focuses the view (`preventScroll`): the button unmounts once the view follows, and focus would otherwise fall to the body, outside a modal overlay. The transcript and a following `OverlayShell` body carry `tabindex="-1"`.
- **The transcript follows again on the user's own submit and when another session replaces it, and on nothing the host sends on its own.** `App.vue` calls `followTranscript()` in `handleSendMessage` and `handleQueueMessage` where the message is posted; the commands handled in the webview (`/rewind`, `/clear`, `/context`, `/usage`, `/stats`, `/btw`) return before it. `sessionCleared` returns `forceScrollToBottom`, because another session's transcript starts at its bottom. A host echo (`userMessage`, injected and command echoes included) and a background task's result do not move a reader who scrolled up. The status strip's Compact now posts `/compact` without following.
- **Growth is read from DOM mutations, and the pin is written in the same microtask.** The MutationObserver watches the view's subtree for child lists, text, and the `style`, `class`, `open` and `hidden` attributes. The transcript's canvas height changes inside its rows' ResizeObserver callbacks (`useScrollEngine.onItemResized`). A ResizeObserver on an ancestor is deferred to the next frame there, and the browser reports a ResizeObserver loop error, so a content observer would paint every streamed line one frame below the view. A ResizeObserver on the view itself covers the viewport changing size, and captured `load`, `animationend` and `transitionend` cover layout that changes with no mutation.
- **The transcript shifts the view for a row's resize only when the change can lie above what the reader sees** (`useScrollEngine.onItemResized`). A row wholly above the view shifts it by every change. A row the view starts inside shifts it only when it was measured before and was not streamed into. A streamed-into row is a row of the message still streaming, or one whose type or text changed since its last measurement: it grows at its end, below the reader, and its last growth can be measured after the stream ends (the last chunk, or the typewriter's unrevealed tail that renders at once when `streaming-text` becomes `text-block`). A first measurement only corrects the estimate of a row whose content is already on screen at its real height. Any other change of a row the view starts inside (an image loading, highlighting arriving) shifts the view by the change, so what the reader sees stays in place.
- **A reply's text keeps one row id from its first streamed character to its committed block** (`useVirtualizedMessages`). The trailing streaming text is `text-<message id>-<index of the block it is committed as>`, and a message with no blocks yet uses block 0. Measured heights are keyed by row id, so a new id at the end of the stream would make the finished reply a first measurement, whose estimate can be thousands of pixels off and, when it puts the row wholly above the view, shifts the reader by the error.
- **The compensation is written once per flush, after the canvas height commits and before the view's MutationObserver reads the position** (a `flush: 'post'` watcher on the frame). Written before the commit, a shift is clamped at the old bottom for a reader within the growth of it. It is an absolute target, the view's position at the flush's first compensated resize plus the sum of every compensated resize in that flush, because a shorter canvas has already clamped the view by the time the watcher runs. Running before the MutationObserver puts a following view's pin after the shift, so the pin lands on the new bottom.
- **A view starts at the bottom, except a finished thinking block.** An overlay starts again at the bottom when its `followKey` changes (another agent selected). The thinking block follows from mount only while it streams, so expanding a finished one starts at its top.

## Design tokens

- **One token layer colors every renderer, and only `tokens.css` reads a host theme variable** (`src/webview/styles/tokens.css`, `src/webview/__tests__/design-token-guard.test.ts`). The shared webview, the desktop shell page and the overlay read only `--d-*` tokens: surfaces (`bg`, `panel`, `card`, `hover`, `code`, `input`), `border` and `border2`, text (`text`, `muted`, `faint`), `accent`, `on-accent` and `accent-soft`, status (`success`, `warning`, `danger`, `info`), `on-danger`, `on-warning`, `on-success` and `on-info`, text on a tint (`accent-text`, `success-text`, `warning-text`, `danger-text`, `info-text`, `faint-text`), `add` and `del`, `shadow`, `scrim`, `opacity-disabled` (a disabled control's opacity), `font`, `font-size`, `mono` and `mono-size`. Beyond the reference's map, `font-size` carries VS Code's UI font size and `mono-size` its editor font size (desktop sets both to 13px), `on-danger`, `on-warning`, `on-success` and `on-info` are the text on a solid fill of their color, and the text-on-tint tokens keep tinted labels at AA (D48, below). The shadcn tokens in `style.css` derive from them, and so does every color the map lacks: the focus ring and primary fill are `accent` on `on-accent`, `accent` and `secondary` are `hover`, `destructive` is `danger` on `on-danger`, a link hover is `text`, blockquotes and table rows take `border2`, `code` and `hover`, and the chart hues are `accent`, the status colors and `color-mix` blends of them. The body text is `font-size`, and the root font size is `font-size` × 16/13, so 1rem is the reference's 16px at the 13px base and the whole rem scale follows the host's font size (`style.css`). The guard fails when any source under `src/webview` or `src/desktop` other than `tokens.css` names a `--vscode-*` variable. The two inline pages, the restore error page (`src/shared/panel-html.ts`) and the browser page (`src/shared/browser-page-html.ts`), are host-neutral HTML outside the guard: VS Code pages load no `tokens.css`, so they carry the host theme style and read `--d-*` first with a `--vscode-*` fallback. The browser page's toolbar renders only in VS Code and keeps its VS Code variables.
- **Renderer sizes are rem, so they follow the host font** (`style.css`, `eslint.config.mjs`, `stylelint.config.mjs`). Text, line heights, spacing, radii and box sizes under `src/webview` and `src/desktop/shell` are rem (px ÷ 16 at the 13px default font, where 1rem is 16px), so nothing moves at the default and a larger VS Code font scales the whole panel. px stays only for border, ring and outline widths, blur and shadows. Container queries are rem too (`@max-[43rem]:`): Chromium resolves rem in a container condition against the root font size, but in a media query against the initial 16px, so there are no viewport media queries. A rule that depends on the panel width queries the `app` container, which `body` establishes so portalled content can query it too (`@min-[45rem]/app:`). The number in a `--text-*` or `--radius-*` name (`text-12.5`, `rounded-10`) is its px at the default font, and `cn()` (`lib/utils.ts`) lists both ramps for tailwind-merge. Tailwind emits no rule for a `text-<n>` or `rounded-<n>` the ramp lacks, so `lib/__tests__/utils.test.ts` fails on any such class in either tree. The composer and the cancel note grow with `field-sizing: content` up to a rem cap, never a height written from script. A size that script compares or draws follows the root font size too: the header folds at 45rem and 35rem of measured width (`ChatHeader.vue`), `SlidingIndicator`'s `radius` is px at the default font, scaled by the root, and every other px number an API takes (popper `side-offset`, the transcript's row gaps, the settings layout's 45rem fold, the stats chart height) comes from `remPx(rem)` (`composables/useRemPx.ts`). It is reactive: no event reports a computed-style change, so a ResizeObserver on a 1rem probe tells it the root size changed, and an open popper re-positions. Icons are sized by a `size-*` class (px ÷ 4), never a numeric `size`, which lucide writes as px `width` and `height` attributes; `LoadingSpinner` and the `icons/` set take the class too. The composer's action row wraps once its labels have folded, so it never overflows down to the narrowest panel, VS Code's 170px side bar (desktop's chat column is at least `COLUMN_MIN_PX`, 300px at the default font, less the 1rem grip gutter). Lint enforces the rule: `better-tailwindcss/no-restricted-classes` rejects a px arbitrary value in a class or variant, `enforce-canonical-classes` rewrites one with an exact scale step (Tailwind folds a `0.25rem` radius into `rounded-lg`, which is 6px here, so that rewrite is ignored), and stylelint rejects px on the sizing properties in `.css` files and `<style>` blocks. `no-restricted-classes` also rejects a viewport variant (`sm:` to `2xl:`, `min-[…]:`, `max-[…]:`, `[@media…]:`) and stylelint a width or height media feature. `vue/no-restricted-v-bind`, `vue/no-restricted-static-attribute` and `no-restricted-syntax` reject a `size` on any element but the components whose `size` is a variant name (`Button`, `Toggle`, `ToggleGroup`, `ToggleGroupItem`, `ToggleSwitch`, `SegmentedToggle`), a `size` in an `h()` call, and a numeric popper offset. Lint covers both trees, each resolving classes against its own entry point (`src/webview/style.css`, `src/desktop/shell/style.css`), and `scripts/__tests__/rem-lint-scope.test.ts` proves a px size fails in each. Desktop fixes `--d-font-size` at 13px (`theme.ts`), so 1rem is 16px there. Only geometry main applies to native views stays px: `setBounds` rectangles, window minimums and the traffic-light position. A size main stores from a pointer drag (the sidebar width, a section size) is px too, and its minimum comes from `remPx`.
- **VS Code maps the tokens to its active theme** (`tokens.css`, per the reference's `Damocles VS Code.dc.html`): `bg` editor background, `panel` side bar, `card` editor widget, `hover` list hover, `border` panel border, `border2` widget border, `text` foreground, `muted` description, `faint` line numbers, `accent` link, `on-accent` editor background, the status colors from the terminal green, warning, error and info foregrounds, `code` code block, `input` input, `add` and `del` the diff editor's inserted and removed backgrounds, and `font`, `mono` and `mono-size` the UI font and the editor font and size. A variable a theme may leave unset (list hover and widget border in high contrast themes, the diff backgrounds) has a fallback in `tokens.css`. `accent-soft`, `shadow` and `scrim` derive through `color-mix`, and each `*-text` is its color mixed 70% with `text`. `on-danger` is black or white, whichever contrasts more with `danger`: relative color syntax computes WCAG luminance in `srgb-linear` and switches at 0.179, where both reach 4.58:1, so a destructive button reaches AA in every theme (VS Code Light Modern's `errorForeground` takes black at 6.26:1, where the editor background gave 3.35:1). `on-warning` is picked the same way from `warning`, for text on a solid warning fill (Light Modern's `editorWarning.foreground` takes black at 6.74:1, where the editor background gave 3.12:1). `on-success` and `on-info` are picked the same way from `success` and `info`, for a host's title bar stripe label on its safety color. A VS Code theme switch recolors the panel live, because every token resolves through `var()`.
- **Desktop fills the tokens with the Damocles palettes** (`src/desktop/main/theme.ts`). The host theme `<style>` loads after the stylesheet in the chat views, the shell page and the overlay, so it overrides `tokens.css`; desktop emits no `--vscode-*` variable. The values are the reference's `Damocles Desktop.dc.html` palettes; a color with alpha is written `#rrggbbaa`, because Monaco parses only hex. `THEME_BACKGROUND`, which the window and every view paint before their page does, is each palette's `--d-bg`. `--s-*` (`kw`, `str`, `num`, `com`, `fn`, `type`) and `--d-ansi-0` to `--d-ansi-15` are desktop-only: only Monaco (`components/editor/useMonaco.ts`), the desktop Shiki theme (`composables/damoclesShikiTheme.ts`) and the desktop pages read them, and `tokens.css` never defines them. `src/desktop/main/__tests__/theme-parity.test.ts` checks that every `--d-*` a renderer reads is defined in both palettes and in `tokens.css`, that both palettes define the same tokens with all six syntax and sixteen ANSI colors, and that no other module reads a desktop-only token.
- **Every desktop text color reaches WCAG 2.2 AA (4.5:1)** (`src/desktop/main/__tests__/palette-contrast.test.ts`, D21): the text, status and syntax tokens on `bg`, `panel`, `card` and `code`, `faint` on `hover` (any row may be hovered), `on-accent` on `accent`, `on-danger` on `danger`, `on-warning` on `warning`, `on-success` on `success`, `on-info` on `info` (desktop: `#03121c` dark, 9.51 and 9.08; `#ffffff` light, 5.11 and 5.06), the text-on-tint tokens below, and every ANSI color on `bg`, `panel` and `card` except the slot of the background's own polarity (black in dark, bright white in light), which is a fill. Reference values that failed were moved in OKLCH lightness only, keeping hue and chroma, to the first value that passes (worst surface ratio before and after): dark `--d-faint` `#6b7785` to `#7f8b99` (3.83 to 4.57, worst on `hover`) and `--s-com` `#5f6b78` to `#768390` (3.22 to 4.51); light `--d-faint` `#7d8a97` to `#5d6a76` (3.14 to 4.57, worst on `hover`), `--d-accent` `#0a7fd0` to `#0272bd` (3.76 to 4.50), `--d-success` `#138a5a` to `#037e51` (3.88 to 4.55), `--d-warning` `#a86a00` to `#9b6200` (3.95 to 4.51), `--d-danger` `#d43a40` to `#cd333a` (4.17 to 4.52), `--d-info` `#4b6bd6` to `#4867d2` (4.27 to 4.50), `--s-com` `#8a96a3` to `#646f7c` (2.68 to 4.55) and `--s-type` `#a16207` to `#9f6002` (4.38 to 4.50). The tints (`accent-soft`, `add`, `del`) keep the reference values. The reference has no ANSI palette; the sixteen colors per palette are drawn from its status and syntax hues.
- **Text on a tint takes its color's text shade, and the tint stays the reference's** (D48, `palette-contrast.test.ts`). A label in a status or accent color on a tint of that color (chips, badges, notes, a selected option, an agent's initial, diff pills and signs), and accent or status text on `hover` (inline code, path chips, a hovered text button, a desktop notification center row's status lead, `rowLead` in `overlay/notification-view.ts`), take `--d-<tone>-text`; faint text on a selected (`accent-soft`), hovered or diff (`add`, `del`) row takes `--d-faint-text`. Each is its color mixed toward `text` (`color-mix(in srgb)`) just far enough to reach 4.5:1 on a 22% tint of its color, the strongest tint text sits on, over `bg`, `panel`, `card`, `hover` and `code`; `faint-text`, `text` and `muted` reach it on `hover` and on `accent-soft`, `add` and `del` over those surfaces. The test composites each tint the way CSS does. Desktop values (worst ratio; the plain color on its 14% tint): dark `accent-text`, `success-text`, `warning-text` and `info-text` are their colors (4.96, 5.01, 5.30, 4.80), `danger-text` `#ef7d82` (4.56; 4.00), `faint-text` `#939faa` (4.53; faint on those rows 3.16); light `accent-text` `#065993` (4.55; 3.49), `success-text` `#066344` (4.53; 3.53), `warning-text` `#784f08` (4.51; 3.52), `danger-text` `#9a2b33` (4.58; 3.45), `info-text` `#3952a4` (4.53; 3.53), `faint-text` `#55616d` (4.56; 3.65); `on-warning` is `#03121c` dark (10.18) and `#ffffff` light (5.07). VS Code's 70% mix raises the contrast in dark, light and high contrast themes alike, but cannot reach AA where the theme's own color already fails on its background: on a 14% tint Dark Modern reaches 4.85 to 5.15 (`errorForeground` 4.15, 4.14 on the plain editor), Light Modern 5.05 to 5.61 (`editorWarning` 3.51 and `errorForeground` 3.72, 2.78 and 2.99 on white), and both high contrast themes at least 5.39. A chip whose tone is chosen at runtime (`agentStatusChip`, overlay status badges, MCP, Compass and context usage chips, agent initials, the desktop Projects list's activity and Untrusted badges) takes `.d-tone-<tone>` (`style.css`), which sets that text color and `--tone`, the color its tint mixes from (`var(--tone, currentColor)`), so the tint never derives from the darker text. For the five status and accent tones `.d-tone-<tone>` also sets `--tone-on`, the text on a solid fill of the tone (`var(--d-on-<tone>)`).
- **A host's safety color is a palette tone** (`HOST_TONES` in `src/shared/host-tones.ts`: accent, success, warning, danger, info; `safety-colors.test.ts`). Its badge (`HostBadge`) takes `.d-tone-<tone>` on a `bg-(--tone)/15` tint, and when the selected project is on a colored host the title bar shows a stripe in the tone with the host's name in `--tone-on` on a solid `--tone` fill (`TitleBar.vue`). A host without a color shows no stripe and a muted badge. The host store keeps only a tone id.
- **`damocles.desktop.theme` and `damocles.desktop.reduceMotion` apply live** (`followThemeSettings` in `theme.ts`, called in `index.ts` before the window exists). The theme (`system` by default, `dark` or `light`; anything else reads as `system`) sets `nativeTheme.themeSource`, which also drives `prefers-color-scheme` and the native menus, and `nativeTheme`'s `updated` event re-themes every view. Reduce motion travels on `PanelTheme.reducedMotion` and becomes the `data-reduced-motion` attribute of each renderer's root element: main writes it into the shell and overlay HTML, and the preloads apply it on load and on each change (`preload/apply-theme.ts`). VS Code reports `workbench.reduceMotion` as the `vscode-reduce-motion` class on `body`, and the webview's entry mirrors that class onto the same attribute, on load and on each change (`mirrorVsCodeReduceMotion` in `utils/reducedMotion.ts`, called from `main.ts` only in VS Code), so one attribute carries the host's setting on both hosts. `style.css` cuts every animation and transition to 0.001 ms and zeroes animation delays under that attribute and under `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce`. Motion that script drives reads the same two sources through `prefersReducedMotion()` (`utils/reducedMotion.ts`), the one check in both renderer trees.
- **`damocles.desktop.fileIconTheme` travels the same way** (`PanelTheme.fileIconTheme`, the `data-file-icon-theme` attribute on each renderer's root element). Every file and folder icon in the shell and the overlay is a `FileIcon` component (`shell/components/FileIcon.vue`; a Quick Open item carries its `file` path for it), which resolves through `fileIconLook()` (`shell/file-icons.ts`) and so reads the attribute and the body's theme kind through `hostAppearance()`; a switch of either redraws open views. A Material icon stays one `<img>` whose `src` changes when a folder opens, a name is edited or the theme kind switches, because a changed `src` keeps painting the current image until the next has loaded while a new element paints nothing for a frame (files.spec.ts records the frames of an expand). `material` resolves as VS Code resolves an icon theme (`material-icons.ts`: `parent/name`, then the name, then each extension from the longest, with the light theme's overrides winning at each step) and draws an `<img>` from `app://damocles/desktop-shell/file-icons/`. Two differences: `languageIds` are dropped, since they need VS Code's language detection (a `Dockerfile.base` gets the default icon), and where VS Code's CSS ties a light override against a more specific base match, the base match wins. `app://` serves an SVG with a CSP that runs no script, since these third-party files are same-origin. A caller passes the path it has, not only the name, so parent-qualified associations such as `.github/workflows` match. The associations are `shell/generated/file-icons.json`, generated from the pinned `material-icon-theme` devDependency by `npm run generate:file-icons` (`generate-file-icons.test.ts` fails when they are stale), and `vite.shell.config.ts` emits exactly the icons the table names. `minimal` is the reference's lucide icons in `--d-*` colours.
- **Desktop renders in Geist and Geist Mono, with Greek in Inter, and no font file reaches the webview bundle or the VSIX** (`src/desktop/main/desktop-fonts.ts`). The table names each upright woff2 subset of `@fontsource-variable/geist` and `geist-mono` and Inter's `greek` and `greek-ext` subsets; `vite.shell.config.ts` copies those files and each package's OFL text into `dist/desktop-shell/fonts/` under the table's stable names, and `theme.ts` declares them in `@font-face` rules with `app://damocles/desktop-shell/fonts/...` URLs and the packages' `unicode-range` (`theme-parity.test.ts` compares each range with the package's own CSS). The rules travel in the host theme style, so the shell page, the overlay and every chat view load them under the `font-src app://damocles` they already allow. `--d-font` is `'Geist Variable', 'Inter Variable', system-ui, sans-serif`, so Greek, which Geist lacks, falls through to Inter; `--d-mono` is `'Geist Mono Variable', ui-monospace, Menlo, monospace`. Italics are synthesized. Neither `vite.config.ts` nor the chat panel CSP names them.
- **`HostCapabilities.damoclesTheme` picks the code highlighting** (VS Code false, desktop true). VS Code keeps `getShikiTheme()`, matched to the VS Code theme's name and kind. Desktop uses the `damocles` Shiki theme, whose colors are `var(--s-*)` and `var(--d-*)`, so a desktop theme switch recolors highlighted code without highlighting it again. Monaco defines its theme from the `--d-*` colors and the `--s-*` token rules, re-applies it whenever the host theme style or the body's theme class changes, and remeasures its fonts after a webfont finishes loading.
- **`src/webview/styles/motion.css` is the one motion source** (`motion-source.test.ts`): the reference keyframes `d-pop`, `d-up`, `d-fade`, `d-zoom`, `d-spin`, `d-pulse`, `d-blink`, `d-slide` and `nt-in` from the Desktop template, the Chat Panel ones (`o-zoom`, `o-fade`, `o-up`, `o-sweep`, `o-grow`, `o-bar`, `d-shimmer`), their exit counterparts, and the easing curves `--ease-out` and `--ease-spring` on `:root`, outside the `--d-` family so the token parity test stays meaningful. Keyframes animate only `opacity` and `transform` (the reference's background-position `d-shimmer` and the `stroke-dashoffset` `d-draw`, which draws an SVG stroke, excepted): the reference's box-shadow `d-ring` is a scaling pseudo-element and its shimmer text is the transform pair `o-glint`/`o-glint-hold`. It also holds the shared enter and exit classes (`t-*` for Vue `<Transition>`, `d-popper`, `d-dialog`, `d-scrim` and `d-collapsible` for reka content; a collapsible's height snaps while its content fades). A component that its parent mounts with `v-if` cannot play its own exit, because Vue skips the leave of a `<Transition>` that unmounts with its parent, so the mount site wraps it (App.vue wraps every overlay in `t-overlay` and the dock prompts in `t-up`). Transcript rows animate only while `useLiveArrivals` marks them as arrived live, never on mount, because the virtualized list remounts rows as it scrolls. `webview/style.css` imports motion.css, and the shell page and the overlay load it through that stylesheet; no other source under `src/webview` or `src/desktop` defines any keyframe or uses a Tailwind animation utility: the built-in `animate-spin`, `animate-pulse`, `animate-ping` and `animate-bounce`, or the `animate-in`, `fade-*`, `zoom-*` and `slide-*` classes of the `tailwindcss-animate` plugin, which is not installed. A spinner uses `.d-spinning`. The reduced-motion rules (`prefers-reduced-motion` and `[data-reduced-motion]`) also zero `animation-delay`, so a staggered entrance never hides content.

## Session store

- **A replayed user message carries when it was sent** (`history-loader.ts`). `userReplay.timestamp` is pi's message `timestamp`, else the entry's ISO `timestamp`, for a prompt and a steer chip alike; the webview shows it, and only a live message takes the webview's clock.
- **A resumed or restored conversation continues on the model and thinking level its file recorded** (`session-store/recorded-selection.ts`, `PiSession.recordedSelectionOf` and `adoptBuiltSelection`). pi resumes on the branch's latest `model_change` or assistant reply and latest `thinking_level_change` (`sdk.js` `createAgentSession`), only when given no model, and then checks neither the catalog nor the plan, so Damocles passes a model explicitly to every session it builds and the session factory reads the opened file first: a recorded catalog model (`recordedModelValue`: the recorded pi id is mapped to its catalog value by its exact provider first, and only an id outside the catalog is read as a retired value, whose `LEGACY_MODEL_MAP` successor it continues on, with the recorded level pi clamps for it, only when the recorded provider serves that successor) is the model the chat shows and pi builds the session on, at the level the panel resolves with the recorded one restored (`recordedReasoning`), and once pi installed that session it becomes the panel's, with its level as the effort and thinking switch (`ThinkingManager.restoreRecordedLevel`); a recorded model outside the catalog leaves the panel its model. A recorded catalog model that cannot run (signed out, outside the plan, or a key provider's model absent from pi's registry because its key is gone, which reads as signed out) is still the chat's model, never replaced by the panel's (`BuiltSelection.runnable`), so the sidebar, which reads the file, and the header name the same model; pi is handed its own model, or the fallback model when pi has none, and every send is refused until it can run or another is picked ("Catalog values and plan gating"). pi records the effective level, so Ultracode returns as Max, which runs the same. Permission mode and YOLO are never persisted or restored: a restored chat takes the settings defaults, so a bypass turned on for one conversation never silently comes back.
- **Nothing may append to a session file after it is deleted.** pi's `SessionManager._persist` sets `flushed = true` on its first write and from then on appends blindly, without re-checking that the file still exists. So a late write recreates the path holding only that one entry. Such a file has no `session` header, `SessionManager.open` rejects it forever, `listPiSessions` skips it, and because it is not in the list it cannot be deleted from the UI. Damocles cannot fix the writer, so it refuses to create the precondition, at three points: every holder detaches before the `rm`; a writer that resumes after an `await` re-checks liveness; and the reader caches negatives so one bad file is re-read once per change, not once per rebuild.
- **Detach is routed by session id, not by "is this my panel".** Two panels resuming the same file get the same session id (it comes from the header), and `registerSessionMutator` is a `Map`, so the second registration displaces the first. `deleteSession` therefore detaches the registered owner and `ctx.session`, deduped. The panel you clicked in may be the displaced one, or may only be pointing at the session as a not-yet-started resume/fork target. A panel mid-`start()` has no runtime yet, so detach awaits `startPromise` before resetting; without that, `reset()` bails, `whenReplaced()` resolves instantly, and `start()` goes on to open a manager on the path being removed.
- **`whenReplaced()` must report reality.** `runtime.newSession()` can throw, and returns `{ cancelled: true }` without tearing the old session down when a `session_before_switch` handler cancels. A cancel, and a throw before pi's teardown, leave the old session installed and still writable; a throw after it leaves no session of the panel alive, and the runtime is released (see "A start, or a replacement that fails after pi tore its session down, leaves no runtime"). The promise rejects for all of them, and the logging `.catch` hangs off a separate handle so the rejection stays observable to callers while never becoming an unhandled rejection. `MessageRouter` catches handler throws, so a failed detach surfaces to the user and aborts the delete with no per-handler try/catch.
- **A start, or a replacement that fails after pi tore its session down, leaves no runtime** (`PiSession.releaseRuntime`). `start()` sets `runtime` as soon as pi built it, and a step after that can still throw: the bind (a registry, the restored tool loadout), the active tool set, the session id announcement or the model publication. pi replaces a session non-atomically: `switchSession` and `newSession` first tear the bound session down (abort, `session_shutdown`, the `beforeSessionInvalidate` hook, `dispose`), then build the next one, install it and await the rebind (`agent-session-runtime.js:102-127`, `:128-173` in pi-coding-agent 1.4.0). A build that throws leaves pi holding the session it disposed, which is cut off from its agent, so a prompt on it persists nothing (`agent-session.js:1011-1030`); a rebind that throws leaves the new session half bound. Either way the replacement rejects with no live session of this panel. The `beforeSessionInvalidate` hook records each session pi tears down (`tornDown`), and the reset and the resume switch, the only callers of pi's replacements once a runtime is bound (Damocles never calls pi's `fork` or `importFromJsonl`), hand a rejection to `releaseIfTornDown`, which releases the runtime only when the session the replacement started from was torn down. One that failed before the teardown (a `session_before_switch` cancel, `SessionManager.open` or `assertSessionCwdExists` throwing, `:128-135`) left that session live, and it stays bound. The rebind does no release of its own, and it adopts a resumed record only as its last step (see "The chat's model has exactly one publisher"). Every such failure goes through the teardown dispose uses: the adapter subscription, every registry entry under `registeredSessionId` (set before the bind's first registration, so a bind that threw halfway is unregistered too, and each registry drops only this panel's entry), pi's runtime and with it its session (skipped when pi already tore that session down, since `runtime.dispose()` repeats the teardown and would send every extension a second `session_shutdown`, `:296-303`), then the leases through `syncSessionLeases`, then the checkpoint service. A dispose that begins meanwhile joins the release in flight rather than disposing the runtime twice. The failed start's `startPromise` is cleared, and a failed replacement clears the finished one, so the next `ensureStarted` builds exactly one runtime; every send, compact and `/btw` calls it and shows "pi failed to start" when it fails again, and a send or compact that waited for the replacement calls it again after the wait (`afterReplacements`), whose failure was already told where it failed. A failed start keeps the conversation the panel was opening: a stored target (one a resume, restore or hand-off already showed on a panel that had not started, or one a failed folder switch's recovery claimed) stays `resumeSessionId` with its claim and lease, a fork target stays unconsumed until a start completes, and the model and effort a resumed session's record gave the chat stay, since the next start builds on that same record. The first start that fails to open a claimed stored target tells the user why ("This conversation could not be opened: {0}", from `PiSession.settleResume`); a later one only refuses its send. An open on a panel pi already holds another conversation in is the exception: its failed switch, one that failed after the teardown included, sends the panel back to the stored conversation it held before, which its webview still shows and the next start reopens (see "A resume shows its conversation only once pi bound it"); the switch points `resumeSessionId` back before the release, so that conversation keeps its lease throughout. A reset that failed after the teardown tells the user at once ("Damocles could not open a new conversation: {0}"), lets go of the conversation it left with its lease, and starts a new conversation on the next start, because its webview was cleared and its sends name no conversation. A replacement queued behind a failed one builds nothing on the released runtime.
- **A resume shows its conversation only once pi bound it when pi holds another conversation** (`message-router/handlers/resume-session.ts`, the restore in `session-handlers.ts`, `PanelManager.resumeInto` for a hand-off, `PiSession.openResumeTarget`). Each open claims the conversation (`claimStoredSession` takes its lease and calls `setResumeSession`), then asks `openResumeTarget`. A panel that has started, or whose start is under way, holds another conversation, and a switch that failed with the new one on screen would leave sends going to the old one, so the open waits for the switch `setResumeSession` queued on `resetPromise` (a start under way queues it at its end, or settles the request when it fails). That outcome is `switched`, `failed` with a reason, or `cancelled` when the panel let go of the target first (a later resume, New chat, a lost lease, a dispose); nothing logs it and drops it. Only after `switched` does the open post `resumeAccepted` (a resume, a hand-off), replay the history and post `sessionStarted`. A panel that has not started holds nothing (`ResumeRequest.atStart`: `setResumeSession` ran with no runtime and no start under way), and its start either binds the target, since it opens `resumeSessionId`, or fails and releases everything, while every send names its conversation. Its `openResumeTarget` resolves `deferred` at once, the open posts `resumeAccepted`, replays the history and posts `sessionStarted`, and only then does the session start, because building the pi runtime (its module loading, on the first chat), the folder runtime and project MCP blocks the main thread in long stretches: a restore starts it after the session list, as every `ready` does (the prompt history waits for the complete list, which a cold index parses for long, so it posts whenever that is done), a resume right after the replay without awaiting it, and a hand-off in the folder switch that runs `resumeInto`. In either order the session's own announcement of a resumed conversation (`onSessionIdChange` with `resumed`) posts no `sessionStarted` of its own. A hand-off gives the session its place entry and working-folder note (`beforeOpen`) before the start that writes them. `PiSession.settleResume` is the one place a failed open is told, "This conversation could not be opened: {0}" through the host's notifications, once per request, with the session state published again. After a failed switch a resume leaves the conversation on screen, a restore opens a new conversation, and a hand-off puts the conversation back where it was, which its caller says. A deferred open whose start fails (its file removed after the replay read it, pi throwing) keeps the conversation on screen and as the panel's target with its claim and lease, which is the contract of every failed start (see "A start, or a replacement that fails after pi tore its session down"): each send runs the start again, is refused with "pi failed to start" while it still fails, and reaches the conversation once it opens. A deferred open whose replay read no file (gone, unreadable) put none of it on screen, and the replay told the user why, so the restore and the resume let go of it instead (`PiSession.letGoOfUnreadTarget`, which keeps a target a start already bound): they post no `sessionStarted` for it, its claim and lease are released, and the start opens a new conversation, which a restore reaches through its fresh route. Kept as the target, it would refuse every send while no start can open it. The resume handler also looks the file up before its folder switch, which would dispose the panel's conversation, and refuses a file already gone with the same notice; a file removed between that lookup and pi's open still fails, inside the switch of a started panel or in the deferred start of the new session a cross-folder resume moved to, which then keeps the conversation on screen in the target's folder. A failed switch sends the panel back to the stored conversation it held before the first request that did not land (`ResumeRequest.prior`, its `storedSessionId` then): with a live runtime only when pi still holds that conversation, else to none; with no runtime (a failed start, a switch that failed after pi tore the bound session down) to that conversation, which the next start reopens. Its lease sync releases the failed target's lease, and the session state is published again. A `start()` whose target the panel let go of meanwhile (New chat, another resume) never binds it: it opens a new conversation, or pi's `newSession` replaces the one pi built before anything bound or wrote to it, and the start's tail switches to a later target. A file not found is a failure wherever it shows: at the switch's lookup, in `start()`, which never opens a new conversation in place of a stored one it was asked to resume, and after pi's `switchSession` installed a session with another id, since pi opens a file that vanished as a new conversation (`session-manager.js:685-689` in pi-coding-agent 1.4.0). pi's `switchSession` rejecting (a file it cannot read, which leaves the old session bound, `agent-session-runtime.js:128-135`, or a session build or rebind that threw after the teardown, which leaves none) and a `session_before_switch` handler cancelling are failures too. The turn the switch stopped stays stopped: a switch that ends before the retire aborts it as a Stop (`abortStoppedTurn`), and one that fails after the retire leaves the retired agents to the next prompt on the old session through `interruptionCheckPending` and `undeliveredScanPending`, which are set before pi's switch. The webview's conversation id moves only with `sessionStarted`, its one publisher, so between `resumeAccepted` and the replay's `sessionStarted` a send still names the conversation the panel left and goes back to the composer.
- **A send names its conversation** (`PiSession.sendsTo`). Every user send from the webview (`sendMessage`, which also carries `/compact`, `queueMessage` and `sendBtw`) carries `sessionId`: the id of the webview's last `sessionStarted` (`sessionStore.currentSessionId`); `sendMessage`, before that, names the stored conversation it shows (`currentResumedSessionId`: its saved state's at mount, then a `resumeAccepted`'s), since a restore replays before its session starts; or null while it has neither, which is a new panel before its session is announced and the time after New chat, a delete's detach or a folder switch until the next one is. The composer and Compact read it from one getter, `sessionStore.sendTargetSessionId`. The replay's `sessionCleared` names its conversation, so a send while it runs still names it. An unnamed `sessionCleared` drops the name: a replay that read nothing posts one before its notice, and a restore that never replayed (a lease or another panel refused it, its open failed) posts one, so its sends reach the new conversation its start opens. A `done` never sets it, since a failed replay ends with one too. The saved state keeps the conversation, so the next restore tries it again. Core compares it with the session pi would prompt once nothing can replace that session any more: `promptPi` after the start, the queued replacements and resume switches, the model switch, the abort, a stopped prompt's settle and pi's settle of a run, after which `sendOvertaken` keeps the send on that session; `compact` after the same waits; `queueInput` against the running session; `sendBtw` against the conversation the aside reads. A send for another conversation goes back to the composer (`promptReturned` `conversationChanged`, with its notice) and appends nothing, so a send made while a resume switch is pending reaches the resumed conversation only if it names it and the switch lands; a `/btw` ends its aside with the reason. A send that names none may prompt only a session with no file on disk. pi writes a conversation's file when its first user or assistant message lands (`session-manager.js:794-810`), and the webview knows a stored conversation's id before it shows it (a restore's saved state, a resume's `resumeAccepted`, or `announceIfNewlyStored` at the first write), so an id-less send for a stored conversation was written while the webview showed another. Core's own sends name what they were written for: the plan's "clear context and start fresh" names the session the clear created (`whenReplaced`, then `currentSessionId`), the review tab's comments the session they were composed on (`submitReview`, which keeps the drafts when it changed), a worktree's held first prompt none, since the move to the worktree gave the chat a new session, and Run in project folder the one the webview named. A steer names an agent, not a conversation: a resume switch retires the agents of the session it leaves. `sendOvertaken` still covers what the comparison cannot: a Stop, and a replacement or disposal during the waits after it (the Always-loaded MCP wait, the working-folder note, the undelivered results, the UserPromptSubmit hook).
- **The session metadata index is a pure cache, trusted only per file** (`session-store/meta-index/`). A worker thread (`worker.ts`, started on first use by `client.ts` and closed after 5 idle minutes with the same 2 s bounded stop as `close`, which waits for it; it holds its host open only while a request is in flight or it is closing) owns `~/.damocles/cache/session-meta/index.s<SESSION_META_SCHEMA>.pi-<pi version>.db`, a SQLite table `files(key, dir_key, size, mtime_ms, stored, prompts)` with each session file's list row and prompts. A row is used only while its file's size and mtime both still match, so a stale or foreign row costs a re-read, never a wrong row. A write that fails (a full disk, an I/O error, a read-only file, a lock held past the busy timeout) is logged and costs re-parses later: the read it belongs to still answers with the rows it parsed, and a put whose commit failed stays served from memory. That is what makes last-writer-wins across processes safe: the desktop app and the VS Code extension open the same file in WAL mode with a busy timeout, each batch of rows commits in one transaction, and every row either wrote is checked against its file before use. The file name carries `SESSION_META_SCHEMA` and pi's version, read from pi's `package.json` without loading pi: apps with the same schema and pi share rows, and a pi upgrade, which can change what `sessionFileMeta` derives, starts a new file. A file that is corrupt (failing `quick_check` at open, or a corruption error later) is deleted and rebuilt. One that cannot be opened or deleted, because another process holds it, leaves this process on an in-memory index, logged. Once per worker start the worker deletes, off its request path, the legacy `*.json` caches and the other `index.*.db` files with their WAL siblings once they have gone 30 days unwritten, since an older app may still write its own. pi parses only on the worker's parse thread (`parser.ts` `createThreadParser`: the same bundle in its parse role, started on the first miss, one file at a time in request order), through `sessionFileMeta`, and loads there, so a warm list never loads it and no parse, however large its file, holds the thread that answers from the index: another folder's list, a `cachedRow` or a `meta` hit answers while a parse runs. A parse the thread could not finish (it stopped or closed) yields no row, so nothing caches a file as unreadable for it. The thread runs under a `PARSE_THREAD_HEAP_MB` (2 GB) heap cap, so a file pi cannot read within it stops the thread instead of growing the process, and a thread that answers nothing for `PARSE_STALL_MS` (60 s) while parses wait is stopped. `list` answers at once with the rows still valid and evicts the rows of files gone from the dir, then parses the misses and sends their rows in batches of `LIST_BATCH_SIZE`, and from a timer every `LIST_BATCH_MS` (250 ms), empty or not, a parse still running included, so the client's request and stall timers see the list alive while one parse runs long. Its `listDone` says `complete: false` when a file yielded no row or the dir could not be read; `StorageManager` applies the rows through the list upsert path and treats such a list as incomplete (below). `cachedRow` stats the file and never reads it. A live session's metadata comes from its in-memory manager with the leaf at the last entry, which is where `SessionManager.open` of the file puts it; reading the manager's own leaf would index a rewound branch the file does not show yet. The stat and that snapshot run synchronously back to back on the host thread (`readLiveSessionMetadata`, the one synchronous stat this path keeps), so pi cannot append between them. That snapshot assumes this window's manager is the file's only writer; two windows holding one conversation live already corrupt the file itself. The snapshot is posted as a `put`, which the worker applies on arrival and serves from memory, so a request posted after it sees it on the ordered port; it commits within 2 s, so a turn does not write the index after every tool call. A parse drops a pending put only when it read a newer state of the file (a later mtime, or the same mtime and more bytes, since pi only appends). The worker's close commits what is pending, and `StorageManager.dispose` awaits that close, bounded to 2 s.
- **`SESSION_META_SCHEMA` (`session-store/meta-index/store.ts`) is 5.** It names the index file, so a new value starts a new file instead of migrating one. Any change to what `sessionFileMeta` returns, or to the `files` table, bumps it; `store.test.ts` pins the value and the file name, and `schema.test.ts` pins `sessionFileMeta`'s output on fixtures that cover every field it fills (a new `StoredSession` field does not compile there until it is placed), so a change to that output, in `metadata.ts` or any module it reads, fails until the schema is bumped with it. A development build opens the release's file while its schema matches, so a build that changes the output writes rows a release serves until those files change: bump the schema before running such a build against your own `~/.damocles`.
- **A stored conversation has one writer across processes: its session lease** (`session-store/session-lease.ts`). The lease is a `proper-lockfile` lock at `~/.damocles/locks/sessions/<sessionId>.lock`, outside every session dir so pi's listing, the session watcher and the list cache never see it. It is stale after 20 s against a 2 s heartbeat, so a killed holder's lease is taken over once stale, never before. `claimStoredSession` takes it with `lockSync` inside its synchronous check-and-bind step, after the in-process panel scan, so a claim never waits on a lease: a held one refuses the claim and the user is told the conversation is open in another Damocles window, where an open path offers to take it over (below). On Windows an `EPERM` from a lock another process is removing counts as held, and any other lease failure refuses the claim with the reason, logged; a claim never throws into its handler. A resume and a panel restore read the lease (`sessionLeaseBlocker`, which takes nothing) before they switch the panel's folder, so a refused conversation leaves the panel's own conversation and folder alone; the claim inside the switch stays the race guard. A lease whose heartbeat is more than three beats behind belongs to a holder that died or hung: the user is told so, and a panel restore refused by it is retried once, when the lease turns stale, unless the panel holds a conversation by then. The panel names the retry's conversation meanwhile (`HostInstance.restoreRetry`, cleared once the retry settled), until it holds another conversation (`PanelManager.dropRestoreRetry`, on each activity report), so the host never attributes it to a conversation it moved on from. A lease an ended process held is taken over at once, not at stale (`setEndedLeaseHolders`: desktop passes each Damocles Core process that crashed earlier in the run with the time main saw it end, `CoreInit.endedCores`; VS Code names none). `acquireSessionLease` takes it only while the lock is provably still that process's (`isEndedHoldersLock`): its owner record, of this host, names the pid and was written before the end time, and the lock's heartbeat last moved no later than the end time plus the second proper-lockfile may stamp ahead (`LOCK_MTIME_AHEAD_MS`). A later holder, a reused pid included, moves the heartbeat past that, and no other process can hold the lock meanwhile, since none takes it over before it is stale; within `ENDED_TAKEOVER_MARGIN_MS` of stale it is left to the staleness takeover, which another process may make at the same moment. The takeover renames the lock dir aside (atomic) and locks afresh (`clearEndedHoldersLock`). It is exact knowledge from the host, never a liveness probe. The lease is per process: panels of one process share it by holder count, and the one-panel rule inside a process stays the panel scan's job. `PiSession.syncSessionLeases` keeps the leases equal to what `holdsSession` reports, so every unbind path releases through it: a switch or reset (the rebind), a delete (`detachFromDeletedSession` resets), a folder switch and a panel close (dispose, after the runtime's teardown has appended its last entry), and host dispose, because `ChatPanelProvider.dispose` awaits every session's dispose (the extension's `deactivate` and desktop's `before-quit` both run it), including those of panels closed before it: `PanelManager` tracks every session disposal it starts until it settles, which is what covers desktop's window close on Windows and Linux, where the loaded chats close before `before-quit`. proper-lockfile also removes held locks on a normal process exit. A dispose cancels every queued resume switch before its first await, a reset's replacement that has not reached pi yet creates no session once a dispose began, and a dispose waits for a switch or `newSession` pi is already running (`piReplacement`), whose session it disposes without binding or registering it, so no replacement leaves a session on a runtime being torn down. A session with no file yet (a fresh conversation, a fork's branch) is leased when it is bound, on the path pi will write, since the lock never touches the session file itself. When the lock is taken from a holder (`onCompromised`: another process found it stale, or it was deleted), the holder replaces its SessionManager's `_persist` with a no-op in that same tick, because pi's manager has no close and a detach lands only after an await, then detaches as for a delete and tells the user. Loss is detected at the heartbeat, so if this process's event loop stalled past the stale window while another process took over, an append queued ahead of the heartbeat can still land once. A file write outside a live panel (a delete, a rename or tag of a conversation no panel here holds, a configured hook's `sessionTitle` rename) runs under the lease too (`writeUnderSessionLease`, through `whileSessionLeased`, which tells the user of a refusal, or `PiRuntime.renameSession`, which rejects with it for the hook to log), as its writer: it is refused when another process holds the session or another write of this process is running on it, and while it runs no new holder of this process joins the lease, so a claim from another panel is refused ("being deleted or changed"). A rename, tag or AI title is also refused while a panel of this process holds the session with no live session to write through (`isSessionHeldByPanelHere`; the user is told it is opening or closing), because that panel may already have read the file and would fork around the write; only a delete joins those panels' lease (`joinPanels`), since it detaches them before the `rm`. A delete takes the lease before any detach, so neither another process nor another panel can claim the session between the detach and the `rm`. A session id reaches the lock path only after pi's own id rule (`isSafeAgentPathId`). A team member's or subagent's session file takes the same lease, keyed by its own pi session id, as its writer (`agent-file-owner.ts`, see "Teams and subagents").
- **A lease holder hands a stored conversation over when another process asks** (`session-store/session-lease.ts`). A process can hold a lease with no window to show it: VS Code Remote-SSH keeps an extension host whose renderer left without a terminate message alive for its 3-hour reconnection grace, its `deactivate` never runs and its heartbeat goes on, and a clean reload races the old host's `deactivate` the same way. Refusing until the holder dies is not enough. Beside each lock, never inside it (proper-lockfile removes the lock dir with `rmdir`), the holder writes `<sessionId>.owner`, `{ v: 1, nonce, pid, hostname, panelToken, acquiredAt }`, through a temp file and a rename, right after `lockSync` and again whenever the panel token it names changes: a token that arrives with `ready` after the lease was taken, or a holder joining or leaving. A failed write is logged once and retried at the next poll, and never fails or releases the lease. `nonce` is fresh per acquisition. On a normal release the holder removes the record before the lock, and only while it still names its own nonce; `onCompromised` never touches it, because the record then belongs to the process that took the lock. A requester asks with `<sessionId>.release`, `{ v: 1, nonce, requestedAt, requesterPid }`, naming the nonce of the owner record it read, so a record a crashed holder left behind matches no live lease. Every process that holds a lease reads each lease's request every 2 s, the heartbeat cadence, from one unref'd interval that runs only while it holds a lease. It polls rather than watches, because proper-lockfile itself polls mtimes (watchers are unreliable on network and remote filesystems, where `~/.damocles` may live). A request counts only when its nonce is the lease's and its `requestedAt` lies within `SESSION_RELEASE_REQUEST_TTL_MS` (20 s) of now either way, which tolerates clock skew between hosts. The holder then removes it and calls `onSessionReleaseRequested` on each holder once, and asks nothing more for that lease until they all answered. A lease a writer holds (`whileSessionLeased`, or a handover's own writer) defers the request to a later poll. Every lease file is input from another process: a session id reaches a path only after `isSafeAgentPathId`, no path comes from file content, a read takes at most 1 KiB, every field is validated, and a missing or bad file reads as no record (a bad one logged once), never a throw.
- **A handover finishes this process's writes before the lease goes** (`PiSession.onSessionReleaseRequested`). It joins the lease as a `writer` first, synchronously: the detach's rebind releases the panel's own hold while the old session can still append, and while a writer holds the lease no panel of this process claims the session. Then it lets an in-flight start settle, stops a running turn through the Stop path (`interrupt`), so the `damocles-turn-stopped` entry lands while the lease is held, and detaches as a delete does (`detachSession`, which `detachFromDeletedSession` calls): the reset aborts the turn, drains checkpoint work for up to 2 s, installs a fresh session, posts `processing: false`, `sessionCleared` and the session state, and waits up to 10 s for the aborted agents. It tells the user "This conversation was opened in another Damocles window, so this panel closed it." and only then releases its writer, which removes the owner record and the lock. The writer's loss handler mutes the old manager, because after the reset the panel no longer holds the session and its own loss handler ignores it. A disposed panel ignores the request. A dispose that begins during a handover takes over: the handover stops, waits for the dispose's teardown, which appends the aborted turn, then releases its writer once. A replacement that fails before pi's teardown leaves the old session installed and the panel's own hold keeps the lease, so the requester times out; one that fails after it releases the runtime, and with it the panel's hold.
- **Only an open asks, and only the same panel asks without the user** (`chat-panel/session-ownership.ts`, the restore in `message-router/handlers/session-handlers.ts`). The webview makes a `crypto.randomUUID()` panel token once, keeps it in its persisted state, which every `setState` spreads and which VS Code's panel and view state and desktop's `panels.json` keep across a reload or restart, and sends it on every `ready`. No path copies one panel's state into another. The host keeps a well-formed token on the `HostInstance`, hands it to every session the panel runs (`setPanelToken`, on each rebind in `PanelManager` too, so a folder switch keeps it), and reads anything else as no identity. `requestSessionHandoff` writes the request, polls the lease every 250 ms, counting its own polls so a suspend never cuts the wait short, and returns `released` once the lease is free or `timeout` after `SESSION_RELEASE_REQUEST_TTL_MS`, always withdrawing its own request. The wait and the TTL are one constant: a requester withdraws its request when it stops waiting, so a request older than the wait was left by a requester that died, and the TTL keeps a holder from acting on it. A holder that picks up a live request near the end of the wait can still finish after the requester gave up. The conversation is then closed there and open nowhere until the user opens it. After `released` the caller claims through `claimStoredSession`, and a third process can still win that claim. A restore its lease refuses decides in this order: a holder whose heartbeat stopped keeps the retry at stale; a live holder whose owner record names this panel's token (this panel's previous host, orphaned or still deactivating) is asked in the background with no notice while the panel opens empty on its saved folder, and once it let go the conversation resumes (`resumeStoredSession`) unless the panel closed or holds a conversation by then; on timeout, and for any other live holder, the user gets the refusal with Open here. Only the open paths offer Open here (the restore, `resumeStoredSession` and desktop's unloaded-chat select, each passing a `LeaseTakeover` to `announceLeaseRefusal`), never a rename, tag or delete, and only when an owner record was readable. Choosing it re-reads the lease and the owner record, asks, and opens through the same open path. No toast choice and no takeover is awaited inside `ready`, the folder queue or desktop's chat queue. A holder from a release before handovers writes no owner record and reads no request, so it is refused with no Open here, and a stale record left beside its lock only times out into the refusal. Every refusal logs the holder's pid, hostname and when it took the lease.
- **A list upsert never applies metadata older than the list it lands on** (`chat-panel/storage-manager.ts`). A watcher event whose file still matches its cached size and mtime applies the cached row and never reads the file: on Windows a read updates the last-access time, which `fs.watch` reports as a change, so reading would re-trigger the event. An upsert captures the list generation before its metadata read and is dropped if the list was invalidated (rename, delete) or its folder closed meanwhile, because the list loaded since then read the file later. A delete event during an in-flight load invalidates that load, which may have listed the file before it went. Rows the index parses after a list answered carry that list's generation and the same drop rule, and one never replaces the row of a session a watcher event, an upsert or a delete settled since the list loaded (`readSinceList`), because that read came later. A parsed row from the project's own session dir replaces one a registry folder listed for the same session, as the list's own merge does; between registry folders the first listed wins. Their pushes and change events go out at most once per 250 ms (`PARSED_ROWS_PUSH_MS`), so a cold list of many batches posts a few first pages, not one per batch. A decision a missing row would make wrong (removing deleted worktree records, the prompt history, which nothing rebuilds when rows arrive, and the desktop app's stored-chat lookup once the shown lists miss) reads the list only once every parsed row of its load was applied (`StorageManager.completeSessionsOf`, the catalog's `listComplete`). `completeSessionsOf` rejects when a file of the list yielded no row, so the worktree prune is skipped (logged) and the lookup fails instead of deciding on a missing row; the prompt history reads such a list anyway and misses only that file's prompts. Display, a worktree's share count included, reads it as it fills in and is republished on each change. A catalog rename or tag puts the written file's row in the index before the list reloads, so the row never drops out.
- **A prompt index has one definition, `pi-session/session-store/prompt-index.ts`, read by both the live session and the reload.** A prompt is a user message entry on the active branch other than one delivered mid-run (a queued batch or a cancel note, both keyed by a `damocles-mid-stream` entry), counted from the root. `PiSession.sendMessage` stamps a new prompt with `nextPromptIndex` of the branch it extends, `agent-start.ts` keys its injection with `nextPromptIndex` of the dispatch's own branch, and `history-loader.ts` stamps a replayed prompt as it walks the branch with the same `promptTest`, so the `userMessage`/`userReplay` index, the injection message's `details.promptIndex`, the Injected Context record, the permission turn key and the memory candidate name the same prompt live and after a reload. A per-panel counter cannot do that: a resumed or forked panel starts it at zero, and a reload that counts only the messages it displays restarts after every compaction. Counting from the root keeps an index unique within one session file, which is what lets the injection database key on it. That holds because Damocles never moves a leaf back within a file (a rewind forks to a new session and so a new database); anything that navigates a branch in place must first re-key those records by user entry id. A fork inherits its parent's indices below `nextPromptIndex` of the branch at the fork point, so `spawnPiFork` copies exactly those records into the fork's database through `MemoryService.copySessionInjections`; the fork's own prompts start at that index. A session-id migration whose source has no database leaves the destination's records in place (`renameInjectionDatabaseFile`), because every session start migrates from the panel id. The injection reads the dispatch's branch rather than panel state because pi commits the user entry only after `before_agent_start` and adds no prompt before it, while a run can start with no `sendMessage` in flight (a resent steer that finds the run settled). A cancel note's entry gets the mid-stream marker once pi commits it, so it consumes no index and reloads with the amber styling of its injected live echo. A run a note starts, because pi accepted it with no run in progress, dispatches before that entry exists, so `agent-start.ts` recognises it with `isCancelNoteDispatch` and keys it to `nextPromptIndex - 1`, the prompt the note annotates; it builds no memory injection, as a note steered into a running run gets none, because a record under that index would replace the annotated prompt's Injected Context, and it posts no injection chips. A note's run still sends a changed Compass status, keyed to that index; a note written before notes were marked has no marker, keeps the index it was counted with, and reloads as a prompt. An echo that commits no user entry (a pi extension command such as `/todos`, a locally handled `/compact` or `/init` notice) is emitted injected with the latest prompt's index, so it names no prompt and the next prompt keeps its own number. That uses the existing `isInjected` flag; never add a message type for it. A `/compact` echo is posted from `PiSession.compact`'s `onAccepted`, so one it refuses (a turn or another compaction running) leaves no echo, only its notice.
- **A reload shows a stopped turn as the live view did** (`session-store/turn-stopped.ts`). On Stop the adapter abandons each call in flight that pi has not started (no `tool_execution_start`, which pi emits for every call it handles, before the gate: `agent-loop.js:384-389`, `:420-425` in pi-agent-core 1.4.0), because pi sends no end for it, and drops any later assistant error, but pi still writes each started call's aborted result (its text varies by tool) and can write an error entry for the request the Stop cut off. Neither says the user stopped the turn, so `beginAbort` appends one `damocles-turn-stopped` entry after the run settles (and a resume switch away from a running turn appends it through `persistTurnStopped`, before `switchSession`): every call in flight at Stop (`toolCallIds`), and the ids of the assistant error entries written after the leaf at Stop. A started call keeps its live card until pi ends it under the aborted signal, so it never shows Not executed first and then Stopped. A call pi executed was running when the Stop landed: pi kills it, records its error result with its `durationMs` (`:589-597`) and then emits its `tool_execution_end` (`:403`). The adapter shows that end, and `history-loader.ts` replays the call with that result and time; the stopped-result marker (see "Shell cancellation and process lifetime") makes both show it stopped, with its output. A call with no `durationMs` never ran (the abort settled it before `execute`, `:506-510`, `:525-529`, `:441-444`, a call waiting at the gate included), so the adapter abandons it at its end, and the loader replays it abandoned without the result pi wrote while stopping. An aborted run's `agent_settled` abandons any started call whose end never came, so no card spins after the turn. The loader drops the error rows the entry names. Never infer a stop from result text. A session written before the entry existed replays them as failed. Nested sessions (subagents and team agents) have no Stop path that writes the record, so the nested extension factory registers `registerAbortSettledCallRecord` (`stopped-tool-result.ts`), which appends one `damocles-turn-stopped` entry per call whose `tool_execution_end` has no `durationMs` while `ctx.signal` is aborted, whatever aborted the run. pi appends it and emits its `entry_appended` inside the extension pass, before listeners get that `tool_execution_end` (`agent-session.js:752`, `:2783-2788` in pi-coding-agent 1.4.0), so the live transcript reads the same entry a reload does: the subagent bridge sends `toolAbandoned` `stopped` instead of the failure and passes the ids to its sealing snapshot, and the team runner sends no `teamAgentToolResult` for the call and re-seals its message with it `stopped` (`resealStopped`). On reload `parseAgentEntries` collects the ids (`AgentFile.stoppedToolCallIds`), and `piMessagesToHistoryAgentMessages` and `memberHistoryMessages` mark such a call `stopped` with no result, by the main chat's rule: named and without `durationMs`. An agent file written before the entry existed replays the call as failed. Both the nested factory and the main extension register `registerWindDownErrorRecord`, which appends, as a `turn_end` draft, a `damocles-turn-stopped` entry whose `entryIds` names an assistant error entry whose `turn_end` arrives while `ctx.signal` is aborted (`isWindDownError`): pi dispatches `turn_end` from `finishTurn` once it persisted the message (`agent-session.js:523-525`, `:751-763`), so the entry id is known, and commits the draft before listeners get that `turn_end`, which is where every live transcript reads it (see "An error card is shown only for a model call pi will not re-run"). A Stop's own record leaves out an error that entry already names (`turnStoppedRecord`), so no error is named twice. `history-loader.ts` replays a named error with no card, its calls and the result-less calls of the batch before it `stopped`; `parseAgentEntries` maps the ids to their messages (`AgentFile.windDownMessages`), and `piMessagesToHistoryAgentMessages` and `memberHistoryMessages` replay each as an aborted stop with no error row. A file written before that record existed replays the error row.
- **A turn's `userMessageIdAssigned` names the entry its own `prompt()` committed** (`prompt-entry.ts`, read by the adapter through `promptEntryId`). The webview keys the prompt's rewind control, its fork point and its checkpoint marker on that id. Never read the branch's last user entry for it: pi persists a message only after its `message_end` has reached every listener (`_handleAgentEvent`), so at the prompt's own `message_start` the last user entry is the previous prompt's, and a steered batch or note can commit after the prompt within the same run.
