- **The two Damocles-owned MCP files are migrated once to pi's format** (`migrateOwnedMcpFile` in `mcp-config-write.ts`, run before each read: a lock-free read first, and the file's `writeJsonConfig` critical section only when something needs migrating, so a load never waits out a stale lock). `$env:NAME` becomes `${NAME}` (pi would read `$env` as a variable named `env`) and `oauth.redirectUri` becomes `oauth.callbackUrl`; no alias for `redirectUri` remains. The rewrite is idempotent and touches nothing else, so a bare `$`, `$$` or leading `!` in an old value now has pi's meaning. `.damocles/mcp.local.json` is migrated only in a trusted folder and written only inside its `.damocles` directory (`confineTo`). Damocles never rewrites `.mcp.json`, `.pi/mcp.json`, `~/.pi/agent/mcp.json` or another tool's file.
- **Remote OAuth runs on pi-mcp's flow over the keychain store** (`mcp-auth-flow.ts`, `mcp-oauth-provider.ts`, `mcp-auth.ts`). Credentials stay in the host secret store keyed by server name and URL; `createOAuthStateStore` maps the stored entry to pi-mcp's `McpOAuthState` and back, so sign-ins stored before pi-mcp keep working, and discovery results stay in memory. A connection never opens a browser: it sends the stored token and refreshes after a 401 through `adaptOAuthProvider`; with no refresh token, or on an `insufficient_scope` challenge (a refresh keeps the granted scope), it throws `McpOAuthAuthorizationRequiredError` and the server shows as needing authentication (`hasScopeChallenge` marks the step-up). A connection's store never changes the stored PKCE verifier or CSRF state, so a background refresh cannot clobber a sign-in in progress. The Authenticate action runs `authorizeMcp` with the configured scope (on a step-up, also the granted and the challenged scopes, skipping refresh). Dynamic registration sends `client_name: oauth.clientName ?? 'Damocles'`. `client_credentials` stays a Damocles `AuthProvider`: discovery plus a token POST, because pi-mcp has no such grant. Every OAuth request goes through `createOAuthFetch` (`mcp-oauth-provider.ts`): both `authorizeMcp` calls of a sign-in and the connection refresh (pi-mcp threads `OAuthFlowOptions.fetch` and the refresh context's `fetch` into protected-resource and authorization-server discovery, registration and the token endpoint), Damocles' `discoverOAuthServer` calls, the `client_credentials` registration and token POST, and revocation. It refuses a URL that is not https or loopback http with `OAuthInsecureEndpointError` before anything is sent, so discovered endpoints (`authorization_servers[0]`, `registration_endpoint`) are held to the rule too. It never follows a redirect, because a followed 307 re-sends the body (code, client secret) to a URL the check never saw; the 3xx reaches pi-mcp as a failed response. It bounds each request, body included, by 15 s on top of the caller's signal (revocation keeps its own 10 s). The browser's authorization URL must be https or loopback http as well. A connection never registers a client: its provider's `clientInformation()` throws `McpOAuthAuthorizationRequiredError` when no client is configured or stored, and pi-mcp asks for it right before registering, on the first run and on its `invalid_client` retry alike. The keychain store is safe for several connections of one identity: a save whose tokens or client equal what that store last loaded keeps what is stored now, because pi-mcp's provider saves what it loaded outside the identity lock. Every state write goes through the provider (`McpOAuthProvider.update` reloads just before it saves), never a Damocles `load` followed by an `await` and a `save`, which would write tokens loaded before a concurrent refresh rotated them. A save that changes only the in-memory discovery writes nothing to the keychain. Server error text is flattened and capped before it reaches a message, and the failure log lines (connect, ping, list, transport, OAuth) get `failureForLog` (`server-manager.ts`): an HTTP failure logs its status, a non-JSON server reply fixed text and an OAuthError its code, never response-body text or server output. A connect error decides its log text from the error it wraps (`McpServerConnectError.logText`), because its message copies that error's server text. A stored credential blob that fails to parse is logged without the parse error, which quotes the blob.
- **pi-mcp enforces RFC 9207 and the configured metadata document; the refresh-path metadata document is local** (`mcp-auth-flow.ts`, `discoverOAuthServer` in `mcp-oauth-provider.ts`). The code exchange passes the redirect's `iss` to `authorizeMcp`, which rejects with `OAuthIssuerMismatchError` before sending the code when `iss` differs from the exchange's issuer exactly, is missing while the metadata sets `authorization_response_iss_parameter_supported`, or is present when discovery found no authorization server metadata, since there is then no issuer to compare it with (`runFlow` in `oauth/flow.js`). With no `oauth.authServerMetadataUrl`, `signInWithBrowser` reads the discovery pi-mcp saved for the redirect right after the first `authorizeMcp` returns `REDIRECT`, failing closed if there is none, and runs the exchange on the provider with that copy pinned (`withDiscovery`), so the redirect and the exchange see one authorization server even if another connection of the same identity dropped the stored discovery during the callback wait. With a URL, sign-in passes it as `authorizationServerMetadataUrl`: pi-mcp fetches that document for each flow (trusted as configured, so its issuer is not checked), caches nothing, and re-reads it for the exchange, so nothing is pinned. `adaptOAuthProvider` takes no metadata URL and refreshes at the provider's discovery, so a connection with a URL overrides `discoveryState` to read the configured document inside each refresh flow, which pi-mcp runs once for concurrent 401s; a discovery cached before the URL was configured never receives a refresh token. `client_credentials`, revocation and that `discoveryState` override call `discoverOAuthServer`, which is pi-mcp's `discoverOAuthServerInfo` through `createOAuthFetch` with the configured URL. Step-up scopes come from pi-mcp's `stepUpScope`.

- **Per-tool exposure: Off, On (deferred), Always loaded (`direct`).** Each MCP descriptor carries `exposure`, `exposureSource` and `configExposure`. The manager sets the config value (`mcp/exposure.ts` `configToolExposure`: the server's exact `toolExposure` key, then the first matching `*` pattern in file order, then the server's `exposure`, then deferred; pi's `hidden` reads as off and `codemode`/`codemode-deferred` as deferred). The pattern matcher treats every character but `*` literally, is anchored at both ends and runs in linear time (each literal segment found once, leftmost, by KMP); never swap in a generated regex, which backtracks. `FolderMcpView` overlays `damocles.mcp.toolExposure`, resolved per tool from `inspect()`: local over project over user, project and local only in a trusted folder, never from the host's merged value, whose object merge differs between hosts. The overlay lives in the view, not the manager, because the user manager serves every folder and trust is per folder. The panel's three-way control writes one entry at one scope (`mcpSetToolExposure`, `toolExposureMapForScope`): it reads that scope's map from `inspect()`, sets or removes the entry, and removes it when the scopes below and the config already give the chosen value. The scopes offered are those the host store can write in that folder (`McpManager.toolExposureScopes`: User; Project in a trusted folder with a project layer whose write target, the window's project (`windowSettingsProject`, none on a daemon's core), exists and is trusted too, because `damocles.mcp.toolExposure` is a window-level key written with no folder and desktop refuses an untrusted project; Local where the host keeps a local file, i.e. desktop). Renaming a `~/.damocles/mcp.json` server moves its `damocles.mcp.toolExposure` key at every scope, project included. The panel's writes run one at a time on the process-wide settings write chain (`settings-write-chain.ts`, which `SettingsManager.serializeSettingWrite` and the runtime's list migrations use), the one chain every setter that reads a list or object setting and writes it back shares (`tools.disabled`, `effortByModel`, the Explore, Background and Memory judge model and effort pairs, a rename's `toolExposure` and `resourceExposure` moves, and both `tools.disabled` migrations: renamed tools into `toolExposure`, retired resource tools into URI-scoped entries), each `inspect()` read and `update()` write inside it, so a quick second change never starts from a value without the first; a write that a higher scope's entry for the tool still overrides posts a notice naming that scope. `PiRuntime` listens on the setting itself, so a change from any writer (the panel, a `settings.json` edit, Settings Sync, another window, the desktop settings editor) runs every folder's `refreshActiveTools`, which re-applies each session's active set, republishes ToolSearch and re-sends that panel's MCP status; the handler has no refresh path of its own and sends status only to the requesting panel, since a failed or unchanged write fires no change event.
- **The first prompt of a session waits, up to 10 s, only for servers with Always-loaded tools that are still connecting** (`mcp/startup-wait.ts`, pi's startup wait). It runs in `PiSession.promptPi` before `prompt()`, for a prompt that would open a run (not an extension command such as `/todos`, and not a prompt pi would queue into a streaming run), and the panel keys "already waited" by session id, so a replacement session waits again. It must stay out of pi's `before_agent_start`: pi counts the run as busy from before those handlers (`_startRun`, `agent-session.js:1423-1424`) and cannot refuse it from there, so a prompt stopped during a wait there would still be committed. Before `prompt()` the wait is a pre-dispatch step of the send ("A send holds the session from its first step"): a Stop, a reset (New chat), a resume switch or the session's disposal aborts the send's signal, which ends the wait at once, returns the message with its images and chips to the composer, and leaves nothing in the session. Messages queued during the wait are held and steer into the run the prompt opens. The wait fails soft: a throw from it is logged and the prompt is still sent. Its notice is an ordinary `notification` (plural for several servers, and a second one naming the servers still connecting when the 10 s pass), and a server that connects later has its tools active from the next turn. Eager connects emit tools-changed once the batch settles, so a slow sibling can hold the wait to the cap.

## MCP OAuth callback server

- **Damocles keeps its own callback server instead of pi-mcp's `OAuthCallbackServer`,** because the rules below (port reuse across sign-ins, strict ports, CSRF state reservation) live here. `oauth.callbackUrl` with a port binds that port strictly, and the redirect URI is sent exactly as written, because servers compare it as a string; `oauth.callbackPort` binds strictly on the callback URL's host and path or on `127.0.0.1/callback`; with neither, a pre-registered client (`clientId`) binds `MCP_OAUTH_CALLBACK_PORT` (default 19876) strictly, and a dynamically registered one binds non-strictly, trying the port of its stored client's registered redirect URI first. A `localhost` callback binds `127.0.0.1`. The callback hands `code` and `iss` to the flow.
- **The loopback callback server lives only while an authorization is pending or reserved** (`mcp/mcp-callback-server.ts`, RFC 8252). It closes when the last one settles (success, error redirect, cancel, `CALLBACK_TIMEOUT_MS`), and `ensureCallbackServer` re-checks after its serialized bind, so a flow that settled mid-bind leaves nothing listening. The bound port, host and path survive the close: a settled flow's token exchange still sends the redirect URI it was authorized with, and the next flow rebinds. A non-strict flow first rebinds the port of the stored client's redirect URI, else the port the previous non-strict flow was given (`lastEphemeralPort`, kept for the process lifetime, through `stopCallbackServer` too), and falls back to an OS-assigned port only on `EADDRINUSE`, because a dynamically registered client is registered with its redirect URI and a changed port discards the client and its tokens on the next sign-in. On a strict (pre-registered) port, `EADDRINUSE` names the likely cause, another Damocles window completing a login for that server, before the config fields and `MCP_OAUTH_CALLBACK_PORT`. The security headers and the state-keyed CSRF lookup are unchanged; an unknown state settles nothing and closes nothing.

## Desktop host

- **Main reaches the engine only through the core protocol** (`src/desktop/main/core-client.ts`, served by `src/desktop/core-host/core-service.ts`, which builds `ChatPanelProvider`; main serves the engine's platform from `core-services/host-services.ts`; the protocol is in "Platform boundary and shared stores"). `CoreClient` holds what core pushes, core's events and core's requests. The chats mirror holds each registered chat's core panel id, view id, session id, `hasConversation`, folder, settings folder and held prompt; the folder targets mirror holds the registry's projects and owned working folders with their branches and the default target. Events carry activity, settled turns, chat review totals, usage crossings, worktree state and catalog changes, each after the mirror delta it depends on. Main reads the mirror for what it shows and to choose what to ask; it never reads the mirror and then acts on the engine with it: a decision that needs core's exact state is one request core decides. An unload is `chats.unload`, which core refuses once the chat is active, holds its worktree setup, holds a browser page or waits to retry its restore, and, for an empty chat, once it has a conversation or a session file; D33's busy rule for removing a project is `chats.projectBusy`; a lease check is `chats.leaseRefusal`, whose takeover comes back as `chat.takeover`; a worktree finish hold is `worktree.beginFinish` and `worktree.endFinish`. A chat view main creates (a restore, New Chat, selecting an unloaded chat, New Chat in This Worktree) is relayed from before main asks core to attach to it, since core asks for its page at once, and its view state is published after that request, which creates the proxy it updates. The e2e hooks reach the engine only through core's `e2e.*` requests, served only when `CoreInit.e2eHooks` is set (unpackaged, `DAMOCLES_E2E_HOOKS=1`), with fixtures in place of closures (a browser route rewrites to a host and records the URLs). Main's value imports from `src/core` are limited to `MAIN_CORE_IMPORTS` in `eslint.config.mjs`: stateless helpers (the error classes main rebuilds, git's version text, the worktree record rule) and main's own per-process services (its log, launcher pool, config writes, platform accessor), and none from `src/desktop/core-host`; type imports stay allowed, written as `import type` (`no-import-type-side-effects`), because `verbatimModuleSyntax` keeps `import { type X }` as a side-effect import that bundles its module. `scripts/__tests__/main-core-imports-lint.test.ts` lints the forms and bundles main to check that its own files import exactly the allowlisted modules and that the bundle holds no engine entry (`chat-panel`, `PiSession`, `ScmService`, `core-service`).
- **The engine runs in the "Damocles Core" utility process** (`src/desktop/main/core-process.ts`, `bootstrap.ts`, `src/desktop/core-host/index.ts`). Main forks `dist/core-host.js` (`coreHostEntryPath`, from inside `app.asar` when packaged) after `app.whenReady()` with `serviceName: 'Damocles Core'`, the environment `hostEnvironment` makes of main's after the login shell merge (Electron's own variables dropped; no secret, nonce or token in the environment or argv), stdout following main's only in the unpackaged app, whose log sinks echo there, and stderr piped to a tail main logs only when core ends without main asking. The one ordered channel is `child.postMessage` and `process.parentPort`, which core, the pty host and the formatter host read through `parentChannel()`, the same adapter that reads a forked child's IPC channel (whose fork deletes `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE` from its own environment); a host whose IPC channel disconnects ends, core after its `core.shutdown` steps or 3 s (main's `CLOSE_TIMEOUT_MS`), whichever comes first. No renderer gets a `MessagePort`. Main's first message is the init (`CoreInit`, parsed with `parseCoreInit`; a bad one exits core): app info, the e2e flag main computed (core never reads `DAMOCLES_E2E_HOOKS`), git's probe, main's app paths and askpass paths, the logs folder, the pids of the cores that ended holding session leases, the worktree records main last read from a core (`worktreeRecords`, which the new core's registry serves while `registry.json` does not parse) and every mirror read at fork time; core holds what arrives after it until its service serves (`createParentPortTransport`). Every core start forks a new process with a fresh init, endpoint, host services and `CoreClient`, so no mirror version or state crosses a restart; a reload (`CoreHost.reload`, `lifecycle.reload`, the e2e `reloadCore`) is one too. Core is handed to callers only once it published its folder targets (`DesktopCore.ready`, `CoreHost.current` and `ready`), which `CoreClient.defaultTarget` and every chat restore read. A core not ready within `CORE_READY_TIMEOUT_MS` (120 s; VS Code waits 60 s for its extension host's earlier ready message) is logged (`[core] not ready within`), killed and takes the crash path, so a hung startup never holds the launch or a request forever. Desktop main keeps the windows, views, menus, overlay, shell IPC, dialogs, trust prompts, notification center, theme, `panels.json`, the pty and formatter hosts, Quick Open's worker, `safeStorage`, Chromium proxy resolution, `powerMonitor`, the updater, its stall monitor and its launcher pool (search, Quick Open, terminal profiles, the login shell and git probes). Core runs `ChatPanelProvider` and all it builds, source control and the worktree services, its own launcher pool (the POSIX shell sentinel among its children), the network dispatcher and its own stall monitor. Core writes `logs/Damocles_Core.log` and `logs/Git.log`, main `logs/Damocles.log`, so each file has one writer; core's stall profiles go to `logs/stalls/core` and its stall lines name "the core process". Main logs `[core] started`, `ready`, `shut down in <ms> ms`, `exited (pid, code)` on every exit and every crash, and only for an exit it did not ask for `ended (pid, code, reason)` with the stderr tail.
- **An engine crash restarts the engine without taking down the window** (`core-process.ts` `restartDecision`, `index.ts` `coreCrashed`, `CoreHost.restart` and `halt`), VS Code's extension host policy. Every exit main did not ask for counts: a crash, a kill from outside, a protocol violation kill, a missed ready deadline, a bad init or an exit before core was ready. The first three inside a rolling 5 minutes restart core at once with an information notice ("The Damocles engine stopped unexpectedly and was restarted."); the fourth forks nothing and halts the engine (`CoreHost.halted()`, published as `ShellState.engineHalted`, which shows the title bar's Engine stopped pill). Its error notice is sticky (`NotificationCenter.stickyNotice`): it never times out, dismissing its toast leaves Restart on its bell row, Clear all keeps that row, and it is withdrawn once a reload starts. Its Restart, the pill and Restart Engine (`MENU_IDS.restartEngine`, in the Chat menu and the palette, which replaces a running core too) clear the count, start core and reopen the tabs. While the engine is halted, a second launch, the tray, the Dock and a toast action still reveal an existing window (`showWindow` reopens a closed one only with a running core), and Remove Project skips `chats.projectBusy`, since nothing can run; during a restart or reload it still asks the new core. A crash after the quit stopped the host (`disposing`, `done`) is only logged. On a crash the endpoint closes, so every pending request rejects, the mirrors go with their client, `HostServices.dispose` closes every view the dead core left open with the tabs' states retained, and the reload path reopens the chats, each with the permission mode and YOLO the core that ended held for it; a request made during the restart waits for the new core, one made while core stays down is refused. That holds for a request made while the tabs reopen into the core that crashed, one waiting on a core that ends before it is ready, and one waiting on a reload whose new core does: `CoreHost.next` stays with the crash path, whose restart resolves it and whose halt refuses it. Main's `WorktreeRecords` and `ScmClient` outlive a core: the records stay until the next core publishes its own, which start from them while `registry.json` does not parse, and the scm mirror resets, telling `ScmHost` every repository closed, until the next core reports. Repository ids carry a nonce each `ScmService` draws (`repo-<nonce>-<n>`), so an id main held across a restart names no repository of the next core, which answers it as unknown; a worktree finish hold is core's (`FinishHolds`, `core-host/worktrees.ts`), and its action goes to the core that gave it (`CoreClient.beginWorktreeFinish`), so a restart in between fails the action with a notice instead of running it on a core that holds nothing. The pid and end time of every core that ended without `core.shutdown` answering (a crash, or a kill after the shutdown failed or timed out, `CoreStart.leftLeases`) go into the next init, so the next core takes over the leases it held at once and a chat whose conversation it held reopens with it ("Session store"); retention keeps the chat loaded while its restore runs. A reload core asks for itself (`lifecycle.reload`) is bounded the same way (`coreRequestedReload`), so a core cannot fork cores without end. A quit during launch opens no chat: the launch reopens its tabs only once its first core is ready and the quit has not begun (`openTabsAtLaunch`), and `openInitialChats` takes the ready core and makes the views before its first await, so a request a reload held finds them. A restart never changes what a chat may do, as VS Code's extension host restart restores its state: a chat's permission mode and YOLO live only in core's `PermissionHandler`, which core publishes on the chat's mirror entry (`ChatEntry.permissions`, again on every change through `PanelManager.onPermissionsChanged`, before an open prompt the change approves runs). As a core ends (`DesktopCore.dispose`, a crash, a halt and every reload alike), main keeps for each open chat view what that core published, else what main handed that core for it, so a core that ends before it registered a reopened chat passes it on (`KeptPermissions`); a chat no core ever published reopens with the defaults. Only the reload's own reopen (`CoreHostDeps.openTabs(afterRestart)`) hands them back, in `chats.restore`: core sets them on the new panel's handler before its session is created, which takes its tool set from the mode, and the restore's move to its conversation's folder keeps them (`HostInstance.permissionsHandedBack`) where a launch's takes that folder's YOLO default. A launch and a reopened window hand nothing and forget what main kept, and main keeps it in memory only, so a relaunch starts every chat from `damocles.permissionMode` and `damocles.dangerouslySkipPermissions` (YOLO is per panel and resets on restart). A plan-mode chat reopens bound to the same plan file, which follows the conversation's session id (`getPlanFilePath`).
- **One process owns every git queue: Damocles Core** (`core-host/scm.ts`, `core-host/worktrees.ts`, `main/scm/scm-client.ts`, `main/worktrees/engine.ts`). Core builds the git runner from main's probe, the askpass server and `ScmService`, so two runners never race on `index.lock`. `ScmHost` in main reads the `scm` mirror (each project's view and real path, each open repository's facts and snapshot, decorations, the clone state) and calls the rest as `scm.call` (a method of `SCM_CALLS` with its arguments), `scm.commit` (the save-before-commit list, and a token for the new branch's name, which core asks back with `scm.branchName`), `scm.ignoredIn` (an array), `scm.commitBlob` (bytes), `scm.generateMessage` (by repository id, run on core's runtime) and `scm.repo` (the reads of `REPOSITORY_CALLS`); core publishes each mirror delta before the `scm.changed` event and before any reply whose work caused it. A repository goes out whole (its facts and snapshot, status included) once per status revision, and a change that lands no status (the queue going busy or idle, a remote operation starting or ending) as `repositoryState`, its busy, running and last remote flags alone, which main parses field by field. The worktree registry is core's; main reads its records from the `worktrees` mirror (`WorktreeRecords`), each checked with the registry's own rule (`validRecord`, `core/worktrees/records.ts`) and looked up with its own predicates (`liveChatRecordAt`, `liveChatRecords`) against main's registry folder before trust follows a working folder to its project, and `WorktreeController` keeps the questions, dialogs and the Setup terminal while its git and registry work runs in core (`worktrees.call`, `WORKTREE_CALLS`, typed by `WorktreeCalls` in `core-protocol/scm-calls.ts`, from which main's `WorktreeEngine` and `FinishHold` derive). Core reaches main's worktree UI over `worktrees.reviewSetup`, `runSetup` (its steps come back as `worktrees.setupStep`), `showSetupTerminal`, `stopSetup`, `confirmHandOff`, `showRefusal` and `showApplyConflicts`, and the askpass prompts over `askpass.prompt`, whose answer is a credential and never reaches a log line or an error text.
- **Renderer traffic is relayed through main** (`views.ts`, `core-services/host-services.ts`, `preload/panel.ts`). `isPanelSender`, `asJsonValue` and `isWebviewMessage` stay the one validation point for untrusted renderers, and a validated message goes to core as a panel envelope. Core sends a webview message as JSON text with its type (`post`), which main forwards to the page unparsed and the panel preload parses (text that is not JSON is dropped there with a console warning), so `damoclesBridge` is unchanged; main parses only the page-state types it persists (`urlChanged`, `navigationState`, `pickingStateChanged`). A message with a top-level binary field, a browser page's frame, goes as a structured clone (`postBinary`); binary nested deeper would reach the page as `{}`. The preload's synchronous `init` is answered from main's own state (the saved webview state and the theme), never by waiting on core. A panel id core issues names the page URL and the views map, so `PanelViews.create` refuses one already open or outside `[A-Za-z0-9-]` (`isPanelId`), and host services refuse a create for an id they already relay; either refusal answers core `disposed` (an adopt of a relayed id throws in main). A diff core opens in the editor pane is held by its view id only until its tab closes (`HostDiffView.closed`), since core never closes a checkpoint diff.
- **Trust is per folder, by exact path** (`src/desktop/main/trust-store.ts`). An allowlist of absolute folder paths plus a schema version lives in `<userData>/trusted-folders.json` and matches only on `folderKey` equality: a trusted parent never trusts a child, and a trusted child never trusts its parent. An unreadable or unknown-schema allowlist trusts nothing. Adding an untrusted project asks once through the overlay's dialog (Don't Trust is Cancel and takes focus first). The folder stays untrusted until the user grants trust, and a decline persists nothing. A grant writes the allowlist atomically, then trusts the folder in memory, fires `onDidGrantTrust([folder])` and takes effect with no restart. VS Code's trust database is separate, so a folder trusted there still prompts on desktop. A remote project's trust, and its chat worktrees', is the host store's (`<userData>/remote-hosts.json`), found by the project key of the root (`createProjectTrust` in `main/remote/remote-trust.ts`), so no target path is ever looked up in or written to this allowlist.
- **Desktop stores change memory only after their file write lands** (`trust-store.ts`, `projects.ts`, `usage-warning-store.ts`, `local-services/key-value-state.ts`, `platform/secrets-store.ts`). Each builds the next state inside the `writeJsonConfig` mutation, from memory as it stands once the earlier writes to that file have landed, and commits it and fires its listeners after the write resolves. A write that fails changes nothing, so memory and disk never disagree for the rest of the run and a retry is not a no-op; a folder trusted in memory but not on disk would never fire the grant and would be untrusted again after a restart.
- **The Projects section's order is one list in `remote-hosts.json`, and main writes it before the shell moves a row** (`main/remote/host-store.ts` `setOrder`, `setGroupOrder`, `setGroupCollapsed`, `addProject`, `placeAfter`; the `SHELL_CHANNELS.projectsReorder`, `projectsReorderGroups` and `projectsSetGroupCollapsed` handlers in `main/shell.ts`). `order` holds every project key, local and remote, so both views and every list of projects shown to the user (Open Chat in Project, the palette, the New Terminal picker, Quick Open and source control roots, through `workspaceProjects`) follow it; `projects.json` stays in append order for an older version to read. A reorder write accepts only a permutation of the keys shown when the write runs, checked inside the store's serialised write, so a stale renderer can neither drop nor invent a project; anything else resolves false and writes nothing. Keys the remote gate hides keep their stored slots, and the shown keys fill the slots the shown keys held; a removed project's key leaves `order`, local and remote alike, so a folder added again comes last. The group order (`groupOrder`, by authority key, `local` for this computer) and the collapsed groups follow the same rule; switching views never reorders projects. The shell awaits the write and renders the order from the ShellState that follows it, so a reorder made just before a quit is on disk once the quit's flush settles. A project added after another (Files' Open as project) is placed in `order` before `projects.json` lists it, so it shows in its place at once.
- **A quit waits for every desktop store's writes, and both hosts' teardown waits for core's config writes** (`jsonConfigWritesSettled` and `allJsonConfigWritesSettled` in `src/core/config/json-config-write.ts`, `flushCoreWrites` in `src/core/chat-panel/index.ts`, `settlePending` in `src/shared/settle-pending.ts`, `flushStores` in `src/desktop/main/index.ts`, `core.shutdown` in `src/desktop/core-host/core-service.ts`, `deactivate` in `src/vscode/extension.ts`). Each store that writes through `writeJsonConfig` has a `flush` that settles once its files' write queue is empty, asking the queue again after each round so a write queued while it waits is waited for too, and never rejects: `panels.json`, `window-layout.json`, `projects.json`, `trusted-folders.json`, `remote-hosts.json` (`main/remote/host-store.ts`), `remote-state.json` (`main/remote/remote-state.ts`), `usage-warnings.json`, `state/global.json` and `state/workspace.json`, `secrets.json`, and the settings store's user file, every file it wrote and every loaded project's project and local files. The quit awaits them all together after the window closed and core's dispose, which can still write them, within the 10 s bound, with `allJsonConfigWritesSettled` for main's own writes outside the stores (a settings file editor save); main's launcher pool is disposed after them; core's `core.shutdown` awaits `flushCoreWrites` after `ChatPanelProvider.dispose`, so core's own writes have landed before main flushes. `flushCoreWrites` settles every `writeJsonConfig` write in the process, asking again after each round: `~/.damocles/mcp.json` and `.damocles/mcp.local.json`, settings-file editor saves, setting writes, and permission rule writes and renames. VS Code's `deactivate` awaits it after `ChatPanelProvider.dispose`, even when that dispose fails, for at most 5 s, the time VS Code gives `deactivate` before it ends the extension host. Hot-exit backups flush the same way earlier, when the editors are released.
- **Desktop secrets are encrypted with the OS credential store, or kept in memory and the user told** (`platform/secrets-store.ts`, `src/core/volatile-secrets-notice.ts`). `SecretsStore.isPersistent` is false when safeStorage cannot encrypt (no encryption, or Linux `basic_text`); `withVolatileSecretsNotice` then warns once per run, on the first secret stored, that it lasts only until restart, with an action that opens the README's "Desktop credential storage". An entry that fails to decrypt (a locked keyring, a denied keychain) keeps its ciphertext in the file across later writes and is dropped only when its key is stored again or deleted, because a keyring unavailable for one run must not cost the user every saved token.
- **The renderer is untrusted.** Every WebContents (the window, each chat and page view, the overlay view and the notification popup's view) runs `contextIsolation: true`, `nodeIntegration: false` and `sandbox: true`, with `app.enableSandbox()`; three bridges, one per kind of page: a chat or browser page reaches main only through the panel preload's `damoclesBridge`, whose calls into main are `postMessage`, `setState` and the `init` read (`getHost` only reads the label main's init answer names from its host store, and `onHost` hears the host of an init main refused at first once it answers), the window page only through `damoclesShell` and the overlay view and the notification popup only through `damoclesOverlay`; no page gets a second bridge. `app.on('web-contents-created')` hardens every WebContents: popups are always denied (http/https go to `ShellService.openExternal`, which accepts only http and https and opens the canonical href); `will-navigate` and `will-redirect` are cancelled for anything but the exact `app://` page main loaded; `will-attach-webview` is prevented. Permissions are denied by default, media and notifications included. The single exception is `clipboard-sanitized-write` (`navigator.clipboard.writeText`, for the copy buttons), granted only to a registered panel view's own webContents, main frame, exact `app://damocles` origin, through one predicate shared by the request and check handlers; `clipboard-read` stays denied, and device permissions are denied. No paste path reads the clipboard into a renderer without a user action main observed itself (`src/desktop/main/clipboard-gestures.ts`): a key or mouse press in `before-input-event` or `before-mouse-event`, an overlay pick, or an application-menu click or accelerator. Synthetic DOM events and Playwright's CDP keys never reach those hooks, so tests press keys with `webContents.sendInputEvent`. The window page can still run commands in a terminal it creates, so keeping script out of that page remains the real boundary. The `app://` handler serves only files that stay inside `dist/webview` or `dist/desktop-shell` after decoding, resolution and realpath (plus the single app icon file), and in-memory panel pages by exact panelId plus the shell, overlay and notifier pages at their exact paths; anything else is a 404, including the built `index.html` files, which carry no CSP (every page is generated in memory under one). A script response carries its own `default-src 'none'; script-src app://damocles`, because a worker does not inherit its page's CSP. Panel IPC uses fixed channel names registered on each view's own `webContents.ipc`, which only that view's messages reach, so the renderer never carries its panel id (a process argument would be wrong once Chromium reuses a renderer process across same-site views); each handler still accepts a message only from that view's webContents, main frame, on its own `app://` page URL, and JSON-normalizes and bounds the payload before core sees it. A refused `init` answers null and the preload asks again on its next call. `EditorService` never launches a file: an approval diff shows in the asking chat's read-only overlay and everything else in the editor pane, both built by main. The shell's file IO goes only by a main-issued id or by a project key plus a relative path, and core confines a mention again through `Platform.confinement` (`resolveMention` refuses a file outside the chat's folder and never realpaths a named file itself). `ShellService.openFolder` opens only a folder, on both hosts, and `openExternal` opens only http and https in the canonical form on both hosts (`src/core/web-url.ts`), so a model-named path or a third-party URL can never launch a local handler. The window page opens the application menu only after a primary click or an activation key (Enter as it goes down, Space as it comes up) that main observed in that page: a one-shot grant in `ShellHost` (`isPrimaryClick`, `isActivationKey` in `clipboard-gestures.ts`), cleared when the page loses focus. A page script's call alone is logged and opens nothing.
- **The login shell environment is merged once, before anything spawns or connects** (`src/desktop/local-services/shell-env.ts`). On macOS and Linux the app runs `$SHELL -l -i -c` once on the process launcher, which main installs first, in its own process group with a 10 s deadline that kills the group, and reads the environment its own binary prints (running as Node) between random markers, so profile noise on stdout cannot corrupt it. Stdout is decoded as UTF-8 across reads (a non-ASCII `HOME` or `PATH` split between two reads stays whole), its 4 MB cap counts bytes, and the environment is taken as soon as the closing marker arrives, so a profile's background job that keeps stdout open neither delays the launch nor is killed; the launcher lets go of that output once the shell has exited and it has been silent for 1 s (`exitOutputIdleMs`). The command holds only the binary path and the marker; no environment value is ever interpolated into it. A failure is logged and the launch environment kept. Windows GUI apps inherit the user's environment and skip the merge. Then `probeGit` checks `git` once, within 5 s: a timeout kills its tree and settles at once, so a `git.exe` wrapper whose real git holds the output never holds the launch. Its absence is one localized warning, and `setCheckpointGitAvailability` turns checkpoints and rewind off with that reason instead of failing per operation. The extension never sets it, so its checkpoints probe `git --version` per session as before.
- **A quit closes the window before anything disposes, and resumes on a new task after its prevented `before-quit`** (`src/desktop/main/index.ts`, `shutdown.ts`, `e2e/desktop/quit.spec.ts`, `e2e/desktop/shutdown.spec.ts`). `before-quit` is prevented while the quit asks about running terminals and unsaved editors (`releasing`; a cancel leaves everything running). Every close and quit shares the question under way, and every quit the quit under way, which resolves true when the user cancelled it (`QuitLifecycle` in `shutdown.ts`, VS Code's `pendingQuitPromise`), so a second Alt+F4, tray or Dock Quit while it asks opens no second dialog. A question a close or quit asks first shows the window, restoring it when minimized, since a window hidden to the tray would hide it and while it tears down (`disposing`). Once nothing can cancel it, nothing opens a chat or a page (`PanelViews.create` refuses while the chats close with their states kept, as for a window close and a host reload), and the window closes through its own close path. The settings modal is dismissed with it and follows none of its chats (`OverlaySettings.close`, `OverlayHost.dismiss`). The placement is saved and `panels.json` and `window-layout.json` are sealed (`seal`; a window opened again unseals them), as VS Code saves its window state on `onBeforeShutdown` and a closing window does not rewrite it (`windowsStateHandler.ts`), so the next launch gets the selection, chats and layout from before the teardown. Every chat then closes with its state kept, and main waits for its `closed` event and for every shell and overlay request it was still answering (`ShellHost.settled`, `OverlayHost.settled`); only then do the core, the terminals and the file watchers dispose and the stores flush, as VS Code's `onWillShutdown` follows the last window's close (`lifecycleMainService.ts`). No page can call into a disposed service: a request either arrived before the window closed and is answered against the live core, or never arrives. Nothing reopens the window meanwhile (a second launch, the tray, the Dock), and the shell is sent no chats-changed while a closing window or core closes the chats. The wait for the window and its requests is bounded at 3 s inside the 10 s teardown, after which the core and the terminals dispose and the stores flush anyway; once the quit can no longer be cancelled, a question a closing page was asking answers Cancel (`createMessageAsker` `closing`) instead of asking again in the OS message box, so its request settles. The whole teardown is bounded at 10 s, and then `app.quit()` runs again. Electron's `Browser::Quit` stores the handler's prevented result only after the handler returns, and a quit started in native code (Cmd+Q, the Dock, logout, CDP `Browser.close`) runs the handler as the outermost JavaScript frame, so its microtasks run first. A dispose that settles in microtasks would then call `app.quit()` inside the first quit, which overwrites it, so the second `app.quit()` waits for `setImmediate`. Disposing core sends `core.shutdown` (`ChatPanelProvider.dispose`, the runtime, source control, the worktree temp folders, core's config writes and its logs; each step runs after a failed one, and the reply is the first failure) and waits for the reply at most `CLOSE_TIMEOUT_MS` (3 s), then kills core and waits for its exit. On macOS and Linux the kill is SIGTERM, which signal-exit's listener (`proper-lockfile`) takes and runs only on a free event loop, so a core that has not exited `CORE_KILL_GRACE_MS` (1 s) later gets SIGKILL (`forkCore`); on Windows the kill is TerminateProcess. The log records each step (`[shutdown] quitting`, `[window] closed`, `[core] shut down in <ms> ms` in main's log after `Damocles core disposed` in core's, `[shutdown] disposed; quitting`, `[shutdown] will-quit`); `window-all-closed` during the quit's own close is not logged, and the log sink closes at `will-quit`. A host reload keeps the window open while core is replaced. A shell, overlay or menu request that needs core runs through `CoreHost.run`, which hands it the running core at once, or during a reload the core the reload starts, and the request passes that core to everything it calls; only callers that cannot run without a core use `requireCore`. A request safe to repeat (the chat list and search) runs through `CoreHost.read` instead: one whose core ended under it (`hasEnded`) runs once more on the core main is already starting in its place, so a crash never fails a list; with no core coming, after the fourth crash, it is refused like every other request. A mutation never repeats, since its first run may have applied. The launch's restore passes its core to the page restore too, and a chat closed while it was being restored, by the quit among others, gets neither a session nor its pages (`restoreInCore`, `restorePages`). A reload disposes the old core only after the requests running on it settled, waiting at most `CLOSE_TIMEOUT_MS` (3 s) and logging each one still running then, as VS Code's lifecycle joins pending work before a shutdown; a quit during a reload waits for that bounded drain inside its own 10 s bound. The teardown's first step is `CoreHost.stop()`, which refuses a request still waiting on a reload, so the window's requests settle before it closes. Before the terminals' question, a close or quit asks about remote agents whenever a usable connection runs a chat turn this device started (`control.listOwnRuns`, never another device's; a daemon without the feature cannot tell devices apart, so the question lists every run its frozen `listRuns` answers) (`close-remote.ts`, `close-release.ts`, `damocles.desktop.remote.closeWithRunningAgents`). Each host's list and each Stop is bounded by `CLOSE_REMOTE_STEP_MS` (3 s): a host past it is left out with a log line, as one that fails. The dialog shows at most `MAX_OVERLAY_ITEMS` rows, each title and project cut to `MAX_OVERLAY_LABEL_LENGTH` with an ellipsis. The answer acts only once the terminals' and editors' questions passed too, so a later Cancel leaves the agents running and writes no `away` record: Keep running then pushes the refreshed tokens within 5 s and records the runs (`remote-state.json` `away`, with the core epoch they ran under), the views detach as the window closes, and the teardown lets a session mirror sync under way land and then sends `control.disconnect`, each wait bounded by `REMOTE_DETACH_WAIT_MS` (3 s, `detachRemotes`); Stop agents runs each chat's Stop path (`chats.stop`); Cancel cancels the quit, and any cancel asks every question again next time, as does the next close after a window opens again (macOS keeps the app after a window close). An OS shutdown (`powerMonitor` 'shutdown', Windows `session-end`), the updater's restart and a dialog that cannot show keep the runs with no question; that holds only for the quit it started, so a cancelled one leaves the next close asking.
- **Main-process failures reach the log, never Electron's error dialog** (`src/desktop/local-services/process-errors.ts`, `index.ts`). An uncaught exception or unhandled rejection in main is logged, as VS Code's main process does, and so is one in core, in core's log. The stores are read after the log sink is installed and inside the startup error handling, so what they skip is logged and a store that cannot be read ends in the startup error dialog. The log sink reports its own write failures once on stderr (a failed rotation keeps appending, a failed append drops that batch); lines logged while a write runs go out together in the next one, and past 5 MB waiting they are dropped and counted in the log. A chat or window page that crashes more than three times in a minute is left dead and the user is told: a toast with Reload Chat for a chat, the overlay's dialog with Reload Window for the window page, which draws the title bar and sidebar. A toast raised before the window exists, while it is closed or before the popup window's page has loaded waits in main, and its timeout starts once that page shows it. `app.setName` runs before any path is read: `damocles` when packaged, `damocles-dev` unpackaged, so a dev build shares userData, the safeStorage key and the single-instance lock neither with every unpackaged Electron app nor with an installed Damocles. For the same reason the unpackaged app's AppUserModelID is `io.github.aizenvoltprime.damocles.dev`. Electron creates a Start Menu shortcut for the AppUserModelID only when the app calls `new Notification` or `Notification.isSupported()`, and the app calls neither, so no shortcut carries the ID and Windows takes the name and icon of a taskbar pin, the jump list and Task Manager from the running binary. The unpackaged app therefore runs on a branded copy of the Electron runtime, never the downloaded one: `dev:desktop` and `test:desktop` run `scripts/brand-dev-electron.mjs`, which copies `node_modules/electron/dist` to `dist-damocles-dev` with Damocles' icon and the product name "Damocles Dev" in `electron.exe`, as electron-builder brands `Damocles.exe`, and redoes it only when the icon or the downloaded binary changed. The copy keeps the name `electron.exe`, because Electron reports `app.isPackaged` for any other executable name, and lives in its own folder, because the shell caches an icon by path. `scripts/launch-desktop.mjs` and `playwright.desktop.config.ts` select it with Electron's `ELECTRON_OVERRIDE_DIST_PATH` (`devElectronDist` in `scripts/dev-electron-binary.mjs`), never Playwright's `executablePath`, which drops the preload Playwright adds to the app it launches. A host reload (`core-host.ts`) joins a reload already running until that reload starts its core, since the core is built from what holds then; one asked for later runs once that reload settles, and every request meanwhile joins it (VS Code's `Throttler`), so a remote host's reconnect during a reattach's reopening attaches again over the newest connection and a connection never gets two attaches. A reload starts only once the chats being reopened into the running core are back (`CoreHost.openTabs` is the only caller of `openInitialChats`: the launch, a reopened window and the reload itself), starts no core once the quit stopped the host, stops waiting for its new core to be ready once the quit stops the host (the quit then disposes that core), and still starts a new core when the old one fails to dispose.
- **Chats stay loaded by retention, and leave memory only by retention or Delete** (`chat-pool.ts`, `index.ts`, plan AD8). `chatsToUnload` is a pure function over the loaded chats: it keeps the selected chat, every active chat (running, waiting on the user, `background` true, holding browser pages or restoring them), every remote chat holding messages its host has not taken (held or not sent, `holdsMessages`), every remote chat of an authority with no usable connection (an unload would reach core only on the resume), every restoring chat and the 3 most recently viewed idle chats that hold a session file, and unloads the rest; a chat with no session file is never unloaded, except an empty new chat (no conversation and no file), which is dropped once the selection leaves it. A chat core is still setting up is neither idle nor empty. A chat is restoring while its saved state (`DesktopPanel.savedSessionId`, the session its `panels.json` entry names) names a session core has not bound, and while a restore its lease refused waits to retry at stale (`HostInstance.restoreRetry`, published as `ChatEntry.restoring` before the refusal reaches the page, which then drops the saved session), because core registers a restored chat and reports its activity before the webview's `ready` claims that session: one rule (`sessionIdOf` in `index.ts`: the bound session, else the saved one) makes it neither idle nor empty and lists, selects and persists it under that session id, never as a `new:` chat, until core binds it or the webview's state drops it (a refused claim starts a fresh conversation, and each change of the saved session re-runs retention and the list). Retention runs after every selection, activity event, settled turn and view change, and each unload waits in that chat's work queue (`ChatWorkQueue`), behind any selection, rename, tag or delete of it, and runs only if the pool still says so then. An unload asks core (`chats.unload`) only over a usable connection, and a drop can hold the request, and with it the page's close, until the resume, so a chat whose unload core has not answered is never shown: a show of it waits for the answer (`ChatUnloads` in `chat-pool.ts`), then shows the chat core kept, or opens the one it closed again from its session file, and is dropped when another chat was shown meanwhile. Core closes the chat only while it is still idle, holds no browser page, is not waiting to retry its restore and, unloaded as empty, is still empty; the close is the existing close path (dispose, drain checkpoints, release the lease), which also deletes the chat's `panels.json` entry. `background: true` keeps a chat until a later activity event reports it false; main never times it out. Selecting an unloaded chat asks core's lease check first (`chats.leaseRefusal`, `leaseRefusalFor`): another process's lease shows the refusal toast and the selection does not move. Otherwise main creates a view restored with `{ sessionId, workspaceFolderKey }` and the webview's `ready` resume path binds it, through `claimStoredSession` like every other bind. Quit, window close and a host reload close every loaded chat in core's order with the states retained, so the persisted selection and each chat's saved pages stay as they were; no chat or page opens meanwhile, and a window close or quit seals `panels.json`: a closed selected chat then selects nothing, and the next launch restores every saved chat, shows the saved selection (by panel, then session, then project) before any loads and loads it first. A mention or Add to Chat for a chat main is still restoring (`chatRestores`, recorded for every chat: those main restores, `openInitialChats`, New Chat and selecting an unloaded chat, and those core opens itself, Open Chat, a fork or a host prompt with no chat loaded (`openCoreChat`), which wait for `PanelManager.whenSetUp` through `chats.whenSetUp`) waits until core restored it and its page is ready, then delivers to it; when core restored it without registering it, a toast says the chat could not be loaded. A restored background chat lays out when first selected, because Chromium sends a view's size to its page only once the view has been visible (a never-shown view's page reports a 0×0 viewport whatever its bounds). Main remembers in memory the last chat viewed per project; selecting a project selects it while it exists, else a new chat in that project, so the title bar always names the selected project and chat. A remote project selected or added while its core does not run moves Files, Search and terminals at once, and its chat opens once that core has reopened its chats (`remote/remote-selection.ts`, after every start, restart and reattach), unless a chat of that authority, or another project, was selected meanwhile; until then the chat view keeps the chat it showed, and the title bar and window title name the project alone, never with another authority's chat. A chat's project is its folder's while the project list holds that folder, else the default target's, since core sets the chat up or moves it there (`projectOf`); the selected project follows the selected chat on every view change and folder registry change, so main never publishes a project its own list refuses.
- **One process-wide network dispatcher in Damocles Core, installed before the first connection** (`src/desktop/core-host/network/`). Main makes no Node connection of its own (the updater uses Electron's net). `installCaCertificates` sets Node's default CA list to the bundled roots plus the OS store (`tls.getCACertificates('system')`, which returns the OS store on Electron 44) plus the `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` file, read after the login shell merge and deduplicated by fingerprint. In the packaged app Electron strips an inherited `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` (see "Desktop packaging and updates", fuses). `installProxyDispatcher` installs an undici dispatcher with `setGlobalDispatcher`, plus undici's `fetch`, as pi's CLI `configureHttpDispatcher` does, and with its settings (pi's `httpIdleTimeoutMs`, `allowH2: false`, CONNECT tunnels, the ignored `error` listener). pi's library entry never calls `configureHttpDispatcher` (only its CLI `main()`, `rpc-entry` and interactive mode do), and nothing in Damocles Core may, or it would replace this one. When `HTTP(S)_PROXY` is set it governs every origin through undici's `EnvHttpProxyAgent` (with `NO_PROXY`), because Chromium ignores those variables on Windows and macOS. Otherwise each origin asks main's `session.defaultSession.resolveProxy` over the `network.resolveProxy` request, which applies the OS settings, PAC and WPAD, and takes the first route undici supports (`PROXY`, `HTTPS`, `SOCKS5`, `DIRECT`); `SOCKS` (v4) and `QUIC` fall back with a logged reason. Answers are cached per origin for 60 s, dropped once expired at the next lookup, and cleared on `powerMonitor` `resume`, which main pushes to core as `power.resume`, because Electron exposes no network-change event. Logs name a proxy by scheme, host and port only, never its credentials. `safe-fetch.ts` validates every hop before it calls `fetch`, so its SSRF guard applies unchanged through a proxy. NTLM and Kerberos proxy authentication are not supported.

- **A sidebar section's title actions take no space at rest, so its title reads in full.** They show while the section is expanded and the pointer is over it or focus is within it (header or body), and whenever it is expanded with `damocles.desktop.sidebar.alwaysShowHeaderActions` on; a collapsed section shows none, and its count badge always shows. `SidebarSection.vue` owns the rule for every section, ported from VS Code's `paneview.css` (`.pane:hover`, `.pane:focus-within`, `.actions-always-visible`, each with `.pane-header.expanded`) and `viewPane.ts` `updateActionsVisibility`. Focus leaving the page (to the overlay or a chat view) hides them, as in VS Code, so a title action that opens a popup wraps it in `holdActions`, which keeps the actions shown until the popup settles; otherwise the menu's own button would vanish under the menu. A section with no actions renders no actions bar. The setting reaches the shell as `ShellState.sidebar`. e2e specs reveal actions by hovering the section (`sectionAction` and `clickSectionAction` in `e2e/desktop/support/shell-ui.ts`), never by turning the setting on, so a test that calls either takes the `foreground` fixture, since the OS gives the pointer's hover to one window at a time.
- **A sidebar section moves by CSS `order`, never by moving its DOM node** (`Sidebar.vue`, `SidebarSection.vue`, `layout/section-order.ts`). Dragging a section's header onto the upper or lower half of another puts it above or below that one, as VS Code's `ViewPaneContainer` and `ViewPaneDropOverlay` do, and the header's menu (right-click, Shift+F10 or the Menu key) offers Move Up, Move Down and Reset Section Order, the non-drag path WCAG 2.5.7 asks for. A detached node loses its scroll offset and focus, which would leave the virtualized lists in Chats, Files and Source control drawing rows for a scroll position they no longer have, so the DOM order never changes. `Sidebar.vue`'s template lists the sections in `SIDEBAR_SECTIONS` order (Projects, Chats, Files, Source control, Graph, Repositories, Search), so in the default layout the DOM order is the visual order. Each section's `order` is odd and each sash's even, and the sidebar's `reading-flow: flex-visual` makes Tab and assistive technology follow the order on screen. The drag carries `SECTION_DRAG_MIME`, so a file drag over a section is ignored. Chats still fills the height the others leave wherever it sits. The sashes follow VS Code's `SplitView` (`section-sizes.ts` `sashRange` and `moveSash`, ported from `resize` and `updateSashEnablement`): one in every gap, whatever the order, which moves height between the sections above and below it nearest first, past collapsed ones, and is disabled (no pointer, no focus, no value) while neither side has height to give. A sash's value is its position, the WAI-ARIA window splitter's. The order is `ShellLayout.sectionOrder`, a permutation of `SIDEBAR_SECTIONS` that main refuses otherwise (a malformed one in the file falls back to the default alone), except that an order 3.9.0 saved, the six sections before the Graph, gets the Graph right after Source control with no log line; Restore default layout resets it. e2e drives the drag through the DevTools protocol's drag interception (`dragSection` in `e2e/desktop/layout.spec.ts`), because Playwright's `dragTo` starts no HTML5 drag in Electron.
- **The shell renderer is the window's own page and reaches main only through `window.damoclesShell`** (`src/desktop/main/shell.ts`, `src/desktop/preload/shell.ts`, `shell-channels.ts`). Main generates `app://damocles/shell/index.html` on every load with a fresh script nonce (CSP `default-src 'none'`, nonce `script-src`, no inline handlers) around the built `dist/desktop-shell` assets. It draws the title bar (logo, breadcrumb, sidebar and theme toggles, and on Windows and Linux the window controls, which send a `WindowControl` main validates against `WINDOW_CONTROLS`; macOS draws its traffic lights) and the sidebar's Projects and Chats. The shell preload exposes exactly the calls `DamoclesShellApi` declares (`shell-channels.ts`), from project, chat (`chats:list`, `search`, `select`, `new`, `rename`, `tag`, `delete`) and window calls to source control's `damocles:scm:*` channels (`DamoclesScmApi` in `scm-channels.ts`), and has no access to the panel or overlay channels. Every `damocles:shell:*` handler accepts a call only from the window's own webContents, main frame, on the exact shell URL (`isPanelSender`, read at call time). Chat ids are issued by main (a session id, or `new:<panelId>` for a loaded chat with no file). Project keys, `new:` ids and the chat a select names are resolved against main's own lists; a stored chat id for rename, tag or delete is resolved by the catalog, which accepts only one safe path segment that `StorageManager.folderOf` finds among the open folders' session files; ids are bounded to `MAX_ID_LENGTH` (209, the root key of a worktree of a 200-character project key, `MAX_ROOT_KEY_LENGTH` in `folder-root.ts`, which `MAX_SCM_ID_LENGTH` and `file-drag.ts` follow too) and searches to 200 characters, a name to 1..200 and a tag to 1..50 after trimming, an overlay request is rebuilt by `parseOverlayRequest`, the app menu anchor and the chat slot must be finite, non-negative and below 100000 CSS px, the sidebar layout (`sidebar-layout`) and the grid's sash sizes (`grid-sizes`) arrive as separate reports, each taken only whole and within its minimums and merged by main, and the focused part is only `sidebar`, `editor`, `terminal` or null. Every refused select, rename, tag or delete reaches the user as exactly one toast (core shows the lease refusal), so the shell shows nothing of its own; a rename, tag, delete or popup request that fails in main is reported the same way and resolves (`reportFailure`), a popup the overlay could not show as dismissed. A host rename and a Projects reorder that fail in main are only logged and resolve null and false, which the shell says in place: the rename field that "the name could not be saved", the list's inline alert that the order was not. A chat list that fails to load is the one failure the shell shows itself, with a retry, and it never leaves another project's chats on screen. A chat list or search names the `ShellState.revision` its project key came from, since main may change the project list while the request is on its way (`ShellHost.chatList`): a key that state did not name is refused, and a key it named that main's list no longer holds answers null once main has sent the state without it, which the shell waits for, listing the project that state selects; main never answers null for a project its own state still names.
- **The overlay view is the window's top-most view and reaches main only through `window.damoclesOverlay`** (`src/desktop/main/overlay.ts`, `src/desktop/preload/overlay.ts`, `overlay-channels.ts`, plan AD1). Menus, dialogs, the settings modal and the notification center render in one transparent `WebContentsView` per window at `app://damocles/overlay/index.html`, which main generates like the shell page (nonce `script-src`, `font-src app://damocles`, `img-src app://damocles data:`, `base-uri` and `form-action 'none'`, no workers) around `dist/desktop-shell/assets/overlay.js` and `overlay.css`; the shell build fails when any page's fixed-name asset is not emitted, because Rollup emits identical stylesheets once. It is not a registered panel view, so it holds no permission, and it loads without taking focus. Main owns its two bounds modes: `hidden`, and `full`, the whole content area at the shell's zoom while a popup is open, so the shell's CSS-pixel anchors land unchanged; toasts never render here (D52's popup window). It restacks last after every view is added or moved up (`PanelViews` calls `onRestack`), so nothing draws over it. A popup is a typed request main validates field by field and rebuilds (closed `OVERLAY_ICONS` allowlist, at most 100 items, bounded ids, tags and dialog text, a menu's label, caption and item labels cut past their bound to `…` and their tail so a path keeps its file name, finite coordinates); main issues its id, focuses the overlay only while the window is focused (otherwise it flashes the taskbar button and focuses the overlay from the window's `focus` event, `focusOnActivation` in `index.ts`) and forwards it. That flash and pending focus end when the last popup closes (`popupsClosed`, called before focus returns, since focusing a view can activate the window) or the window does, so a popup answered before the window activates leaves neither behind. The overlay page acknowledges a request as its handler receives it, before rendering it, and must do so within 2 s or main hides the overlay, logs it and rejects every open request; an acknowledged popup never times out. The acknowledgement proves only that the page is loaded, mounted and answering: a first render can take seconds on a cold or starved renderer (a first launch of freshly built files, CPU contention), and a deadline on the render would hide a popup about to show, so the user's click would do nothing and the popup's own requests would find it closed. What waits on the popup being on screen (the bell marking its entries seen) runs once the page reports it shown, after Vue rendered it, with no deadline. Main counts this deadline, and the rasterize one, in 250 ms ticks of its own timer rather than wall-clock time: a block of main's event loop costs one tick, so an acknowledgement that arrived during the block still counts, where a single 2 s timer would fire first and drop the user's answer. One popup is open at a time: a second request dismisses the first, except that a `message` dialog or a `worktreeDialog` (main's questions, never the shell's) stacks above whatever is open and nothing but its own answer closes it, so Quick Open, the palette or a menu opened by a key cannot answer a setup review, a Delete or a Merge (`stacks` in `overlay.ts`). An answer counts only for an open request, and only of its kind (a menu answer names one of its enabled items, a tag is trimmed and 1..50 or null); a malformed answer dismisses the request, since the overlay closed its popup before answering. When the popup closes, focus returns to the webContents that asked, never to the overlay itself: a hidden view keeps keyboard focus, so whenever the overlay hides while it holds focus and no such page is left, main moves focus to the selected chat. An overlay popup answers main only after its exit: menus, confirmations and the tag picker leave through a `TransitionGroup` whose after-leave sends the answer (a leaving popup is inert and aria-hidden), and the settings modal's exit is a Web Animation whose `finished` sends `{ kind: 'settings', closed: true }`, at once under reduced motion, so main's switch to `hidden` never cuts an exit short. A crash hides the overlay and rejects every open request. Main follows the page's life from its own webContents events, never from its own loads alone: a committed main-frame navigation (`did-navigate`: a reload by any route, DevTools, Ctrl+R in a dev build or Chromium itself) is a new document holding none of the old page's popups, so it rejects every open request and drops pending rasterizations as a crash does, and main sends the page nothing until `did-finish-load`. A request made while a page is loading (the first load, a crash's reload, any other reload) is held and sent once it has loaded, one held popup at a time as one is open (a later request dismisses an earlier one; a `message` or worktree dialog stacks), and is rejected only when no page will load: a crash past the reload limit, a main-frame `did-fail-load` other than `ERR_ABORTED`, or the window closing. The page state flips only at commit, so a request made between a main-frame, cross-document `did-start-navigation` of a loaded or loading page and its commit is held too (`navigating`), and held requests wait for that navigation's `did-navigate` or `did-stop-loading`: a page that finishes loading meanwhile is about to be replaced and gets none. Requests already open stay open, since the old document can still answer them. A `did-stop-loading` while the flag is set means the navigation never committed (stopped, blocked by `will-navigate`, a 204, a download), so the held requests go to the page it left once that page has loaded. A renderer that dies mid-navigation also stops loading: Electron 44 emits that `did-stop-loading` before `render-process-gone`, while `isCrashed()` is still false and the renderer's process id already reads 0, so for such a renderer the flag stays set, the requests stay held, and the crash's reload takes them (`e2e/desktop/overlay-crash.spec.ts`). A main-frame `did-fail-load` other than `ERR_ABORTED` while the flag is set marks the page `none` and rejects open and held requests, and `did-finish-load` counts only while a page loads, because Electron emits it after a failed load too (electron/electron#5128). A request with no page loaded or loading is refused. The shell page and the popup window's page likewise take no push from main between a reload's commit and its load, nor after a main-frame `did-fail-load` other than `ERR_ABORTED` (`mainFrameLoadFailed` in `security.ts`), whose `did-finish-load` they ignore until the next commit or load. Its `damocles:overlay:*` channels live on its own `webContents.ipc` and accept a call only from that webContents, main frame, on the exact overlay URL. Invoke handlers stay registered for the view's life, because its page can still ask while the window closes (it reads its state unprompted at mount) and a call with no handler, or one whose handler throws, is an error in main; only the send listeners are removed at dispose. `OverlayHost.handle` is for reads the page may make at any time (`getState`, `prefsGet`), answered after dispose too; a channel scoped to one popup goes through `handleWhileOpen`, which answers `PopupAnswer` `{ ok: false, reason: 'closed' }` as a value while its popup is not open or the overlay is disposed, since that is an expected state and not an error. Malformed input and real failures still reject. The overlay page reads such an answer through `answerOf`, or `ran` for an action that answers nothing (`src/desktop/shell/overlay/popup-answer.ts`): `closed` means the popup is leaving, so the caller shows nothing for it. Every caller handles a rejection itself, so none becomes an unhandled rejection or shows an invoke error: a failed action says so in the user's language where it was taken (About's update actions and Copy version info, the notification center's Clear all and Do not disturb, Restore default layout, a desktop setting's write, the Language row's Restart now), and a failed read shows a localized could-not-load state, never an empty result (About's version and update status, What's new, the notification center, Settings › Terminal's profiles, Quick Open, whose index also answers `failed` when its worker fails, and the command palette). What main sends an open Connect dialog or Settings about hosts is rebuilt field by field as its request is, push and answer alike (`parseConnectHosts`, `parseConnectStep`, `parseHostEntries`, which the `hostsEntries` push and the `hosts.list` answer share): a push past the bounds is logged and the page keeps the last one, and a `hosts.list` answer past them rejects, which Settings › Remote hosts shows as its could-not-load state. Native `Menu.popup` stays only for the application menu, which the title bar logo opens where the menu bar is hidden. The first popup shown after each overlay page load is logged with its acknowledgement and render times.
- **The notification center holds the run's notifications, and main owns every toast's life** (`src/desktop/main/notification-center.ts`, `preload/notifications.ts`, plan AD10, D26). Entries are structured data (kind, chat, project, summary, outcome, timestamps); every renderer localizes them. Each new `pendingPrompts` id of a chat raises one entry, once per core panel and prompt id: a prompt a team agent raised is `team` whatever it asks, an `input` prompt is `question`; a prompt that resolves withdraws its popup and flash and keeps its entry, and closing its chat, or a core reload for every chat, does the same for all its prompts and forgets their ids (`panelClosed`). A settled turn raises `done` only when its chat is not selected or the window is unfocused, `error` for an error or a rate limit (which names the full window and its reset: the 24-hour time when it is today, else the weekday, or past a week the date, with the time; with no window, no time), `limit` for a budget stop, and a usage crossing raises `limit` under Usage (next bullet). Core's non-modal notices are entries and popups too and keep the `NotificationService` answer (an action label or undefined). Lives are the kind table's (12 s, `done` 7 s, `limit` 9 s; notices 8, 12 or 20 s, 120 s with actions), and a sticky notice of main's (the engine halt's) has none: it stays until answered from its toast or its center row (`awaitingAnswer`), or withdrawn, and its toast shows no life bar; main's timer pauses while the popup page reports a toast held (pointer or focus), a card that collapses into the "N more" pill releases its hold, and a reloaded page that main replays the toasts to starts with none held. The 2px life bar is a transform keyframe paused the same way and hidden under reduced motion, where main still times the toast. Do not disturb (global `KeyValueState`) and Notify me off silence the seven kinds' popups, every popup's sound and the flash; entries still collect and count, and notices still pop up. Both take one path (`popupPolicyChanged`), so turning either on also drops the seven kinds' popups already showing and stops the flash. The bell's state rides on `ShellState.notifications`; the center is an overlay request with only an anchor, opening it marks every entry seen once the overlay reports it shown, its clear and Do not disturb channels act only while it is open, and a row's answer names an entry id main issued. An action selects the chat (loading it, a lease refusal shown as its toast), then posts `focusAttention`, `openTeamOverlay` or `runChatCommand` to that chat's own view, only once the view is ready, only while that chat is still the selected one (`runEntryAction`), and after the `panelFocused` its show posts, which focuses the composer. An entry turns read when its action runs, when its chat is selected while the window has focus, or when the window gains focus on it; one raised for the chat being looked at arrives read (D52).
- **A usage warning shows once per window reset, across launches** (`usage-warning-store.ts`, D54). `<userData>/usage-warnings.json` holds `{ version: 1, warnings: [{ provider, windowId, resetsAt, threshold }] }`, one record per provider window, with a `windowId` of at most 100 characters (a longer one is never recorded). A record matches a crossing whose reset time is within core's 60 s tolerance (`sameReset`), and a recorded 95 covers 80. The center records a warning as it raises it, silenced or not, since its entry collects. Expired records are dropped on read and on each write; a warning with no reset time is never stored, since nothing says when its record would expire and core re-arms it within the run. A corrupt file is logged, ignored and replaced on the next write.
- **Desktop popups are the app's own window, not OS notifications** (`notifier.ts`, `overlay/NotifierApp.vue`, `overlay/chime.ts`, D52). Windows' notification switch and Focus Assist therefore do not hold them back; there is no Action Center history, and a Wayland compositor may ignore the position. Every toast, the seven kinds and core's notices alike, shows only in one frameless, transparent, always-on-top window at the bottom-right of the primary display's work area, whether or not the main window is focused; the app draws no toast inside its window. It shows inactive and loads with `focusOnNavigation: false`, so a popup never takes focus (only F6 focuses it), is sized to the stack its page reports and hides when empty. Showing without activation keeps the window's old place among always-on-top windows, so a full-screen player (itself always-on-top) activated since would cover it; main calls `moveTop()` each time the window appears and on each new toast while it shows, which restacks it at the top without activating it. Its size is rounded up to whole device pixels at the primary display's scale (`popupBounds`), because Windows rounds a fractional device-pixel origin and ceils a fractional size, so at 150% a 381x121 DIP window at the corner reached 1 px past the work area onto the taskbar; the page anchors the stack bottom-right, so the extra length is transparent margin. Its window type (`POPUP_WINDOW_TYPES`) is `panel` on macOS, non-activating and floating over full-screen apps on every Space (`setVisibleOnAllWorkspaces` is not used, because it hides the Dock icon), `toolbar` on Windows, which keeps it out of Alt+Tab and Task View, and none on Linux, where Electron 44 aborts on X11 creating a window with a type. On Windows and macOS the shown window ignores the pointer with forwarding (`setIgnoreMouseEvents(true, { forward: true })`) except while the page reports the pointer over a toast or the pill (`damocles:overlay:toasts:pointer`), so a click beside the toasts reaches what is under the window; forwarding is off while the window is hidden. On Linux the window's shape is the toast and pill boxes the page reports in its toast area message (`setShape`), which clips their shadows. The page is the overlay bundle at `app://damocles/notifier/index.html` (`NOTIFIER_PAGE_PATH` in `preload/page-paths.ts`, which main's router and the bundle both read) with the overlay preload, rendering the `ToastStack` under the entries' ids and the lives main owns, and announcing each toast as its own node, removed with the toast, in one of two hidden live regions that stay rendered while empty: approval and question toasts and core errors assertively, every other one politely. Main answers only `getState` and the toast channels there, the pointer channel included, from that page's main frame. A page whose window quitting already closed still gets its `getState` answer, so main never throws while it loads, but its toast messages no longer count. A popup's action shows and focuses the window, then runs the entry's action. Focusing the main window never hides, moves or replaces a popup: it stays until its life ends, it is answered or dismissed, or its prompt resolves. Each popup that Do not disturb and Notify me let through plays a synthesized chime (rising for a prompt that waits, one note for a finished chat or info, falling for an error or limit; a burst plays its most urgent tone, by `CHIME_URGENCY` in `preload/notifications.ts`) while `damocles.desktop.notifications.sound` is on, and the window allows audio without a gesture.
