Main holds the most urgent tone for popups that arrive before the page loads and plays it once the page has loaded; it holds one only while the popup window exists, so popups that wait for the main window play no sound when they show. It opens only while the main window exists and closes with it, or when quitting closes it first, so it never keeps the app running; toasts raised meanwhile wait and show once the main window opens again.
- **The taskbar shows the bell's unseen count, and prompts flash it** (`taskbar-counter.ts`, `taskbar-badges.ts`, `notification-art.ts`, D52). Every count change applies once per frame: a drawn red badge (`9+` past nine) as the Windows overlay icon with a localized description that counts new notifications, since the count is the unseen count that opening the bell clears, and `app.setBadgeCount` on macOS and Linux (Electron derives `damocles.desktop` from the package name, the deb and rpm file's name), where a call that returns false is logged once. Electron makes one HICON from the image's 1x representation at whatever pixel size it has, and Windows draws an overlay icon at 16 px times the display scale, so the badge is drawn once at 16 px times the primary display's scale factor and held as that single representation. `TaskbarCounter.reapply` sets the count again for a new window, a change of that scale (drawn at the new scale), a UI language change (the description is localized) and an overlay page load or the page's first answer after a missed deadline since it loaded (a dot drawn while the page could not draw is replaced). Approval, plan, question and team entries flash the taskbar button while the window is unfocused, until it gains focus or every prompt that flashed resolves; a focus stops the flash only when something flashes. `index.ts` owns the flash for two reasons, these entries and an overlay popup waiting for the window to activate, and stops it once neither is left. The badge is rasterized by the overlay page, which has the fonts and a canvas: main builds the art from its own data (SVG and text runs, the colour checked as `#rrggbb`), issues the request id, waits 2 s (counted in ticks, as for an acknowledgement), and accepts only one complete PNG of exactly the asked size within 64 KiB (signature, IHDR first, every CRC, IEND last) before decoding it; anything else shows a plain dot drawn in main.
- **Every question the desktop asks renders as the overlay's dialog; the OS message box is only the fallback** (`src/desktop/main/message-dialog.ts`, `overlay/components/OverlayDialog.vue`, D41). Modal `NotificationService` calls, the trust question and the window crash prompt go through one `AskMessage`: a `message` request main builds and bounds (`parseMessageRequest`), shown by the same `alertdialog` the shell's confirmations use, on the overlay stack's modal layer so it paints and takes Escape above the settings modal, and a reka layer so it takes the pointer and focus above an open reka dialog. Focus starts on the first action, except a danger dialog and the trust question, which focus Cancel (Don't Trust); Enter activates the focused button, Escape and the scrim cancel, the exit plays before the answer, and focus returns to the control that asked once it is enabled again; when focus was inside a listbox, menu or dismissable layer, that is the control that opened it (its `aria-controls` owner), never the owner of an ancestor some other control merely controls. Main accepts the answer only from the overlay page for the id it issued, so no chat view can answer a trust question. `dialog.showMessageBox` asks only when the overlay has no page loaded or loading, crashes or reloads while the question is open, or misses the 2 s acknowledgement, or the question exceeds the overlay's bounds, and the fallback is logged. File and folder pickers stay native.
- **The desktop settings modal is the overlay attached to the selected chat** (`src/desktop/main/overlay-settings.ts`, plan AD2). The title-bar gear (`damocles:shell:window:open-settings`), Ctrl+, / Cmd+, (`MENU_IDS.openSettings`, File on Windows and Linux, the app menu on macOS), every in-chat settings link (`openAppSettings`), the Voice settings action of a reply that could not be spoken, and core's `EditorService.openHostSettings` (which calls `WindowService.openAppSettings` with its caller's section, so the browser DevTools notice opens Tools & integrations) open one `{ kind: 'settings', section?, account?, generation }` request (`account` opens that Accounts row expanded; a chat that needs OpenAI sign-in asks for `openai`); a second open while it shows sends its section and account to the modal (`damocles:overlay:settings:target`), which goes there, and Escape belongs to the modal. Main attaches it to the selected chat's core panel with `PanelManager.attachView`, once core has set that chat up (`restoredCorePanel`, so a launch or host reload restoring it never makes the modal open a chat), opening an empty chat on the default project first only when no chat is selected (`promptTarget`), and re-attaches on every selection change except during a window close or quit, which dismisses it first (`OverlaySettings.close`), and once the core it was attached to ends (`OverlaySettings.coreEnded`: a crash, halt or reload, after which what the modal sends is dropped and logged until it attached to the next core), pushing `damocles:overlay:settings:attached { generation }` before the attach: the renderer resets its stores when the modal opens and on that push, so no other chat's or earlier session's values show or get written, and the attach itself posts the host prompts it moves into the view, which the view accepts from inside `attachView`. The modal sends `requestSettingsState` once when it opens and once per attachment, on both hosts, and no other request for state: core answers with everything its sections render (the chat's settings and models, every account's status, Explore, MCP, tools, image generation, the voice settings, the voice runtime's status and the size of the voice files, and on a host that keeps settings files which of them apply), posting `hostCapabilities` first, and later changes reach it as pushes; its sections request nothing when they mount, so neither a search keystroke nor a section switch asks again. Core copies each message of a `SETTINGS_VIEW_MESSAGES` type the chat receives to the view (`damocles:overlay:settings:message`); replies to the view's own requests go to the chat too, so a This chat change updates the chat's composer. The view sends `damocles:overlay:settings:send { generation, message }`; main drops it unless the generation is the current attachment's (so a write meant for one chat never lands on another after the selection moved), it is JSON within 64 KiB, and its type is in `SETTINGS_VIEW_REQUESTS`, then `dispatchFromView` checks the allowlist again and runs the router with the chat's context. The allowlist holds settings requests only: no `ready` (the view bootstraps with `requestSettingsState`), no permission, plan or PiSession dialog answers, no trust request, no opener that takes a path (`voiceOpenModelsFolder` reveals only the voice models folder), no settings file load or save (Edit settings.json sends `openSettingsFileInChat`, which opens the scope's settings tab in the editor pane). Only message types are logged. `prefs:get` returns the `damocles.desktop.*` values and the language this run launched with (the Language row offers Restart now only for another one), and while the modal shows main pushes them again on each change of those keys (`damocles:overlay:prefs:changed`); the first read never overwrites a value the modal set after asking, and a push never one whose write main has not answered; `prefs:set`, `app:relaunch`, `layout:reset` and `terminal:profiles` answer closed (`PopupAnswer`) unless the modal is open, `prefs:set` takes only a key declared in `desktop-configuration.ts` (`Object.hasOwn`) with a value of its type and enum (`isDesktopSettingValue`) and writes user scope, and `app:relaunch` and `layout:reset` take no arguments. `app:relaunch` answers once main has started the quit (`QuitLifecycle.relaunch`), never after it: the quit closes the window only once every request its pages made has been answered, so waiting for its end would hold the quit until its deadline. A quit the user cancels in its own terminal or unsaved-editor question leaves the Language row's banner offering Restart now; only a failed call says the restart did not happen. The overlay answers only after its exit animation; main then detaches the view and returns focus.
- **A remote chat's settings show User, host and Project tabs, and each tab lists and writes only its own file** (`overlay-settings.ts`, `OverlaySettings.vue`, `core/chat-panel/settings-view-scope.ts`). The User tab attaches to a local chat (the last viewed one, else the one `promptTarget` opens hidden; only the local chat that answer names stays hidden, `hidden-opening.ts`) and lists no This chat row, since that chat is not the remote one; the host and Project tabs attach to the remote chat's own panel with their scope, and the host tab lists its This chat rows. Core sends a scoped view the keys its tab may write (`writableKeysOf`: the host tab every key but the client-only ones, the voice keys among them, the Project tab the resource keys a project file may hold), and a setter reached from a tab writes that tab's file whatever its home and whichever file holds the value (`settingsViewWriteScope`: the User tab this computer's user file, the host tab the host's machine file, the Project tab a per-folder key's project `settings.json`), so every row writes the file the tab's banner names; Edit settings.json offers the tab's own files. The desktop app's own sections and Voice, which run on this computer, show only on the User tab. A tab click waits for main, and the page lists nothing until that tab's core attached and sent its writable keys; a tab main cannot attach (no chat answers, or core refuses the attach) leaves the tab and the attachment as they were and refuses the click, and the renderer says so. The modal opens on the User tab while the host's core is unavailable, and a core ending re-attaches it only when it is attached to that core.
- **The desktop display language is `damocles.desktop.language` and applies after a restart** (D23; `readLanguageSetting` and `launchLanguage` in `local-services/localization-service.ts`). `start()` reads it from the user settings file before the app is ready, since Chromium fixes its UI locale at launch, and appends `--lang` only for `en` or `el`; `parseDesktopLanguage` (`desktop-configuration.ts`) is the one validator, and a missing, unreadable or broken file or any other value reads as `system`. The bundles then use the setting, or with `system` the language the chat webview stored, else the OS locale; a setting of `en` or `el` clears that stored choice at launch so the webview follows it. Restart now runs the normal quit and relaunches only when nothing cancelled it (`QuitLifecycle.relaunch`, which registers `app.relaunch()` on `quit` as VS Code's `relaunch` does), so layout, chats and editors come back through restore. `damocles.desktop.restoreLayout` off starts the next launch with the default `window-layout.json` and a new chat on the default project, dropping the saved chats; Restore default layout resets the file and applies it live. `damocles.desktop.notifications.enabled` off silences the seven notification kinds' popups as Do not disturb does, and `damocles.desktop.notifications.sound` off mutes the popups' chime; see the notification-center bullet.
- **Browser pages are editor tabs of their chat** (`src/desktop/main/browser-tabs.ts`, `views.ts`). There are no top-level browser pages: `views.create` refuses a page whose `PanelOptions.owner` is not a loaded chat. Only the selected chat's pages show as tabs; selecting another chat swaps them, and selecting the first again returns its tabs and its active page. A page view is laid over the page area the shell reports (`damocles:shell:browser-bounds`) only while its tab is the active editor tab, and is hidden otherwise. A page is a panel view with the same panel preload and `damoclesBridge`, its own channel pair with the same sender check, the same navigation, popup and permission hardening, and the screencast page main builds (`browser-page-html.ts`) with `localResourceRoots: []` and its unchanged CSP. Host messages go out through `webContents.send` (structured clone, so screencast frame buffers arrive intact); webview messages are JSON-normalized and bounded; the page renderer's `setState` persists nothing. The page script picks `acquireVsCodeApi` or `window.damoclesBridge` at load (`BROWSER_WEBVIEW_SCRIPT`, `src/shared/browser-page-html.ts`). The tab's navigation bar never reaches core directly: main turns each action into the webview message the page's own toolbar would post (`navigate`, `goBack`, `goForward`, `reload`, `pickElement`, `openDevTools`), so core's handlers and `isNavigableUrl` stay the authority. The address bar's text reaches core as typed from both hosts: desktop main only checks it (`isTypedAddress`), and the VS Code page toolbar sends it unchanged. Core reads it with `typedAddress` (`src/shared/typed-address.ts`), which refuses text longer than `MAX_BROWSER_URL_LENGTH` and keeps a typed scheme in lower case, and ignores any `upgraded` flag a message carries. Text counts as having a scheme only when the scheme is one Chrome's omnibox knows or is followed by `//`, so `user:pass@example.com` is a host with credentials, and `about:blank` matches in any case. Without a scheme it follows Chrome's typed navigation (`ShouldUpgradeToHttps`): an IP address, a single-label host, a name under a top-level name no public registry assigns (`UNREGISTERED_TLDS`) or an address with a port other than 80 opens over http, and anything else opens over https marked `upgraded`. For such an address core (`src/core/browser/https-fallback.ts`) loads the http address when the https load fails with a connection (refused, reset, closed, aborted, failed), TLS, certificate or empty-response error, and logs the fallback. A DNS failure, an offline network, a cancelled load and a timed-out load keep the error page: since Chrome 150 a typed address never falls back after a timeout (crbug.com/515265983), and there is no fallback timer. `isNavigableUrl` stays the final check in main and in core. Both hosts' address bars mark an http page not served from this computer Not secure (`isNotSecurePage`, same module). Open in system browser passes only the URL main observed, http and https only, to `ShellService.openExternal`; when that is refused or fails, main raises one toast, and the shell catches every rejected browser channel.
- **Main applies the bounds of the shell's slots** (`views.ts`, `browser-tabs.ts`, `window-layout-store.ts`). The shell lays out the title bar, sidebar and the grid's slots and reports the chat slot's rectangle (`contentBounds`) and, while a browser tab is the active editor tab, its page area (`browser-bounds`); main converts each to DIPs, clamps it to the window, and places the selected chat's view and the active page on them. A pane that moves to another slot glides with a script animation, which fires no `animationend`, so the shell measures again once every glide has finished (`LAYOUT_SETTLED_EVENT` in `content-bounds.ts`), or right after the move with reduced motion. The window controls are HTML (`WindowControls.vue`), so Windows 11 shows no Snap Layouts flyout on the maximize button; Win+Z still opens it. Views stack chat < active page < overlay, restacked on every selection and tab change, since `addChildView` puts a newly created view on top. The window is frameless (`titleBarStyle: 'hidden'`; macOS `trafficLightPosition`). Windows and Linux have no `titleBarOverlay`: native controls paint above every view, so the overlay's scrim and modals could not cover them; the shell's `WindowControls.vue` draws them instead from `ShellState.windowState`, which main republishes on maximize, unmaximize and full-screen changes, and hides them in full screen, its menu bar is hidden on Windows and Linux with every accelerator still working, and its minimum size is 900x600. `<userData>/window-layout.json` (`writeJsonConfig`) holds the window's normal bounds, maximized and full-screen state, the sidebar layout and each project's selected repository (`scmSelected`, by root path); every field is validated on read and what is dropped is logged (a malformed Search, Source control, Graph or Repositories section size, Search states, View & Sort options, expanded rows, Graph ref choices or section order falls back to its default alone), a saved position on no display is clamped onto the nearest work area, and a restore is idempotent: Electron's DIP writes and reads of a frameless window do not round-trip on Windows above 100% (electron/electron#51572; at 150% the constructor's 960x640 reads back 963x643 and `setBounds`' 961x641), so main sizes the new window with `setContentSize`, reads the normal bounds as position plus content size, on `resize`, on `move` and again at every save while the window is neither maximized, minimized nor full screen (X11 applies `setBounds` asynchronously, so a `move` can carry the new position with the old size, and Linux emits no `resized` or `moved`), and persists the bounds it applied for as long as the window still reads what it read right after (`boundsToPersist`), and main owns `sidebarVisible` (Toggle Sidebar; Reveal in Files and the Search view's commands show it through a show-only path, `damocles:shell:window:show-sidebar` and `ShellHost.revealInFiles`, so a reveal never hides it) and the grid's slots, visibility and maximized pane. The shell owns the sizes while it runs: the sidebar reports only `ShellSidebarLayout`, its width, section sizes, section order, Search states, Source control's View & Sort options, the Repositories expanded rows (`repositories`) and the Graph's ref choice per repository root (`graph`, at most 100, newest last, each Auto, All or 1 to 50 full refnames; `boundGraphChoices`, taken only whole) (`sidebar-layout`; a project's state keeps the Replace input's text only while its Replace row shows, as VS Code saves `query.replaceText`, within `MAX_REPLACEMENT_LENGTH`), the grid only its sash sizes (`grid-sizes`), main stores each without republishing, and the shell takes main's sizes back only when `ShellState.layoutRevision` changes (Restore default layout), so a state push carrying older sizes never undoes a resize. A quit waits for its pending writes (see "A quit waits for every desktop store's writes"). A page tab's page sits in a rounded card inset from the pane; the shell reports the card's inner corner radius with the page bounds, and main rounds the page view to it (`View.setBorderRadius`, one radius for all four corners), so no square corner of the view covers a rounded corner in any layout.
- **Keyboard focus moves only for the user, except that a launch focuses its chat's composer once; no page outlives its chat** (`views.ts`, `browser-tabs.ts`, `index.ts`). Every chat and page view loads with `focusOnNavigation: false`, and create, reveal, restore, navigation and selection never focus a page view; only a user's action on a tab focuses it. A launch, a reopened window and a host reload show their chat with `focus: 'whenReady'`: `PanelViews` focuses that view once, on its `dom-ready` while the window is focused and not minimized, else when the window activates (Electron focuses the window's own page before emitting `focus`), and the webview's mount has already focused the composer. The user's first key or button press in any of the app's pages (`before-input-event`, `before-mouse-event`, observed in `index.ts`), another selection, a focus main gives the chat, or an overlay popup open at that moment drops it; a focus change never does, because the window's activation focuses its own page. A focus main gives a chat whose page has not committed waits for `did-navigate`, since the renderer drops earlier focus, and then, while the window is unfocused or minimized, for the window to activate, since X11 activates a window only after the focus that asked for it and the activation focuses the window's own page. It yields to focus reaching any other view first, except the window's own page taking focus before the window activates. Electron may focus the window's own page as the window activates, and AppKit does as it restores a minimized window, so main returns keyboard focus to the chat, page or overlay view that held it when the window was left (`activation-focus.ts`); a focus of the window's own page counts only while the window is active, a key or button press while it is inactive decides instead, and `focusOnActivation` wins. The window activates once per activation, at the first `focus` it reports itself focused (`isFocused()`) and not minimized at, and not again until it blurs or is minimized (`onWindowActivated` in `activation-focus.ts`), and deactivates when it blurs or is minimized (`onWindowDeactivated`); only then do main's activation focus, the launch focus, the flash stop and the viewed chat's read marking run. A minimized window that another process restores with `ShowWindow(SW_RESTORE)` and `SetForegroundWindow` emits `focus` on Windows before `restore` while `isFocused()` is still false, and again once Chromium has activated it and focused a view itself, which can be the window's own page, so a focus main moved at the first would be lost. X11 sends `focus` and then `blur` to a window it is iconifying, and `webContents.focus()` activates, and so restores, its window on Linux; a focus gained while minimized activates the window once it is restored, unless a blur comes first. AppKit resigns a miniaturizing window's key status before `minimize` but reports its `blur` up to ~100 ms later, and a restore started in between makes the window key, focusing its own page, before that `blur` arrives; so minimizing deactivates the window too. A chat that unloads or is deleted closes every page it owns, and core closes the chat's pages as well, including a failed subagent's kept tabs, and refuses any that register later; a chat holding pages stays loaded. Application-menu accelerators are the only shortcut mechanism, so New Chat (`CmdOrCtrl+N`), Toggle Sidebar (`CmdOrCtrl+B`), Next and Previous Chat and F6 fire whichever view has focus. Close Editor (`CmdOrCtrl+W`) closes the active editor tab; its key runs it only while the editor (page views included) holds focus, and otherwise reaches the chat. F12 opens DevTools only while a page tab is the active editor tab. F6 and Shift+F6 cycle focus through the parts sidebar (while shown), editor (while it has tabs), chat, terminal (while its pane shows a terminal) and the desktop popups' window (while a popup shows), because Tab cannot leave a WebContents; the title bar is not a stop. The popup stop is a user action, so it focuses the popup window and its page, which focuses the newest toast's action (else its first control) with its focus ring showing; Tab stays inside the stack, and Escape, or the last toast going while it holds focus, brings the main window forward and gives focus back to the part F6 came from (the selected chat when it came from none). Popups focused by a click, or left for another window, leave focus where the OS puts it. The shell reports when its sidebar or editor holds focus and main reads the chat and page focus itself. A menu item is enabled by its command's precondition alone (`Command.enabled`, VS Code's `precondition`), which never reads keyboard focus, so opening the application menu from the title bar greys nothing out. Whether an accelerator runs its command is `keyFires` (`commands.ts`, as VS Code's `menubar.ts` runs an accelerator as a keybinding with its `when` clause): the precondition, `Command.keyWhen` and, while an xterm's input has focus, the skip list. Electron calls a menu accelerator only for a key the focused page left unhandled, and on macOS as a key equivalent too, so a key that does not run its command has already reached the page or the pty. An item with `registerAccelerator: false` only shows a key another owner handles (the chat items' Ctrl+Tab routing, the xterm's Ctrl+Up and Ctrl+Down) and never runs from it, because macOS registers its key equivalent anyway. A menu click acts on the active editor or terminal wherever focus is; F4, Shift+F4 and Toggle Query Details act on the active Search Editor from the menu. New Browser Page and every browser affordance do nothing while `damocles.browser.enabled` is off.
- **`panels.json` v2 holds the loaded chats and the selection** (`panel-state-store.ts`). `{ version: 2, selected: { projectKey, sessionId?, panelId? }, chats: [{ panelId, state, pages, activePage }] }`: each loaded chat's webview state and its browser pages (addresses in tab order and the active index). Fields an older file holds for the retired side pane are ignored. A v1 file migrates on read: tabs become chats, and the selected tab becomes the selected chat with the project and session its state names; the next write is v2. Every field is read with own-property checks and validated, and what is dropped (a malformed panel id, state, address or selection, a non-chat tab) is logged. Unloading a chat deletes its entry and keeps the selected project and session. A restart reopens every saved page as a tab of its chat through `BrowserService.restoreChatPages`, which honours `damocles.browser.enabled` and refuses a non-navigable address per page. A quit waits for its pending writes (see "A quit waits for every desktop store's writes").
- **Desktop settings come from three files, and a folder's project and local files apply only while that folder is trusted** (`src/desktop/local-services/settings-store.ts`, one instance in main and one in core). `damocles.*` keys sit flat beside `permissions` in user `~/.damocles/settings.json` (always), project `<folder>/.damocles/settings.json` and local `<folder>/.damocles/settings.local.json`. Precedence is local over project over user over the `package.json` default, and `get` merges a plain-object value key by key, recursively, across those layers exactly as VS Code does (`mergeSettingValues` in `src/core/config/settings-file.ts`; the fake platform and the vscode mock merge the same way), while `inspect` reports each layer as written. `SettingsStore.get`, `inspect`, `update` and `scopeFile` take an optional `SettingsFolder` (`path`, and `personalPath` for a worktree chat, D34). A read made for a chat passes that chat's folder (D38), as VS Code's `getConfiguration(section, folderUri)` does: `settingsFolderOf(target)` in `folder-registry.ts`, which is undefined on the home target, so a chat with no project reads user settings and defaults only and its Workspace writes go to the user file, never to `~/.damocles/settings.local.json`. The folder always comes from core's `ctx.folder` or `instance.folder`, never from a message field. The keys read with a folder are `CHAT_SETTING_KEYS` (`src/core/config/chat-settings.ts`: budget, task budget, auto-compact, the thinking and effort defaults, the permission-mode and YOLO defaults, the team role models and efforts, subagent concurrency and the two notice switches); each declares `"scope": "resource"` in `package.json`, and `chat-settings.test.ts` fails when core passes a folder for any other key. Every other read is window-level and passes no folder, which reads the registry's default project (`defaultProjectPath`, the rule `WorkspaceFolderRegistry.defaultTarget()` applies), the selected project on desktop. A daemon's core serves every attached client's folders and no window, so its store takes no default project (`DesktopSettingsStoreDeps` admits `defaultProject` or `remote`, never both), and a window-level read or write there has no project or local layer; core's callers that name the window's project use `windowSettingsProject` (`folder-registry.ts`), which applies the same rule. For a folder, the project layer is `<path>/.damocles/settings.json` and the local layer `<personalPath>/.damocles/settings.local.json`, and both apply only while `personalPath` is trusted (trust matches the exact folder, so a trusted parent trusts no child), checked when the layers are read and again on every read; a trust grant re-reads them. Each folder's layers are cached and watched while the default project or a loaded chat uses it (main reports the loaded chats' folders through `ChatFolders`), and a folder no chat uses drops its cache and watchers. `inspect` reports only the layers that apply: an untrusted default project, no open project, or a user-only key leaves `projectValue` and `localValue` absent, because `updateConfigAtEffectiveScope` picks its write target from `inspect` and would otherwise route a settings toggle into a refused or untrusted file (YOLO and the permission mode would become unchangeable). That is the `SettingInspection` contract on both hosts: a store reports `localValue` only where `update(key, value, 'local')` can write it, so a toggle of a key the local file supplies writes the local file however many projects are open. VS Code reports and writes a `WorkspaceFolder` value only for a resource, so its store passes the folder as the configuration resource (`Uri.file(folder.path)`), which also gives multi-root workspaces their folder values; with no folder `localValue` is absent there. `update` to project or local rejects for a user-only key, an untrusted folder (the default project when none is given) and no open project. Writes from the settings UI land per section (D37), decided in core per setter, never by the renderer, except that a remote chat's scope tab writes its own file (below): This chat sets the panel's own state; Workspace (every setter that writes through `ConfigManager.workspaceScope`) writes `local` for the chat's folder (desktop `.damocles/settings.local.json`, VS Code the folder's settings), or user on the home target, and core refuses it with a localized reason while that folder is untrusted (on both hosts, as the MCP exposure scopes do); `settingsUpdate` carries `workspaceWritable`, so the modal shows those rows disabled with a notice, offering the chat's `setProjectTrusted` where the modal sits in the chat page and naming the Projects list's trust action in the desktop overlay, which may not send it; every other setter writes user. `updateConfigAtEffectiveScope` (`settings-manager/utils.ts`) takes that home scope and the chat's folder for a per-chat key, and writes to the highest file that already holds the value when it ranks above home, so a change always takes effect; `damocles.desktop.*` keys are written only at user scope, by the overlay's `prefs:set` and by main's own commands that toggle one (the title bar's theme toggle, Toggle Inline Blame, Search's Toggle Search on Type); core never writes them. After each write core posts `settingWriteResult` (`message-router/setting-write.ts`) under the key of the row that asked, with the scope and file the value the setter wrote now comes from (each setter returns the key it wrote and its home scope, so a setter that writes a key other than its row's reports where that key landed, and a write that leaves its key at the default reports that home scope and no file), or the error, an invalid value included, which the settings rows show as Saved to or an inline failure. Every write goes through `writeJsonConfig`, project and local ones with `confineTo` their `.damocles`. A file that stops parsing (a trailing comma, a `//` comment) keeps serving the layer it last parsed, and the user is told once, naming the file, until it parses again: core's store tells (it reads every folder a loaded chat uses) and main's only logs, so a typo is one toast, though a new core after a crash tells once more; an existing file read as empty while it held settings is taken as an editor mid-write and read again after 300 ms before it counts as empty; an unreadable path (EACCES, a directory) keeps the last layer too. Without that, one typo read as `{}` flipped every setting in the file to its default and back, `permissionMode` and tool availability included. The user file and every open project's two files are watched; an external edit of any loaded folder's files, a trust grant, a default-project change and a project list change fire `onDidChange` for each key whose layers changed. `search.useIgnoreFiles`, `useGlobalIgnoreFiles` and `useParentIgnoreFiles` default to VS Code's defaults (true), which a user or project value overrides as in VS Code. A `damocles.*` key in a `.damocles` file affects desktop only; the extension keeps VS Code's settings. The settings modal shows the file behind each value a project or local file supplies (`settingSources` on `settingsUpdate`, each entry the file from `SettingsStore.scopeFile` and the value it holds): a row's source badge names it, and the Workspace section lists every such key that no row of a shown section writes, with its value, its file and an Open file action that goes the way of the footer's Edit settings.json (`openSettingsFileInChat`); for a per-chat key the chat's folder's file, for every other key the default project's, because that is what applies. Desktop-only keys (`damocles.desktop.*`, the `damocles.desktop.git.*` source control settings among them; `DESKTOP_CONFIGURATION` is the list) are declared in `src/desktop/local-services/desktop-configuration.ts`, an erasable table with no imports so that `scripts/generate-settings-schema.mjs` can import it. The store merges them into its contributed set (`withDesktopConfiguration` in `settings-store.ts`) with their defaults, an `application` key as user-only. They never enter `package.json`, so VS Code's Settings UI never lists them; the settings JSON editor's schema lists them in the user variant and flags the user-only ones in the project variant.
- **Project and local files never supply a user-only key** (`src/core/config/contributed-configuration.ts`). The set is derived from `package.json` at load, never hardcoded: `capabilities.untrustedWorkspaces.restrictedConfigurations` plus every key whose `scope` is `machine` or `application` (VS Code likewise never reads those from workspace settings; `machine-overridable` keys may come from a project). On desktop the `application` keys of `desktop-configuration.ts` join it, `damocles.desktop.terminal.profiles` among them. An entry that is such a key, a prefix of one or under one (`"damocles.compass": { "enabled": true }`) is dropped and logged, trusted or not. Known residual, as in VS Code: `damocles.browser.devToolsPort` is neither restricted nor machine scoped, so a trusted project may turn on the unauthenticated DevTools port.
- **Host dialogs render in a chat's webview** (`src/core/chat-panel/webview-prompts.ts`). Electron has no native text or list prompt, so desktop `DialogService.inputBox` and `quickPick` post `extensionUiRequest` (`password`, `items`) to the last active chat, or open a chat in the selected project and wait for its `ready` when none is loaded (the overlay renders menus and confirmations the shell asks for, not host prompts). A chat still being opened or restored counts as loaded: `PanelManager` registers a panel only once its session exists, well after its webview shows its input box, so `promptTarget` waits for the panels in `opening` before it opens one, which also makes prompts asked at once share one new chat. Request ids carry the `host-prompt:` prefix, distinct from PiSession's `<sessionId>:ui:<n>`, and `extensionUiResponse` is offered to `WebviewPrompts` first. An answer counts only from the panel the prompt was posted to, and a quick pick answer only when it names an offered item. An abort posts `extensionUiCancel` and resolves undefined; the panel closing resolves undefined; a webview restart re-posts the panel's pending prompts on `ready`. While a settings view is attached to the panel (`PanelManager.attachView`, the desktop overlay modal), its host prompts render in that view instead: each pending prompt records where it shows, and every attachment change (`onDidChangeAttachment`, `WebviewPrompts.resurface`) cancels it on the old surface and posts it on the new one, back to the chat on detach. The view's equivalent of the chat's `ready` re-post is `requestSettingsState`, which re-posts only the prompts shown in that view. An answer counts only from the panel the prompt was asked in and only from the surface showing it now, and one that arrives through the view (`ctx.view`) is offered to `WebviewPrompts` only, never to a PiSession dialog. PiSession's own dialogs are never redirected and never copied to a view, which is why `extensionUiRequest` and `extensionUiCancel` are `SETTINGS_VIEW_PROMPTS`, outside `SETTINGS_VIEW_MESSAGES`. Answers are never logged: they carry API keys and pasted OAuth redirect URLs, and `panel-attach.test.ts` enters a sentinel key through a view and checks the log. The VS Code host keeps `showInputBox` and `showQuickPick`.
- **Desktop opens files and diffs in the editor pane, and only approval diffs in the chat (D15)** (`src/desktop/main/platform/editor-service.ts`). Core threads the originating panel into every `EditorService` call (`OpenFileOptions.panelId`, `DiffRequest.panelId`), and the VS Code host ignores it. A `showDiff` with `approvalId` (the `toolUseId` of the pending `requestPermission`) posts `editorShowDiff` to the asking chat without revealing or focusing it (`ChatTabMessenger.deliver`; its notification entry is the way there), where `useEditorStore` holds it until the permission card's Open diff opens the read-only overlay; the permission prompt stays the only thing that decides, and closing the overlay decides nothing. `openFile`, a `showDiff` without `approvalId`, `openUntitled`, `showMarkdownPreview` and `openSettingsFile` open editor pane tabs. Only a user action takes focus: an agent's open passes `preserveFocus`, which shows its tab without focus, and VS Code maps the flag to `showTextDocument({ preserveFocus })`. Core closes an approval's `DiffView` on approve, reject, abort, an approval a mode change settles, and panel dispose (`approval-manager.ts`, `DiffManager.dispose`), which posts `editorCloseView`.
  - **Workspace modules are host-neutral, and main keeps every model** (`src/desktop/workspace/`, `src/desktop/main/workspace/`). Files, tree listing, search, the Quick Open listing, formatting and terminal backends take their dependencies by constructor and import neither electron nor desktop main (eslint, `tsconfig.core.json`); they start processes only through the launcher or `HostStarters`. Main reaches them only through `workspaces.forProject(rootKey)` (eslint: outside `main/workspace/`, main's value imports from them are limited to the contract, constants and pure helpers of `MAIN_WORKSPACE_IMPORTS`) (`ProjectWorkspace`, whose arguments and results are structured-clone values and whose streams are listeners or process handles keyed by run, watch or terminal id), with two kinds of entry: this computer's workspace (`local-workspace.ts`, utility process starters) and, for a project of another authority, that authority's connected workspace (`remote-workspace.ts`), which calls the daemon's `workspace` channel (`workspaces.ts` picks one by the project key's authority, or by the authority key itself, `forAuthority`, for a host's own settings file). While an authority is not connected `forProject` throws `NotConnectedError`, and so does every request on a connection that closed or closes before its answer (`createRemoteWorkspace`), never with the close reason; that fails its projects only: Files answers `failed`, Quick Open leaves them out of a query and ends a listing the worker asks for with the reason, Search finishes with the reason, and a save says the host is not connected. A connection's watches end with it, so once the host connects again Files and Quick Open watch each project they watched over the new connection and reload it (`FileTree.connectionsChanged`), and each open document of that host is watched again and read as a watcher event would read it, a dirty buffer taking the conflict (`DocumentService.connectionsChanged`), since nothing reported what changed meanwhile. Main does path arithmetic on a remote path only in the target's path rules, the OS its connection's `HostFacts` or the host store names (`pathFor`, `folderKeyOn`), never this computer's `node:path` or `folderKey`: names, joins, watch bases, Copy Path, ripgrep's printed paths, search labels and a remote core's worktree records, which main checks with the registry rule in the target's rules (`remoteChatRecord`). A relative path crosses `/`-separated and the host joins it (`files.mtimes`, one request per batch of search results). A system error crosses the wire as its code (the `Errno` fault, `core-protocol/errors.ts`), which main words.
  - **Documents are main's, by id** (`documents/document-service.ts`). Renderers hold only main-issued document and tab ids. A shell open names a project key and a relative path; a core open may name any path, and a file outside every project opens read-only. The one exception is a host's own settings file (`DocumentService.openHostSettings`, a remote core's Edit settings.json for its machine scope): its location carries the authority key (`host`), it opens editable as a settings tab titled with the host's name, and every read, watch and write goes through that authority's workspace, never through a local path that happens to match; a local open of the same path is a document of its own. A file document is its authority plus its path by that host's path rules, so one folder open here and through a host, or the same path on two hosts, is two documents, each read, saved and replaced through its own authority. Its save keeps the settings file rules in main (text that does not parse is not saved, a file on disk that does not parse is not overwritten, a changed one is a conflict), its tab is not persisted by path, and its hot-exit backup (`hostSettings`) restores into it only while that host is connected, else as an untitled buffer.
  - **Confinement** (`local-services/confine.ts`): a path must be relative (`isRelativeFilePath`, plus `hasUnsafeWindowsSegment` on win32), and its native realpath must lie under the project's realpath, compared with `folderKey`. Before anything follows a link, `followsLocalLinksOnly` walks the path with one `lstat` per component and a `readlink` per link, and on win32 refuses as `outside` a link whose target is UNC, a device path (`\\?\`, `\\.\`, any `\??\` path but a drive) or drive-relative, or resolves onto a UNC or device root other than the project's. No realpath, stat or open reaches a share, because opening one sends the user's NTLM credentials; `confineExisting`, `confineEntry` and the Files listing's link kinds share the walk. Rename, trash and reveal act on a link itself, never its target. Save As confines the chosen path. The native dialog shows only this computer's files, so a file on another host is offered under its name in a local project, never at its path on the host (VS Code's `suggestSavePath` for a file the dialog cannot show). A write compares the opened handle's bigint dev and ino with the identity taken before the realpath check, and a new file is created exclusively. Every later read of an open project document (a watcher reload, Revert, the disk check of a save or Overwrite, Compare) confines its path again first and is refused when it now resolves to another file (`DocumentService.readLocation`), and a write's link walk runs before its identity stat. A mention resolves only through `Platform.confinement` (`src/platform/file-confinement.ts`): desktop's is `confineExisting`; VS Code's follows links by realpath, since VS Code's own file service follows every workspace link and no VS Code view names a project file for a mention. The only file outside every project a workspace writes is its host's own settings file (`WorkspaceFileSystem.readHostSettings` and `writeHostSettings`, `HOST_SETTINGS_FILE` in the `damoclesHome` the host names itself, the file its core's settings store reads as the machine layer): the request names no path, and the file is confined like a project file to the realpath of that `.damocles` and must be exactly its `settings.json`, so a link to any other file, beside it or outside it, reads as outside and a write is refused with `WriteRefusedError`; the write is `writeInPlace`.
  - **Encoding:** a read records the encoding (UTF-8, UTF-16LE, UTF-16BE), the BOM and the majority EOL, and a save writes in place with all three. Text that is not valid UTF-8 without a BOM opens read-only. The size limit counts the bytes a write produces, BOM included (`encodedLength`), the same bytes `readFileBytes` measures, so a file saved at the limit reopens.
  - **Conflicts:** each document carries `{ mtimeMs, size, sha256 }`. A save over a changed file is refused and offers Compare, Overwrite (after a confirmation) or Revert. A watcher event reloads a clean buffer and flags a dirty one, so no text is discarded without the user choosing. The bar names the file on the file's own tab and on the Compare tab (`conflictCompare`), which offers only Overwrite and Revert; either, from either tab, closes the Compare tabs and shows the file's own tab with focus, opening one if none is left (`EditorPane.showResolved`, VS Code's `textFileSaveErrorHandler.ts`). A Compare tab's title follows its file's current name.
  - **Saves** (VS Code's `textFileEditorModel.ts` `doSave`, `saveSequentializer`): one document's saves, Save As, overwrites and reverts run one at a time in main (`DocumentService.inTurn`), so none reads the disk while another writes it. The shell runs one save per document with at most one queued, which a later request joins and which is skipped when nothing changed since the last save (`TaskSequentializer`). A save's text joins the buffer in the order it arrived among the shell's edits. A buffer that changed while the write ran stays dirty with the new text and its backup is scheduled; `diskText` is the text written. A write takes every byte before it truncates. A file that cannot be read before a save (a folder now, locked, past the limit) answers with the reason, which the tab shows. Every save, Save As and overwrite failure reads "Failed to save '{name}': {reason}" (VS Code's `textFileSaveErrorHandler.ts` genericSaveError). The reason comes from the errno code (`DocumentService.errnoReason`), or, for a write Damocles refuses itself (the file replaced during the save, a path that now leads out of the project, a disk that takes no more bytes), from the `WriteRefused` it throws; Search's replace in a closed file words its failure the same way. The raw error and path are only logged. A failed write sets `saveFailed` (VS Code's `handleSaveError`): the document is dirty and its backup is written at once, since a write that fails after some bytes landed may have torn the file, so the watcher flags the file as a conflict instead of loading it. Only a save that lands, Revert, or the disk found holding the buffer's text clears it; editing the text back does not, unlike VS Code, because the disk is no longer known to hold it. The shell turns a rejected save invoke into a failed save with a fixed reason under the tab (`saveNow`), so its save queue settles. A buffer with no file is never in conflict. A settings file saves against the sha256 of its bytes on disk. Auto save on focus change fires when the editor control blurs and when focus leaves the pane, once per document; Ctrl+S, Format Document and auto save act on an editable diff tab's modified side.
  - **Deleted files** (VS Code's orphaned model, `textFileEditorModel.ts` `setOrphaned`): a file deleted on disk leaves its document `orphaned`, which is not dirty; the buffer keeps its text and is dirty only when it differs from the last disk text. Its tab (`ShellEditorTab.deleted`) shows the name struck through in `--d-danger-text`, with Deleted in its tooltip and its accessible name. Save writes the file back, and the mark clears once the file exists again. Revert keeps the text and makes the buffer clean while the file stays gone (VS Code's `revert` ignores FILE_NOT_FOUND). Closing a clean orphaned tab and quitting ask nothing, and a clean orphaned document writes no backup. A hot-exit backup whose file is gone restores the same way, dirty, at the path the file had (`DocumentService.openProjectFile` with `missing`, confined through its deepest existing folder by `confineMissing`, VS Code's `doResolveFromBackup`). A save recreates the file and any folder deleted with it (`confineOrCreateFolder`, each folder confined again after its mkdir, as `fileService.ts` writeFile creates parent folders).
  - **A Files rename retargets open documents** (`DocumentService.rename`, `EditorPane.fileRenamed`, VS Code's `workingCopyFileService.ts` move and `editorService.ts` `handleMovedFile`). The rename on disk runs only once no file open is in flight, and a file open that starts meanwhile waits until the documents moved, so an open in flight never makes a second document at the old path, which the watcher would mark Deleted, and an open of the new name finds the moved one. Two opens of one file share one read (`textFileEditorModelManager.ts` `joinPendingResolves`). Every document at or under the old path keeps its id under the new path, confined again, with the new name and language, so its tabs keep their order and active state, the shell moves the document's Monaco model to a new one at the new path (`ShellEditorState.documents`, `editor-store.ts` `moveModel`, VS Code's `textFileEditorModelManager.ts`), carrying the unsaved text, EOL, indentation and each editor's view state; undo starts afresh there, as VS Code keeps undo per URI, and the old model is disposed. A model URI is `damocles-document://<documentId><path>`; a document with no file keeps the URI it was created with. A settings tab's model is at `inmemory://damocles/settings/<scope>/<documentId>.json`, which the settings schemas match by glob. The markdown preview renders the document's buffer when one exists, at most every 300 ms (VS Code's `preview.ts`), else main's content. Each tab's editor view state lives in the store by tab id until the tab closes (VS Code's editor memento), so a code or diff tab keeps its caret and scroll while another kind of tab shows. JSON follows VS Code's languages: plain `.json` flags comments and trailing commas, VS Code's JSON with Comments file names allow both (`JSONC_FILES` in `editor/monaco.ts`), and `.damocles/settings.json` and `.damocles/mcp.json` stay strict because Damocles parses them with `JSON.parse`. A diff tab names the file it shows (`DiffRequest.filePath`, titled by `title(fileName)`, VS Code's `diffEditorInput.ts`): a side's document when one is that file, else the file's place, which `fileRenamed` moves, so its title and path follow the rename while a snapshot side keeps its text. A dirty buffer's backup is rewritten at once under the new path, and the window layout and recent files name the new path; focus does not move. The location changes before any await, so a watcher read of the old path in flight is ignored. A settings file's document keeps its scope's path.
  - **The Files name box** (`FilesSection.vue`, `preload/file-names.ts`, VS Code's `explorerViewer.ts` and `validateFileName`): the name is checked against the loaded listing as it is typed (empty, invalid by `isValidFileName`, or a sibling's, ignoring case on Windows and macOS, the renamed entry's own name excluded). Enter does nothing while it is invalid, Escape cancels, and leaving the box commits a valid name and cancels an invalid one; only Enter and Escape move focus. Main checks again, and a refusal closes the box and shows one toast (`runFilesAction`).
  - **Files failures** (`index.ts` `runFilesAction`, `files/files-failure.ts`, VS Code's `fileActions.ts` deleteFiles): every failed create, rename, delete, copy path, reveal or folder expand (a click, Enter, Space or Right Arrow on a folder whose listing fails, which also collapses it), a throw included, is one error toast naming the entry and the reason; a cancel is silent. A New file or New folder into a folder whose listing fails opens no name box, and the listing's failure is that one toast. A listing reloaded after a file change fails silently. When the trash fails, the overlay's danger dialog offers Delete Permanently with the trash's reason; it deletes only the entry the user confirmed (bigint dev and ino compared again after the questions), a link as itself, and the tree reloads the folder after every outcome but a cancel.
  - **A Files listing shows the newest request's answer** (`FilesSection.vue` `load`). Answers for one folder can arrive out of order, because main's listing waits on git's ignored check and a remote one also crosses the wire. An older answer is dropped and its caller waits for the newest one, since no later watcher event would correct a stale listing.
  - **Hot exit** (`documents/backups.ts`): a dirty buffer backs up to `<userData>/backups/` 1 s after its last change, through a temp file renamed into place, and quit first flushes the shell's last edits (`editor:flush`, 2 s). With "Reopen where I left off" on, backups restore as dirty tabs; off, quit asks Save or Don't Save, and Escape cancels the quit. Every backup's text comes back (`EditorPane.restoreBackup`, VS Code's `workingCopyBackupTracker.ts` restoreBackups): into its file's document, a file deleted on disk included, else (its project removed, the file now outside it, unreadable, an image, binary, too large or not UTF-8, a settings file that does not apply now, a host's settings file while that host is not connected, a second backup of a file another restored first) as an untitled buffer that keeps the backup, with one warning naming the file and why. No path removes a backup whose text was neither restored nor saved. A save of a dirty buffer writes its backup at once and waits for it to land before it writes the file in place, so a write a crash tears leaves the text in the backup; a write that fails backs the buffer up at once too, clean or not. A backup rename Windows refuses (EPERM, EBUSY, EACCES) is retried for a bounded time with `writeJsonConfig`'s `renameWithRetry`. A Revert that cannot take the file's text (too large, unreadable, binary, moved during the read) keeps the buffer, its conflict and its backup, and the tab says why.
  - **`readText`** returns the disk text on desktop, so a proposal diff's original side equals what the agent's tools read; VS Code returns the editor's text.
- **Format on save runs the project's own Prettier in a formatter host, only in a trusted project** (`src/desktop/main/formatting/`, `src/desktop/workspace/format/`, `src/desktop/formatter-host/`). Main's `FormatService` keeps the orchestration, notices and trust; the project's workspace (`Formatter`) resolves Prettier and its config and runs the hosts. With `damocles.desktop.editor.formatOnSave` on, a user save (Ctrl+S, Save As, the close and quit prompts, auto save on focus change) asks main to format the document by id first; auto save after a delay and agent writes never format. The result is applied as one undoable edit and dropped when the buffer changed meanwhile. Main resolves Prettier with Node resolution from the file's folder and uses it only when its package and entry lie inside the project root. It then finds the config file Prettier of that major would load, by that version's search order and rules (`prettier-resolve.ts`), and refuses one outside the root. The host loads exactly that file (`resolveConfig(file, { config, editorconfig: true, useCache: false })`, then `getFileInfo` with `resolveConfig: false` and the config's plugins); with none, it names Prettier's own `package.json`, which loads as no config, so Prettier never searches past the root. Config results are not cached, since nothing restarts the host when a config file changes. A `module.registerHooks` resolve hook in the host (`confine-modules.ts`, one synchronous hook for `require` and `import`) refuses every module whose real path is outside the root, builtins aside, so a plugin or shared config in a parent folder, a global folder or `NODE_PATH` never runs, and the notice names the path. `.editorconfig` files above the root are still read, as data. Prettier runs in `dist/formatter-host.js`, an Electron `utilityProcess` per root with the login environment minus `ELECTRON_*`, and no `MessagePort` reaches a renderer. A request has 3 s to load Prettier, its config, plugins and parser (the host's `loaded`) and then 3 s to format. Past the first, the save goes on unformatted while the host keeps loading (it is killed after 30 s, `HOST_STARTUP_MS`); past the second, main kills the host; either notice offers the Format log. Main also kills it on project removal and after 5 minutes idle, and restarts it when `package.json` or the lockfile changes. An untrusted project starts no host, and its first save in a run shows one notice with Trust. Without Prettier, TypeScript, JavaScript and JSON format with Monaco's built-in formatter in the shell. Prettier is never bundled.
- **Search runs ripgrep in the project's workspace, and the renderer gets matches only** (`src/desktop/main/search/`, `src/desktop/workspace/search/`). The project's workspace runs ripgrep and reads its `--json` lines (`search-runner.ts`); main keeps the results, buffers and every rule below. `rg-args.ts` builds an argv array: `--json --no-config --crlf --hidden`, never `--follow`, a regex as the value of `--regexp` with `--engine auto` and a fixed string after `--`, so a pattern starting with `-` is never an option. Globs are comma-separated and refused with a `..` segment or an absolute path; the shown folder is the only path, and `damocles.desktop.files.exclude` plus `damocles.desktop.search.exclude` add `-g !` entries unless the user turns exclude settings off. Main converts ripgrep's UTF-8 byte offsets to UTF-16 columns, bounds previews, and keeps only copies of the text it cuts from a ripgrep line (`detached`), because `--json` prints each matching line whole (`--max-columns` has no effect there) and a V8 substring keeps its whole parent alive. It kills ripgrep at `MAX_SEARCH_RESULTS` (20,000), on a new query, a project switch, a change of the folder the project shows (D31) or Clear; a worktree's chats starting or ending a turn never stop it; one search runs per owner (the Search view, each Search Editor), 300 ms after the last keystroke. ripgrep searches files of any size, as VS Code does; main's memory is bounded by dropping any JSON event over `MAX_RG_EVENT_BYTES` (32 MiB), which marks the results a subset, and by keeping a match's text whole only up to `MAX_KEPT_MATCH_CHARS` and past it as its length and SHA-256, which replace verifies against (`keepMatchText`). Whole Word is ripgrep -w's: no Unicode word character on either side. Results carry main's smart-case `matchCase`, which the shell's buffer replace and row preview use; a file found closed and opened since is replaced only where its recorded text still is. A Search Editor run is dropped only when a searched field changed, and its running state clears either way; closing its tab or the window stops its ripgrep. Main searches the project's open documents and untitled tabs in their buffers, under one `runWithin` per run, and a buffer's matches replace ripgrep's for that file. `matchingPattern` makes every line break a regex names also match `\r\n` (VS Code's `fixRegexNewline`, lookbehinds included, which needs the bundled PCRE2 10.43 or later), and a multi-line regex scans a buffer's lines joined with `\n`, as Monaco does; "Search only in Open Editors" searches those buffers alone. Main owns the results and their dismissals, and an edit to an open document searches that file again 250 ms later, so a dismissed match stays dismissed until Refresh. Replace expands `$1` to `$99`, `$<name>`, `$&`, `$0`, `` $` ``, `$'` and `$$`, VS Code's `\n`, `\t` and `\\`, and its `\u`, `\l`, `\U` and `\L` before a numbered group, and applies Preserve Case, in one place (`replacementAt` in `src/shared/text-search.ts`): an open file changes in its buffer as one undoable edit, and a closed file is written through the document service only when every match's recorded text is still at its range, else it is skipped and listed, as is a file the service opens read-only. Overwrite after a save conflict sizes `savedText`, the text it writes, as Save and Save As do. A JavaScript RegExp built from user input runs under `runWithin` (`time-bound.ts`), which interrupts catastrophic backtracking. Replace All asks through `createMessageAsker`, and replaces run one at a time.
- **Quick Open lists and scores in a worker thread; main runs its ripgreps and relays** (`src/desktop/quick-open-worker/`, `src/desktop/main/quick-open.ts`, `src/desktop/workspace/quick-open-listing.ts`, VS Code's `rawSearchService.ts`). `dist/quick-open-worker.js` is plain Node (no electron import, enforced by eslint), started on the first query from `app.asar.unpacked`. For each project it lists, the worker asks main, which has the project's workspace run `rg --files --hidden --no-config` (never `--follow`, excludes as `-g !`, the root after `--`) and streams its output in; the worker reads the paths, keeps every file sorted by path, and caches each list until main invalidates it (a create or delete under the root, the root leaving main's root list, an exclude setting change). There is no file cap. A query that extends an earlier one of the same open request scores only that one's matches, unless it adds a path separator or the earlier one was quoted; a failed listing is retried and its matches are never reused. A newer generation of the same request stops the older query between batches of 10,000 files. Main checks each reply as untrusted (at most 30 results, relative paths, ranges within the name, each file in a queried project and the scoped folder), names results from its own project list, and accepts a pick only if some query of the open request returned it. The worker posts progress at most once a second while ripgrep prints files and between scoring batches, and a worker that posts nothing for `QUICK_OPEN_WORKER_SILENCE_MS` (30 s, in main's timer ticks) while a query waits is stuck; a listing that still prints is never cut short, and a new query never extends the deadline. A worker error, exit, unreadable or malformed reply, failed query or silence answers every waiting query `failed` with no results, which the overlay shows as could not load rather than no matches, is logged, terminates the worker and kills every ripgrep main runs for it, and the next query starts a fresh one. Main starts each ripgrep on the process launcher and holds it, because terminating a worker thread leaves the processes it spawned running; ripgrep starts no process of its own (its Windows console host exits with it), so a plain `kill()`, which the launcher worker runs through the process's handle, is the whole kill, with no pid to reuse. VS Code's search process bounds a query only by cancellation.
- **A Search Editor is a document main owns, in VS Code's `.code-search` format** (`src/desktop/main/search/search-editor-format.ts`, `src/desktop/shell/editor/SearchEditorTab.vue`). Its text is the results body plus a header holding the query, toggles, globs and context lines, saved byte for byte as VS Code saves it, so a file opens in either app; dirty state and hot exit go through the document service. Results come only from main's search service. Parsing a `.code-search` file is bounded and never runs the query it holds; opening a result line reads main's own copy of the results and opens only a path inside the tab's project.
- **The command registry is the only command list** (`src/desktop/main/commands.ts`, plan AD11). The menu bar (`menu.ts`) and the palette are built from it; an app-level shortcut exists only as a menu item's accelerator, and native roles stay roles. Keys local to the focused widget stay in that widget: the terminal's find, copy, paste and context-menu keys (`terminal-keys.ts`) and Monaco's own bindings. Command ids are `MENU_IDS`. The palette's list and run channels take the focus context main captured when the palette opened; main refuses an unknown or disabled id, returns focus to the webContents that had it, then runs the command. A chat command reaches the selected chat only as `runChatCommand` after that view's `ready`, and the webview has one consumer for the union. A menu click runs an enabled command; an accelerator runs it only when `keyFires` holds. A title may follow the context (`Command.titleIn`, read through `commandTitle` by the palette and the menu), as VS Code's context keys do: while the selected repository's commit box amends (`ScmMenuState.amending`), each commit variant takes the title of the amend it runs, the shell's own label for it, and the amend variants leave the palette and the menu (`Command.hidden`), as they leave the commit box's menus. Electron documents a built item's `enabled` as changeable but not its `label`, so `updateMenuState` rebuilds the menu when a title or a hidden command changes.
- **The editor's context menu is the overlay's, never Monaco's** (`src/desktop/shell/editor/context-menu.ts`). Every shell Monaco editor is created with `contextmenu: false`; a right click on the text, the context menu key or Shift+F10 sends an overlay `menu` request with VS Code's editor menu as far as the page's language services fill it: Go to Definition and Go to References where `hasGoToProvider` (`editor/monaco.ts`) says the language has the provider, Rename Symbol and Change All Occurrences while their precondition holds, Format Document on a writable code, untitled or settings tab, Cut, Copy and Paste (Copy only in a read-only buffer) and Command Palette. Format Document runs the registry's format (D29) and Command Palette opens main's palette (`showCommands`). Cut, Copy and Paste are items with a `clipboard` kind that main runs itself on the user's pick, on the shell page while it holds keyboard focus. Main keeps them only in a menu opened by a context-menu click or key it observed (a one-shot grant in `askShellMenu`) and gives them its own label; the shell's `clipboard-read` permission stays denied. The shortcut labels come from `EDITOR_MENU_ACCELERATORS` in `commands.ts`, which must match Monaco's bindings for its commands.
- **Only the desktop chat panel page and the shell page allow workers, and only from `app://`.** Monaco's editor, JSON and TypeScript workers are module workers, and a worker cannot carry a nonce, so `script-src` does not cover it. The worker itself runs under the policy on its script response (`default-src 'none'; script-src app://damocles`), not the page's. The chat panel's workers come from `dist/webview/assets/monaco-*`: `PanelHost.workerSrc` is the one extension point, core appends `worker-src <value>;` to the chat panel CSP only when the host sets it, and the desktop chat sets `app://damocles/webview/assets/` (`CHAT_WORKER_SRC` in `src/desktop/app-urls.ts`). The shell page sets `worker-src` to its own assets folder (`SHELL_WORKER_SRC` in `shell.ts`), with `base-uri` and `form-action 'none'`; the shell build ships only the workers `scripts/shell-workers.mjs` admits by their full paths: Monaco's editor, JSON and TypeScript workers of the checkout's `node_modules`, the quick diff worker (`editor/quick-diff/quick-diff.worker.ts`), which diffs with jsdiff's `timeout` and `maxEditLength` set so a huge file never blocks it, and the merge worker (`editor/merge/merge.worker.ts`), one per merge editor and ended with it, which aligns the three inputs with node-diff3 and rebuilds Reset Result's text, over only the lines between those all three share at either end and not at all past `MERGE_ALIGN_MAX_LINES` (2,500), since node-diff3's LCS grows much faster than the line count on repetitive text. No page adds `blob:`, a `font-src` or a `style-src` for them. `MonacoEnvironment.getWorker` (`components/editor/useMonaco.ts`) takes precedence over a language service's own worker, so every service label a page starts is registered there (the shell adds `typescript` and `javascript` through `addLanguageWorker`); an unregistered label gets the plain editor worker, and every call into that service fails with "Missing requestHandler or method". The overlay page, a browser page and every VS Code host set no `worker-src`, and `chat-panel-csp.test.ts` pins the VS Code chat panel and sidebar CSP byte for byte. The chat panel CSP names `form-action 'none'` on both hosts, because `form-action` does not fall back to `default-src` and every form in the webview handles its submit in script.
- **A settings file saves only text that the settings reader parses, and never overwrites a file it cannot parse** (`src/core/chat-panel/settings-file-editor.ts`). Desktop edits the three files `SettingsStore.scopeFile` names; project and local only for a trusted window project (`windowSettingsProject`, the store's own rule, none on a daemon's core), checked again at save. A daemon's core edits only its user scope, its host's machine file, which main opens and saves through that host's workspace with the same parse and conflict rules ("Desktop host", Documents). `parseSettingsText` (`src/core/config/settings-file.ts`) is the single parser the desktop store, the editor, the permission rule writer (`syncPermissionRulesToSettings`) and the permission rule reader (`readPermissionsFromPath`, for all eight settings files, `.claude` included) share; it ignores a leading BOM, so a rule the store applies is never one the evaluator drops. The permission rule writer refuses a file that does not parse the same way, inside the lock, and the user is told the rule was not saved. Desktop opens each file as a settings tab in the editor pane (`DocumentService.openSettingsFile`), and `DocumentService.saveSettings` saves it through `saveSettingsFileText`, which runs inside `writeJsonConfig`'s lock and refuses, in order: a file on disk that does not parse, a file whose sha256 no longer equals the version the tab loaded (a conflict), and text that does not parse (the parser's message shows under the tab); otherwise it writes the user's text verbatim. A conflict goes to the editor's conflict bar (Compare, Overwrite after a confirmation, Revert) like any file's, and nothing discards the text without the user choosing so. A save is refused, with no conflict, when the default project moved after the tab opened. A path that exists but cannot be read (a directory, no permission) is answered with `unavailable`/`unreadable`, so nothing waits on it. The document service watches the file and reloads a clean tab or marks a dirty one in conflict, never for its own save. The settings modal's footer menu (Edit settings.json) learns which files apply from the `settingsFileAvailability` that `requestSettingsState` posts, which reads no file, and the host posts it again to each panel that asked on a trust grant or a project change. On desktop that footer closes the modal and sends `openSettingsFileInChat`, which opens or shows that scope's settings tab in the editor pane (`platform.editor.openSettingsFile`). The editor's schema (`src/shared/generated/settings-schema.json`) is generated from `package.json` plus `permissions` by `scripts/generate-settings-schema.mjs`: unknown keys are flagged, and in the project variant every user-only key is flagged with a message.
- **A directory that appears under a desktop recursive watch is watched before it is listed, and what it holds is reported as created** (`src/desktop/local-services/file-watcher-factory.ts`, `src/desktop/watch-worker/fs-watch-tree.ts`). On Linux the recursive watch is `startInotifyTree`, one non-recursive `fs.watch` per directory, never `@parcel/watcher`'s inotify backend: that backend adds a watch to a new directory without listing it, so a directory created with its contents in one go, or moved in, loses those files, and its subdirectories are never watched (parcel-bundler/watcher#97, #243). inotify(7) prescribes the order the tree follows: watch the new directory, then list it. The tree reports a change only against what it already reported, so a file both the listing and an event see is created once. On macOS parcel's native recursive watch sees later writes, but a directory moved in still arrives as a single create, so `startParcel` lists it and reports what the batch did not. On Windows the recursive `fs.watch` already covers a new directory, and `startFsWatchTree` lists it and reports what its snapshot did not hold. All three skip `RECURSIVE_IGNORE`. The startup scans are asynchronous (Linux's stats directories only), so a large workspace never blocks the main process.
- **Windows watches recursively with Node's `fs.watch(root, { recursive: true })`, one handle per root, and nothing on Windows loads `@parcel/watcher`, in the app or in tests** (`startFsWatchTree`; `electron-builder.yml` `files` leaves its Windows prebuild out of every package, and its JS loader stays unused). Exclusions stay in the top-level `files`: electron-builder takes a platform `files` set that only excludes as everything but those paths, and ships the whole project. parcel 2.6.0's Windows backend frees a subscription on a libuv worker while its `ReadDirectoryChangesW` read is still pending (parcel-bundler/watcher#262), which crashed the main process on quit and on unwatch (0xC0000005), whereas libuv closes the watch on the loop thread and frees it only once that read has completed. The trees run on one watch worker thread per factory (`src/desktop/watch-worker/`, `platform/watch-worker-host.ts`), because libuv's Windows watch makes a synchronous `GetLongPathNameW` call on the loop that owns it for every created or modified entry, before any JS filter runs: with `npm ci` in a watched 25,000-file tree, main-thread stalls over 50 ms were 1-2 with no watcher, 241-292 (up to 1.25 s) with `fs.watch` on main, and 1 with it on a worker. A worker that exits, errors, sends a reply main cannot read or check, or answers `closed` unasked is terminated, and the host at once starts a fresh one and posts it every live watch with `report: false`, logging one line with the count, as VS Code's watcher restart watches its requests again without replaying them. The snapshot each watch was diffed against died with the worker, so the fresh worker's first scan only records what it finds: nothing is reported as created or deleted that did not change, and a change made between the failure and that scan is not reported. A root gone by then fails to watch and is waited for again, without its deletes. Restarts stop after `MAX_WORKER_RESTARTS` (5, VS Code's limit): the host then logs once and every Windows recursive watch fires nothing until the app restarts, so a worker that dies on every start cannot respawn forever.
- **The Windows tree settles every event against a snapshot of every entry, and diffs the tree again when events were lost** (`startFsWatchTree`). Node reports a create, a delete and both names of a rename as `rename` and a last-access update as `change`, so each path, coalesced for 75 ms, is `lstat`ed and compared with its recorded identity (file id, size, ctime): an unchanged file reports nothing, so a read never looks like a write. Files are recorded as well as directories, at about 250 bytes per entry, because a directory's delete reports its children and the diff below needs them. A burst past libuv's 4 KB change buffer arrives as a null filename: the root is listed again and diffed against the snapshot, one scan at a time with one more queued and logged at most once a minute, while events wait until it ends. An `error` closes the handle, and a root still there is watched again after 1 s, doubling to a minute while that keeps failing, then diffed. A same-size rewrite inside the file system's timestamp tick after the snapshot read the file goes unreported, as with VS Code's mtime-and-size etag.
- **`FileWatcherFactory.onDidRenameFiles` never fires on desktop.** No rename is reported, the Files section's own included: a rename reaches watchers as a delete plus a create, exactly as VS Code reports a rename made outside its explorer. The editor follows a Files rename itself (`EditorPane.filesMoved`).
- **A watcher glob is relative, on both hosts and in the fake** (`src/platform/file-watcher.ts`): `watch(base, glob)` matches paths relative to `base`, and `watchWorkspace(glob)` matches paths relative to each open folder and follows folders being added and removed. VS Code gets one `RelativePattern` per workspace folder, because a string glob there is matched against the absolute path and a glob without a leading `**/` would never fire. The fake platform's `fire*` reaches listeners only for a path the glob matches, so a core test cannot pass with a glob that never fires.
- **Desktop watchers never throw and never die quietly** (`local-services/file-watcher-factory.ts`). `watch()` logs a watch it cannot start and returns a watcher that fires nothing; every listener runs even when another throws (`local-services/emitter.ts`, as VS Code's `Emitter`), which the settings store, the key-value state and localization use too. A watched root that is deleted (a `git clean` removing `.damocles`) reports its files deleted, closes its watch and waits on its nearest ancestor again, and a recreated root replays what it holds; on Windows the closed watch also ends the endless rename events Node reports for a deleted watched directory. A recursive root is watched at its real path, because FSEvents reports real paths (`/var` is `/private/var`), and events map back onto the path the caller gave. A non-recursive root reports a burst of raw events once per file after 75 ms, as VS Code's non-recursive watcher does. Every `@parcel/watcher` subscribe and unsubscribe (macOS) and every Windows tree scan goes through its host's `track`, a dispose before its subscribe lands unsubscribes once it does, and the quit awaits `DesktopFileWatcherFactory.close()`, which also waits for the watch worker's scans to settle before it ends the worker, after which no watch starts native work and no scan emits: a native completion while Node frees its environment aborts the process (`napi_throw` in `PromiseRunner::onWorkComplete`).
- **Editor-scoped chords are Monaco keybindings whose palette entries come from `commands.ts`** (AD11, D28). Electron accelerators cannot express chords, so Next and Previous Change (Alt+F5, Shift+Alt+F5) and Stage, Unstage and Revert Selected Ranges (Ctrl+K Ctrl+Alt+S, Ctrl+K Ctrl+N, Ctrl+K Ctrl+R), one table (`EDITOR_GIT_KEYBINDINGS` in `preload/shell-channels.ts`), are bound in the shell as Monaco editor actions (`editor.addAction`, `editor-keys.ts`): Monaco scopes an action's keys to its own editor, and the component disposes them with its editor, so a remounted editor never stacks bindings and a key never reaches another editor (the merge panes, a peek's inline diff). `addCommand` registers a page-wide command and keybinding it never removes, so it is not used. A code tab binds them on its editor, a diff tab on both sides, and the quick diff peek's Escape is an action of its editor that yields to an open suggest, find or parameter hints widget.
